NEW BED

CHAPTER I 

THE sitting-room of the Jaconelli's flat was completely dominated by the past history of Mrs. Jaconelli, or 

Madame Jaconelli, as she preferred to be called; Francesca

Ann, her daughter, was an alien among its old-fashioned furniture, its photographs, and its silver trinkets set out on small tables of figured walnut. Ann liked marigolds in jugs of peasant pottery, hard chairs painted blue, and plain cream walls because she was seventeen, and Miss Marble, an adored schoolmistress, had taught her that these things were the outward aspects of a life given to pure thoughts, hygiene, and æsthetic appreciation. Ann never succeeded in explaining this to her mother or producing the slightest change in their home. The sitting-room retained its heavy red damask curtains at the high windows and a similar curtain hung over the door to keep out the draughts. A turkey carpet, which had been a good one and could still create a sense of luxury, and several black bearskin rugs added to the general atmosphere of comfort. Two modern armchairs stood one each side of a high brass fender. Their square stolidity brought into sharp relief the finely-scrolled woodwork of the walnut suite, whose curly legs were set upon the floor with a mincing, almost finicky gait, and whose Genoa velvet upholstery, once wine red, was faded now to dusty crimson and looked strangely flat and prim in contrast to the monstrous cube-shaped cushions of the new arm-chairs. The lace curtains were darned so that their design of mythological landscape was lost, but they still retained some of the opulent and expensive appearance which

NEW BED 

had been theirs when new many years before. The pleated silk inside the carved front of the piano would have split into shreds had a rough hand touched it. A rich polish on the furniture, and radiance from brass and silver never allowed to tarnish, redeemed the room from a conscious shabbiness. The knitted white cotton antimacassars were fresh and clean. The pictures on the walls were large photographs of women with capacious bosoms, elaborate coiffures, bouquets, and generous but highly artificial smiles. It was not difficult to discover that they were portraits of singers past and gone. Some of them looked large and important enough to have been prima donnas, and the signs were easily read that one and all they were exponents of bel canto. 

A portrait of Ann's father, a feeble oil-painting fast deteriorating into bilious yellows and pinks, stood on a gilt easel in one corner. It showed him as a delicately-made man, with a weak chin, long fair hair, a small red mouth, and an almost girlish softness in the contours of his cheeks. 

The eyes were brown, and as Mrs. Jaconelli had been heard to say that Ann took after her father, they must have been large dark eyes, fringed with long lashes. He held a violin in one hand to show the world that he was a musician. 

He had been first violin in a good class touring company playing opera in the provinces when he first met Ada Hudson, a singer fair and plump, with a baby face, a thin sweet voice like a piping bird, and a knack of resting her responsibilities on any shoulder that offered. He had a small but pleasing talent, and when he fell in love he wrote some songs for her as an offering and taught her to sing them note by note and phrase by phrase. They married and were successful; his songs, with her singing of them, had a vogue, his accompaniments on the violin being particularly applauded. They made far more money than either of them could have expected to make in the ordinary course

NEW BED 

3 of their profession, and Ada Jaconelli's head was turned and she became extravagant and voracious. Her figure developed with her vanity, and the force of her vitality, as she fed on the plaudits of her admirers and the foolish adulation of acquaintances and hangers-on, increased with incredible speed. She had no brains and less foresight, and would not save during the time of their success. She could not see that it might end as suddenly as it began. She dragged her husband up and down England, from concert to concert, from party to party. It was on him that most of the work fell. He wrote her songs, taught her to sing them, played accompaniments, arranged the tours, booked lodgings, heated her soup when they came in at nights. He died of influenza soon after Ann was born. He was not a strong man, and his wife had worn him out. 

She had loved him, but under her fair prettiness and helpless babyish ways was vigour and a rude health which made her impatient of his delicate mind and constitution. 

At the bottom of her heart she despised his tenderness and devotion, interpreting it as weakness. Her affection would have been deeper, perhaps, if he had been less yielding and gentle.

She followed her career as a singer for several years after he died, but her affairs did not run smoothly without his capable management, and in her vanity she economised on a poor accompanist. She tried a manager, but he cheated her so she finally settled down as a teacher of singing, for which her name was a good recommendation. The rise of a school of singing which made more of the interpretation of a song than of the voice had already made itself felt in the dwindling of her pupils when the war came and further diminished them. The invasion of jazz and mechanical music after the war had made things still more serious. Her pupils had shrunk to half a dozen non-

NEW BED 

descripts who sang Jaconelli's ballads atrociously at local charity concerts. She was still pretty in a florid buxom style with her hair skilfully dyed to recapture its original fairness, a skin still pink and white unmarked by wrinkles, good nature smiling out of blue eyes, and an insatiable desire for pleasure. She had always had plenty of men friends to take her to the races, the theatre, or for a weekend at Brighton, and, as her funds diminished, she began to make these friends help to keep her. She had innumerable ways of doing this and became skilful at dropping a hint at the right moment. It was a hazardous business, and a host of complications had arisen. For one thing she did not want Ann or her neighbours to discover what she was at; then there were the men themselves, the inevitable coarsening of the relationship, her ever-increasing age, the fear they might go elsewhere to younger women. She had begun to look round for a permanent partner, someone who would take over her troubles and let her float serene once more on a life of ease and comfort. 

Francesca Ann had no inkling of her mother's schemes. 

Mrs. Jaconelli did not discuss her affairs with her daughter, treating her always as a child, though Ann was clever in a bookish impractical way. Ann was like her father in disposition, docile and affectionate. She accepted her mother's explanation of their mode of life unquestioningly, prepared the meals, practised her music when her mother was out, and lived a life of dreams in a world which had no relation at all to the world that was about her. She did not know how they lived, though she knew money was scarce and new clothes as rare as her mother's pupils. She supposed her mother had money coming in from her father's songs. Ann earned a meagre salary of seven pounds a term, called pocket-money so that it should not appear so inconsistent with the amount of work she did

NEW BED 

5 as a student teacher in Miss Farthingdyke's kindergarten school. She was supposed to keep this pocket-money for herself, but most of it went in housekeeping expenses. 

Mrs. Jaconelli was always short of small change. "Just put a shilling in the gas meter, darling. . . . There's the boy from the cleaner's, just pay him for me. . . . .. Bring some flowers home with you; Charlie is coming to supper. .." ." By a host of similar requests Ann's pocket-money never stayed with her long. "But, mother, my music exam; I must save the fee. I haven't a pair of stockings . . . and my school coat is getting frightfully short. I saw Miss Farthingdyke looking at it yesterday. It makes me feel like one of the children. And I must have some books and music." "Books! Music! Nonsense! The place is full of books and music. You can have my pink silk stockings. They only want a little darning. Your school coat is quite nice for a young girl. I don't believe in finery for children. It looks absurd and is a waste of money. You don't want to be grown up before it's necessary." "But, mother, I am nearly eighteen. I've had that coat three years. I've grown a lot since then." "Now, darling, don't be tiresome. Suppose you go and see if Mrs. Pollard is finished in the kitchen and tell her she need not stay any longer. She only gets three and six if she leaves at four o'clock. Be a good child and pack her off, and make the tea and bring it in on a tray and we'll have it nice and cosy by the sitting-room fire. Then you can practise your music, because after tea Charlie (or it might be Sydney or James) is taking me to the 

Coliseum. . . ." Ann's attempts to talk facts to her mother usually ended in this way. 

But now, as she moved about the sitting-room putting the finishing touches to the table laid for three, Ann was

NEW BED 

in one of her rare moods of rebellion at her mother's selfishness. It was Easter Bank Holiday, and Mrs. Jaconelli had gone joy-riding with Charlie Dibbs, the latest and most favoured of her admirers. She had arranged to bring him home at one o'clock to partake of the lunch that Ann, with the help of Mrs. Pollard, the charwoman, was to cook for them. Mrs. Jaconelli had impressed upon her daughter that it was a special lunch, and loyally Ann had responded. It was now half-past one, and the chicken and the bread sauce, the new potatoes and asparagus Ann had cooked so carefully were spoiling. Mrs. Pollard was frantic with anxiety, because she was going to Epping in a charabanc with the Mother's Union that afternoon and was afraid she would be late; and the position was further complicated by Ann's own arrangements, for she had an appointment with Miss Marble at three, and if she let 

Mrs. Pollard go before lunch it meant she would have the washing-up and clearing away to do alone; to think she might keep Miss Marble waiting was unbearable. She wandered into the kitchen again. "Mother promised she wouldn't be late. It's shockingly mean of them. I'll have to leave the plates and dishes until to-night, I simply daren't be late with Miss Marble.

She would think I was careless, and she hates carelessness. 

You'd better go, Mrs. Pollard, if they don't come soon."

Mrs. Pollard was full of self-reproach, but she was fidgeting with her apron strings as if she longed to fling them off. "It's the only 'oliday I 'as, Miss. And I've paid me threepence regular every Monday afternoon. My husband says charas b'aint safe. Give 'im a train, he says; but I like a bit of risk on an outing. We 'as our tea in a 'ut and sings coming home. Not rowdy, Miss. It always sounds sad to me, singing on the roads at night. Many a time I've

NEW BED 

7 had a good cry when I've heard 'em coming home past our street. But those have been mostly club outings, where they take drink-not respectable, like ours is." "Let's dish it up and put it in the oven. If it's ruined we can't help it," said Ann bitterly, for she had meant the meal to please her mother and had worked hard on it all the morning. "Then you wash the pans for me and get off. I don't see why your holiday should be spoilt as well as mine." 

Mrs. Pollard looked at Ann sorrowfully; she imagined the bitterness was meant for her. She felt it was all her fault for daring to plan this one afternoon away from the daily grind. She was full of remorse that she must leave Ann to battle with the washing-up alone; she was humble and apologetic yet at the same time she was terribly anxious lest she should miss this one treat of the year. If she stayed until the last minute and did not change her hat and boots-but then she must go respectable, because the vicar would be there-and her working boots were cracked. There was her new hat, a summer felt, the young lady in the shop had called it, and what chance would she have to show it off again? She banged the pans and dishes in the sink and scrubbed them fiercely; Ann turned down the jets of the gas oven. It was nearly two o'clock. Mrs. Pollard in one last bustle swung the pans on the shelf, swilled down the sink, threw off her apron, thrust it into the dresser drawer, struggled into her mackintosh and went in a flurry of apologies and selfreproach. Her struggle between what she conceived to be her duty and her desire for the outing was at once tragic and comic. Ann, who had never taken very much notice of Mrs. Pollard before, except to wonder why everything was not cleaner when Mrs. Pollard seemed to work so hard, was touched by her loyalty. She went to the

NEW BED 

window, and seeing the charwoman just issuing through the gate and giving a last apologetic look back, waved her on cheerily. "Lunch will certainly be spoilt," said Ann, and washed her hands of it, feeling suddenly cold and exhausted and very hungry. Anxiety, disappointment, irritation, annoyance, had all passed over her, to be succeeded by a calm despair. There would still be time to eat, to change her frock, and meet Miss Marble at three o'clock, if her mother and Charlie Dibbs came soon; but her mother's carelessness, Charlie Dibbs's disregard of her earnest "Do be back by one; I've a very important friend to see this afternoon," when they set off at ten had spoilt her pleasure in the afternoon. It added to her dislike of the large redfaced publican with his jolly laugh, his good-nature, his ostentatious generosity to her mother. He had never been generous to her, Ann thought with a flash of insight caused by her intensified dislike and hunger, because he knew that all the presents in the world would not make her like him or accept him willingly as her mother's friend. She had barely spoken a dozen phrases to him in her life, and those polite and amiable, yet she knew that he knew she thought he was loud, common, and not good enough in manners and breeding to identify himself with them. 

"Charlie's a good sort," her mother had said once when a look on Ann's face had suggested her disapproval. There had been others accepted because they were "good sorts," but Ann had not worried over them as she worried over Charlie. Ann, as a rule, was no judge of character, but her anger, as the minutes went by and still they did not come, went deeper and deeper until it turned into a cold, dispassionate hatred which was clairvoyant. Charlie was a business man. He never gave things away for love of giving. His lavish spending had an object. There was a ruth-

NEW BED 

9 lessness about him she alone had perceived. Because it suited him, because there was something to be gained, he was keeping her mother out. 

She peeped into the oven. The delicate brown of the chicken was darkening, but it smelt good. The vegetables were drying, but all would not be spoilt if they came soon. 

She went into the sitting-room, where the fire glowed red and warm. The table with its silver and freshly laundered linen and daffodils in a cut glass bowl looked attractive. 

Her mother had wanted to impress Charlie Dibbs, and

Ann had done all she could to help her, perhaps because she, also, wanted to impress him. She nibbled a square of cheese, a biscuit or two, an apple from the dish on the sideboard. A noise in the street outside roused her, then she heard her mother's high-pitched laugh, the squeal of a brake, an impudent double hoot of Charlie Dibbs's motor horn. It was his usual way of announcing himself, and Ann's anger worked itself up in a frenzy. "Letting all the street know! So common!" At that moment she wished fervently with all the passion of childhood that he might drop down dead. She fled into the kitchen and began to serve up the food. 

Her mother was apologetic but hilarious. "We stopped to have a drink. . . ." Her smile was fatuous and entirely happy, but Ann felt that at any moment it would turn to an aggrieved pout if she were thwarted. Plainly her mother was in no condition to tell if the chicken was overdone and the sauce thickening into clamminess. The curves of her face were relaxed by the drink she had taken; there was a suggestion of blowsiness, hard to attribute to any particular part of her, for her fair hair was neatly waved, her grey lace dress as fresh as when she first put it on that morning, and she had perfect control of her limbs; it was more a state of mind than an actual manifesta-

ΙΟ 

NEW BED tion; but Ann was aware of it and shrank into herself, chilled with disgust. Charlie Dibbs looked as usual, smiling, red-faced, voluble with banter peculiarly his own. "Don't blame your mother, Annie. It's all my fault.

I'm the naughty boy you want to smack. We've been doing a bit of celebratin', and I've somethin' for you in my pocket that'll soon make you forget our short-comin's."

He had a rich, juicy voice, with a boom he could swell at will in the lower notes. He cast his eye over the table appreciatively, took two bottles of beer from his pocket, then picked up the carvers and began to sharpen them as if he were the man of the house. Ann resisted an impulse to throw the soup tureen at him. Words choked in her throat, and she hurried to and from the kitchen, relieving her feelings on the kitchen door, which closed itself behind her each time she came out and could be given a vicious push each time she wanted to pass through. "This," boomed Charlie, as he carved the chicken dexterously, "is a bit of all right. I must say you've brought up your daughter in the proper way, Ada. If there's one thing I like it's a meal properly set out. . . ." He would have rambled on fulsomely, but Mrs. Jaconelli interposed, after a quick glance at Ann, who was eating her food quickly, with her eyes on her plate, her cheeks burning red with inarticulate anger and disgust. But Charlie Dibbs felt big. He was enjoying himself and he wanted to be congratulated. Ann, silent, vulnerable, piqued him. Although she had given no sign, her quivering nerves were revealed by the intensity of her silence and the bird-like precision with which she ate. "By jingo!" boomed Charlie suddenly, a colossal joke discovered. "We forgot Annie's appointment. But he'll wait for a nice girlie like you. What time did you say you'd meet him? Shall I run you there in the car?"

NEW BED 

II 

Ann swallowed, thrust back the huskiness in her throat, and forced the words from her lips. Her voice was only an ungracious mumble when it came. She felt utterly defenceless. She would have given all she possessed for Miss 

Marble's cold, superior voice and her air of breeding and aloofness with which to humiliate him. "If you've finished I'll take the plates away." She got up quickly, rudely, bent on destroying something if she could not destroy her tormentor, and gathered the plates and dishes together. She knew she should have asked if they wanted another helping, but all she cared for now was to get out of the room into the quiet kitchen. She was struggling with sobs when her mother came after her. "Come and have some pie, dear. Don't be such a baby -showing off because we were late." "It's not that. Not because you were late," sobbed Ann, "it's..." But what was it then? How put into words her fears, her disgust, her hatred of Charlie Dibbs? 

Her mother was uncomprehending. "Now don't start having tempers, Ann, there's a good girl. It's very unkind of you to spoil my bit of pleasure. I'm sure I don't get much nowadays, what with no money coming in and things the price they are. . . ." "But Miss Marble will think I didn't want to come. Ann felt the futility of expecting her mother to understand how important it was, and a fear was also creeping over her of the futility of expecting Miss Marble to understand why she was late. The world was full of misunderstanding. "You run along then. I'll clear the table and make your excuses to Charlie." Her mother became benevolent, goodnatured again. "Him!" snorted Ann contemptuously as she crammed

NEW BED her blue beret on her head, and thrust her arms into her coat. If she were lucky enough to catch a bus straight away she might not be too late. Surely Miss Marble would wait five minutes, or ten.

CHAPTER II 

THE holiday spirit was in the air. Families, never seen together at other times, trailed in holiday garb down the Brompton Road, spreading themselves over the pavement in exasperatingly slow-moving clusters. Ann, rushing furiously along, was brought up time after time to steer her way round toddling infants or straggling parents. She had a wild desire to sweep all obstructions out of her way and leave them heaped on either side of the pavement behind her, like a reaper cutting corn and throwing the sheaves aside as it passed by. She was hot and sticky, her hair stuck to her forehead and her beret was rakishly tilted where she had pushed it to cool her forehead; she was in no proper state to meet Miss Marble.

A glance at a clock as she passed by said ten past three; she prayed it might be fast, but another said the same. She was ten minutes late already and it would still take her five minutes, even if she walked her quickest, to reach the Victoria and Albert Museum, where she had arranged to meet Miss Marble. Miss Marble never listened to explanations; she called them excuses. It was her invariable rule. Ann could not hope it would be relaxed for her now, even though this was a friendly meeting and had no connection with school. Miss Marble had always exacted unquestioning obedience from her disciples. It was one of the reasons, maybe, for the spell she cast over them. She had no human qualities. You gave of your best to her and she accepted the tribute. Many a young girl's spirit had been broken by Miss Marble's autocratic rule. She was just and 13

NEW BED 

righteous, and when they secretly prayed for some sign of common humanity, a little understanding for their failings, her aloofness could be terrifying. Yet it had a magnetic quality, for she had a train of youthful adorers about her always.

Ann slackened her pace. She had done her best. What was time, after all? Surely Miss Marble would wait fifteen minutes? She could imagine herself waiting an hour willingly. She tried to put her thoughts in order. The witty things she meant to say this afternoon as they looked round the museum, the stories she meant to tell of Miss Farthingdyke's brightest children, questions to be asked over tea about Miss Marble herself and old schoolfellows—they had all vanished and her mind was a jumble of worries and despair, as hot and sticky as the rest of herself, out of which she could devise nothing but: "I'm desperately sorry, Miss Marble, my mother kept me," or "You really must forgive me, my mother's friend's car had a breakdown"; vapid, silly sounds meaning nothing. She could imagine the look of displeasure on Miss Marble's thin, aristocratic face. She began to invent fantastic excuses, bus overturned, a seduction, heart attacks-stories of incredible adventures rushed through her mind one after the other, but they were not needed, for she saw at once as she entered the room where they had arranged to meet that Miss Marble was not there. A young man peering intently at a late eighteenth century carved newel post was the only person to be seen.

Ann walked round the room, feeling vaguely let down as she realised the violent attempts of her imagination to find a convincing excuse would not be needed. She wandered into the next room, but it was empty, except for a party of children gazing at wooden cradles and spinning wheels which reminded Ann of the brothers Grimm. Why,

NEW BED 

15 she would tell that to Miss Marble, who delighted in literary allusions. The thought brought her up with a jump; Miss Marble must have gone. Ann retraced her steps, went through the room where the young man still peered at the woodwork, and out into the entrance. There was no sign of the schoolmistress. Perhaps she was late? 

A wild hope raised its head and was decapitated. Miss

Marble was never late. Ann went back to the late eighteenth century woodwork. She could find no consolation in its almost prim ornament of garlanded satyrs and cupids. 

If she could only discover by some means if Miss Marble had been and gone!

The young man still peered attentively. He looked as if he was a fixture in the room. Ann resented, unreasonably, his serene absorption in the carvings when her own mind was a whirl of disappointment and exasperation. She walked to where he stood in contemplation before a newel post carved in the form of a satyr. Garlands linked the satyr's horns and mouth, giving him a sophisticated grin which Ann, in her present mood, disliked. "Silly-looking thing," she said childishly. 

The young man smiled at it fondly. "Oh, no! It's a lovely piece of work. I can hardly tear myself away from it." "Have you been here long? In this room, I mean?” inquired Ann. "Since one o'clock. Had it to myself most of the time, except for a schoolmarm, which is lucky on a Bank Holiday." "It's like playing Hunt-the-thimble; getting warmer," thought Ann. "Here's for another try." "Schoolmarm! You don't mean a tall, thinnish lady, probably in green, rather, well . . not exactly prettydistinguished-looking, wearing pince-nez." Ann fumbled ...

NEW BED 

for words to describe the adored one. "I was supposed to meet a lady here at three. I was delayed. I wondered if she had been and gone away again. It's dreadfully important.' 

Ann's cheeks went pale with the earnestness of her request.

The young man seemed to realise the importance of the question. He looked up and nodded his head in a friendly manner. "It must be the lady you mean. She left a few moments before you came. She looked exceedingly cross and genteel." "Did she speak to you?" Ann could not resist a grin.

Miss Marble speaking to a stranger, particularly if she was making a request, adopted the manner she would use towards one who had displeased her; a manner of exquisite politeness and frigidity. "She asked me if I had seen a young lady waiting about, and if I knew the right time." "She might have waited another five minutes. She might have known I wouldn't keep her waiting out of carelessness." "Is she a school teacher?" asked the young man, not offensively, but as if he wanted his identification to be verified. "Yes," answered Ann, and said no more. The bloom had vanished from her adoration; it could not endure criticism; and even though Ann was appalled at what she was doing she could not stop pulling Miss Marble down from her pedestal, once she had begun. The young man's "schoolmarm" had cut deep. Ann wondered how she could have been so crazy about her. There had been a band of them the last term at school-silly, crude girls! 

What should she do next? At home her mother and

Charlie Dibbs comfortably settled before the fire would have relaxed into an after-lunch nap. If she went home

NEW BED 

17 she knew exactly what would happen. She would make tea and bring it in to them and they would struggle out of their heaviness, looking undignified and rather ugly until sleep had vanished from their faces; and then as soon as it was evening they would go out again, leaving her to practise her music or amuse herself as best she could. 

Prudence, her girl friend, was on holiday; one never visited

Miss Farthingdyke out of school hours without an invitation; the rest of the day therefore stretched endlessly before her. She had a shilling in her purse; she could not even go to a cinema unless she went without tea. Miss

Marble had arranged to take her back to the chaste lodgings she occupied over a greengrocer's shop in Fulham Road. Would Miss Marble be waiting for her? The heartlessness of the schoolmistress's action had left Ann with no desire to seek her out. Miss Marble meant to punish her for being late. Miss Marble believed one should suffer for one's sins and calculated her punishments to a nicety; she made, indeed, something of a fine art of them. 

A strong desire came over Ann to talk to someone from a world where the refinements of feminine unkindness did not exist.

The young man had moved on into the next room. Ann pursued him, deciding he was interesting and not silly.

She liked his shabby suit, his big boots meant for walking country roads, and his pale, serious, rather heavy face. 

He was standing by the spinning wheels when Ann came up to him again. "Reminds me of "The Sleeping Beauty.' You know, where the Princess steals away into the little room at the top of the palace and finds the old woman spinning," she said. 

The young man smiled. "Look at their long spindly legs. Very delicately made, yet no suggestion of weakness.

NEW BED

Obviously an article for feminine use. They feel right. 

They have a beauty apart from their romantic associations. 

Those old craftsmen knew their job." 

Ann agreed. Clearly he was a person to whom one might speak without fear of being mistaken for a minx. "The cradles too. Fairy-tale cradles. It would be a cradle just like that," she said, pointing to a carved wooden trough on rockers, "the Queen would be rocking when

Rumpelstiltskin came along to claim the baby. It makes it all very real, doesn't it? See how worn the rockers are."

The young man replied seriously that it did; then moved to examine the carving on a chair of walnut wood. "Come and have a look at this," he called. "The shell pattern is perfect."

Ann agreed with him. Although it seemed to her that it was a little too perfect, too conventional, the young man's intense earnestness and air of knowing his subject made her feel she must be appreciative; also she desired humbly and sincerely to be initiated into the mystery of what one should or shouldn't like in art. They walked on together, talking of the woodwork. Ann found herself making surprisingly clever remarks, as if the novelty of the companionship were giving her brain sharp nudges, awakening them to a full appreciation of the things around her.

They came at last to the entrance hall and stood awkward and self-conscious for the first time that afternoon. They did not want to part, yet each became aware the other was a stranger, an unknown quantity, rather frightening, but very attractive. They said "Good-bye," but did not part, fidgeting uncomfortably until the young man said, stifling a yawn apologetically: "It's the air in here. Do you know this neighbourhood? Where can I find a tea shop? I'm only a visitor to London. Got to go back to-night-midnight."

NEW BED 

19 It was a piece of information about himself and it broke down the stranger-out-of-nowhere feeling which had overwhelmed Ann and made her timid when they came to say good-bye. "There are several; I'll show you. Lyons, A.B.C. or just tea-shop?" "Anything at all," he said. "But don't let me take you out of your way." "Silly, stupid, polite idiot," fumed Ann. "Why are people so stuffy with each other?" She walked by his side sedately. They seemed to have lost contact again; to have become cautious strangers. The young man, evidently, was searching for a remark to bring back their former intimacy. "I wonder what's become of the lady you had to meet?" "I was supposed to spend the evening with her. I can't very well go now, can I? I'm afraid I missed my tea," answered Ann laughing. Would he take a hint, she wondered. • • • "Would you Do you think you could . . . I mean 

I should be very pleased 

"It's very sweet of you," said Ann happily, cutting short his groping after formality. "I'm just dying for tea. I had a scramble at lunch time.. ." She found herself telling him about the lunch and Charlie Dibbs, Mrs. Pollard, everything that had happened. Such an outpouring! They sat nearly two hours over tea.

CHAPTER III 

MRS. JACONELLI, coming home from an evening spent in sipping port with Charlie Dibbs in the lounge of The Grand Hotel, was surprised to see a light burning in the sitting-room and to hear the sound of music. It was eleven. o'clock, and Ann was usually abed at that time. The music was familiar. It was one of her husband's songs, and because she had never cared for it it had been seldom sung. 

Ann must have been going through the old music in the cabinet. Standing in the hall, Mrs. Jaconelli fancied she heard a man's voice singing, and stood still to listen. Her mind was blurred a little by the wine she had drunk, so that the past seemed almost as real as the present. Her husband had sung this song to her some weeks before Ann was born. She remembered so well. She had been feeling depressed all day, frightened, reproaching him for the child that was coming and which she had not wanted. He had taken her hands and said: "Come, Ada, and I will sing you my new song," because he could never quite get over the wonder of having written a new song and expected her to share it. She had let him lead her to the piano. Instead of cheering her it had made her cry, and he had almost cried himself when he saw what he had done. He had been tender with her always. Charlie Dibbs was different. He was a man, if you like. He always got what he wanted. She was sorry in one sense that she had promised to marry him. Being married wasn't the same as being courted with a man like Charlie; she saw through his goodnature sufficiently to know that. Ann was going to be a 20

NEW BED 21 problem-to make her see that it was for the best. She was a good, obedient child, but not much go in her. Although she was so pretty, she did not seem to attract the men. Too young, wrapped up in her school affairs. Those silly schoolmistresses, teaching her that "don't-touch-me" look, the dried-up marms. Ann's refinement wasn't a mannerism; it was part of her character. Her mother told herself she was rather afraid of it. She didn't want to make Ann unhappy.

Although the sound was deadened by the heavy curtain over the door Mrs. Jaconelli felt sure it was a man's voice singing-a voice unknown to her. She listened intently: 

Travelling a lonely way, Walking till the close of day, In a cobbled village street 

Rested I my weary feet. 

In the morning I rose up, Drank a pleasant parting cup 

With a maiden who said "Stay- 

Do not go so soon away." 

Long and steadfast was her look

As her clinging hands I took, Saying, "I must hold the quest 

Till the end: there is no rest.

I am seeking high and low, Everywhere my feet must go."

The singing ceased when Mrs. Jaconelli opened the sitting-room door. Ann was at the piano, with a strange young man beside her holding a faded sheet of music in his hand, his head held as if he had abandoned himself to

NEW BED the singing. There was an awkward silence for a second, then the young man flushed deep red; she watched the colour spread gradually till it reached his ears. "Oh, it's Mother," said Ann, turning an animated face to the door where her mother stood. "Mother, this is a friend of mine, Mr. William Darwin. He lives up north and he's got to catch a train at midnight. We didn't quite know what to do, so I thought I'd better bring him home. 

I knew you'd be out. I'm just going to make coffee." She slipped away with a smile at Bill to reassure him.

The young man came forward and shook hands sheepishly. "It's rather unconventional, I'm afraid. Your daughter insisted I should come. I thought that as I had made her acquaintance you would like to make mine. . . ." He stammered a little, yet only because of the awkwardness of explaining himself. It was evident he felt no embarrassment at being found alone with Ann so late at night. Mrs. 

Jaconelli summed him up at once, feeling a subtle dislike, while she acknowledged that here was no adventurer taking advantage of Ann's childlike friendliness. She was certain at once she could have trusted Ann alone with him all night, which was a tribute from Mrs. Jaconelli, for she knew the world and had no faith in men; at the same time it made her despise him. His clothes were shabby, his boots were like the boots she had seen on the feet of navvies, his hair was badly cut, and his linen was badly laundered, but he did not look poor or oppressed; experience had taught her to distinguish poverty, even when it was well-dressed. 

If she had not been told she would have soon guessed he was from the provinces. His face had distinction without elegance, and his clear, cold eyes intimidated her. He was the type of man she had always instinctively avoided; the type that is so seldom taken in by the wiles and cajoleries

NEW BED 

23 of a middle-aged woman of pleasure; the type whose ignorance and honesty protects them and who have no obvious looseness of character for such a woman to play upon. A Quaker, as likely as not. What a genius Ann had for making friends with unprofitable, uncomfortable folk!

Mrs. Jaconelli exerted herself to be pleasant, although she did not feel quite comfortable in the presence of this stranger from the north. She felt her lace frock was too fussy, the pink chiffon vest too low, her whole appearance too dazzling in contrast with Ann's shabby blue jersey.

Ann was very happy. Her new friend was the most interesting person she had ever met. He seemed able to answer all the questions she had ever wanted to ask. His store of knowledge about books and art seemed unfathomable. He had promised to lend her books, to send her some eighteenth century dance music to practise for his next visit, and, best of all, to make her a wooden figure—a miniature fawn playing a pipe. When he had gone her mother said: "What is he?” "He carves wood, but I don't think he does it for a living." "Didn't you ask him?” "Oh, Mother!" Ann protested impatiently. "Don't you know anything about him at all? Where did you meet him?” "Miss Marble" Ann stammered, but could not tell her tale. "He's quite nice. He knows an awful lot. He made Miss Marble seem-well, not half so clever as I thought she was."

There was truth and there was compromise. If she told the whole story of their meeting her mother would read an interpretation into it that would spoil its happiness for ever. Ann's instinct was to conceal her adventures, for her

NEW BED mother had a way, without saying very much, of rubbing off the bloom and revealing them as commonplace and insignificant, so that Ann would feel embarrassed and long to cover them up with any flimsy rag of a tale she could invent. Her mother, she knew, had an insatiable appetite for facts about people-Ann could never see the importance of them, so she detailed the few items of information her new friend had let fall during the afternoon and evening. "He lives in his own house. It has two laburnum trees in front, one on each side of the gate, and a holly hedge. 

It was built for his great-grandfather, who owned land in the neighbourhood. Bill's father was a market gardener, and he died some years ago. He has a mother and three sisters; the youngest is still at school. Bill was in the army during the war, and he hasn't been able to take up his wood-carving again until this year. The war took it all out of him. I'm sure he's very nice, Mother." "Well, he's not what you call smart, is he?" said Mrs. 

Jaconelli. "And I don't see it's much good wasting your time on someone who lives two hundred miles away. He can't be much use to you." "> Ann's happiness oozed away. It was uncanny how her mother, whom she considered obtuse and without understanding, always hit on a vital spot. Two hundred miles away had not seemed any distance when they talked of it, but now it had become as inaccessible as another continent. 

There was the post. He would write. And come again soon. 

Her mother broke in on her thoughts. "Have you forgotten the present Charlie brought you?"

Ann opened the box her mother handed to her. It contained a silver-chain bag. Ann held it out ironically on one finger and tip-toed mincingly round the room, overcome

NEW BED 

25 suddenly by a flood of wanton merriment too urgent to be suppressed. "I should look silly walking along in my old school coat holding that!" The picture of herself prancing down 

Brompton Road, holding out the little silver bag, seemed exquisitely funny. She began to laugh. "Don't be so wild, Ann. It's a pretty bag. It's silver right through. It wasn't bought at Woolworth's, let me tell you. You should be pleased. Some girls- 

Ann dropped the bag into its box. Her cheeks were flushed and her eyes brilliant with wild mocking laughter. "You can give it back to him. Or keep it yourself. I don't like him, or his presents-ugly, fat, vulgar creature!”

Mrs. Jaconelli turned to the door. The gold of her hair had a hard metallic glitter, unkind to the heavy pouches under her eyes and her sagging cheek muscles. "You don't know anything," she said harshly, her voice thick with tears. "You think you're so clever, reading books, ticking people off. With all your ideas. What's the good of them? 

Tell me that? You can't earn your own living. I was singing in "Tosca' at your age. I'm going to bed."

Ann's hysteria was over. She was frightened now. She had not meant to be so outspoken, but a madness mounting to her head had forced the words from her. She had a terrible fear that she was a prig, after all. She hastened to build up again the old friendliness between them. "I'm sorry, Mum. You go on up. I'll bring the hotwater bottle. You'll be able to have a special breakfast in bed to-morrow seeing it's holidays."

She could not bear that her mother should be annoyed with her, and their pleasant pose of affectionate mother and daughter collapse and the hollowness beneath it be revealed. In the wreckage they might lose one another

NEW BED completely. She fussed upstairs with the hot-water bottle, made herself helpful in the bedroom, hung up the lace dress, put away the best hat, talking all the time. "I like you in that lace dress, Mum. Lace suits you. Ecru lace would be nice." "Charlie wants me to have a grey costume-French grey with a silver fox fur." "Lovely," said Ann dutifully. "I thought of green for you-fresh, young green. A long coat with a frock of crêpe de Chine to match the coat lining, a dainty pattern, rather quaint, little sprigs of flowers, say, and a floppy pale green picture hat. We'll have a look in the shops."

Ann went off to bed wondering. It was a long time since her mother had talked of clothes in such an extravagant and wholly delightful fashion. She fell asleep to dream of a garden where Bill sat carving a nymph out of a tree trunk, while from behind another a satyr peeped, his horns and mouth linked up with garlands; and into this garden she strolled in her pretty new coat and her rosebud-sprigged dress, dangling a wide-brimmed summer hat by its long green ribbons. "Why, she has come to life, my nymph,” cried Bill, and rose up, changing as he did so into the sly-faced satyr with garlands in his hands with which to bind her. She tried to remonstrate, and then, as fear overwhelmed her, to run away, but her feet were rooted fast. She awoke in terror, her mouth parched and dry, with her heart beating wildly in her throat.

She was afraid to go to sleep again, and it was too early to get up. She began to amuse herself by saying verses as she used to do when she was at boarding school, and had awakened in the early morning and had been afraid to get out of bed for a book. The words of one of her father's

NEW BED 

27 songs came into her mind. She had read it the night before, when she had been hunting out old music, and now, as she said it over, its cadences brought tears to her eyes. 

Along the trembling breeze it came, Huskily faint, like a muffled horn,

Sounding deep in silent woods; 

Tremulous as the young dawn 

That fain would keep the solitudes 

Ever veiled, unlit by the flame 

Of the great sun that bares the breast

Of the dew-laved earth. It came alone, Echoed and quivering down the vale, Falling as softly as blossom blown 

In a gentle spring, wistfully frail 

Pan was piping down in the west. 

The music had never been finished. Perhaps her father had been satisfied by the words alone. What did it mean?

What had it meant to her father that he had chosen them for his song? She would never know. No one would ever know. The thought was a sharp, sweet pain. A sense of the mystery and sadness of life came over her, in which her little white bed, the blue curtains at her window, through which the grey morning light shone wanly, the shelf of books, the few mementoes of her school life which adorned the room, even her old red dressing-gown behind the door, which she had long outgrown, all took on an almost unbearably poignant significance. 

The impression faded. Milk-carts began to rattle in distant streets. Ann closed her eyes again and fell into a calm sleep.

CHAPTER IV 

"WELL, Annie," said Charlie Dibbs, "and how will I do for a father?" His large plump face was shining with selfsatisfaction. "Has your mother bought you that new dress yet?" His voice suggested that he was enchanted with the idea of bestowing new frocks upon his daughter-to-be. 

Ann smiled shyly and edged to the door. She did not want him for a father, and his air of bland patronage irritated her; but if her mother was determined to marry him Ann did not see how she could do anything about it except keep out of his way as much as possible. She hoped it would mean they would be able to keep a maid now instead of Mrs. Pollard coming for occasional half-days, for she did not like to think she would have to wait on him. "You are not going, are you?" he asked. "Where's your mother?" "I'll go and fetch her," said Ann hurriedly, glad of the excuse to get out of the room and intending not to come back until he had gone. "You'd better stay. There's one or two little things we ought to talk about concerning you. I'll fetch her." 

He went to the door and bawled out: "Ada! Come on down. You needn't stop upstairs titivating yourself up now you've hooked me."

He shook with laughter at his joke, but it received no appreciation from Ann. She gave him a quick, supercilious glance, then turned her back on him and went to the window. She stood looking out into the front garden, where two mangy cats manipulated their lean bodies in and out 28

NEW BED 

29 of the railings in dignified pursuit of one another. She was perplexed and miserable. Her mother had said marriage with Charlie Dibbs was necessary, for her few remaining. pupils finished at the end of the summer term and she had no new ones for the autumn. She owed rent and was in debt to several tradesmen. Charlie was not a gentleman, but he was a "good sort" and would look after them, and, God knows, they wanted someone to do that. It was more than she could stand, struggling along from hand to mouth.

Ann had not liked to ask where her mother's new clothes had come from all this time she had been struggling along from hand to mouth, or the bottles of wine, the chickens and fruit, the week-ends at Brighton. She was afraid to probe further, to ask for plain facts and figures instead of vague generalities-that is, if she could have forced plain facts and figures from her mother, which she doubted. Her mother had brought equivocation to a fine art. It was humiliating that she, Ann, should have to depend on Charlie Dibbs to help her to live until she passed her music exams. She would work harder than she had ever done before, she told herself, setting her teeth hard. She must be sure of passing with honours if possible—and then she would be able to earn her own living and be independent. She would get a post as resident music mistress, perhaps at a co-educational school. It might be jolly. She would have to hang on at home enduring Charlie Dibbs. for another two or three years before she would be sufficiently qualified.

Her mother came into the room. Although Ann had her back to them she could feel her mother and Charlie Dibbs looking at her, exchanging unspoken comments. She turned round and came to the fire-place, sitting down rather tentatively on the edge of a chair, as though the occasion did not call for ease.

NEW BED 

"You should get a bit for this furniture, Ada. It's good stuff, if old-fashioned. Folks are going back to walnut-"

Charlie strolled about the room with the air of a professional auctioneer. "Are we going to leave here, then?" asked Ann, dismayed. "We've got to live where the money is as keeps the home going," answered Charlie sententiously. "Charlie has got a business," began Mrs. Jaconelli, feeling her way carefully. "Not so much palaver, Ada. It's a pub, a nice little pub. 'Ye Old Thatched Inn.' Three hundred years old. It's just been pulled down—we had to blast some of the walls, they were so thick and tough—and a nice new place built instead. In the old-fashioned style, mind you, but modern improvements; black and white, nice new thatched roof, sundial, crazy paving, the garden, all as pretty as a picture postcard. To catch the motorists' eye. And on the main north road out of London.” "Why did you pull it down if you wanted an old-world kind of place?" asked Ann irrelevantly. She was thinking:

Just the hideous vulgar thing he would do. Destroy something really old and lovely and build a cheap gaudy imitation in its place.

Charlie sensed the criticism. He flushed, then almost visibly brushed her remark aside and went on: "When it's finished that's where we are all going to live. We're going to have a tea-garden at the back. Nothing cheap, mind you; all good class, and charge a good price. No picnickers and hot-water, or chara parties. Highclass is my style; that's where the money is. Young chaps in two-seaters out with their girls wanting to show off a bit-they always pay. Natty little tables, basket chairs and fancy crockery amongst the flowers, and a couple of pretty

NEW BED 

31 girls in frilled muslin to attend to them. That's going to be your department. You won't have to do any work except walk round in a swell rig-out like a perfect lady and take the money and keep your eye on the girls. How does that suit yer?"

Charlie Dibbs put his hands on his knees and leaned. forward confidentially. He had no fear, apparently, that his ideas would not get a kind reception. Ann was too overwhelmed with horror to speak. "Well," he repeated, "how does that suit yer?" “I don't think I'd be very good at it,” Ann ventured, inwardly swearing a mighty oath she would not even try. "It's a very pretty place. Everything in the house part beautifully arranged. I'm sure when it's decorated you'll like it, Ann. Charlie is going to bring the papers for us to choose, and the upholstery and curtains." "That's a ladies' job," purred Charlie. "You've got taste, Ada; and a place of this sort is nowhere without taste." He made it sound as if it was something tangible that could be laid on like hot water or central heating. 

A picture of her mother complete with taste behind a bar, chaffing with men as she poured out their drinks, came before Ann's eyes. It was like an impossible nightmare. "Where does Mother come in all this, apart from choosing the papers?" She had not meant her tone to be so hostile. "Your mother will be mistress of the place," said 

Charlie, and added jovially, “and of me. Let me tell you it's a little gold-mine, a little gold-mine. It was before we pulled it down; now, with more room, modern arrangements and the tea garden in summer. 

You're a young girl. You don't know anything about the world, but let me tell you" • · · "I suppose you will pay me a salary?" interrupted Ann.

NEW BED "If I leave Miss Farthingdyke's school I'll have to get my music lessons elsewhere, and a good teacher is rather expensive. I get them now for nothing, because I work for her. If I give them up I shan't be able to pass my exams." "I must have it all clear," Ann thought desperately, "or

I shall get myself in an awful mess, without a chance of ever being independent.' 

Charlie Dibbs broke into rude laughter. "She's your daughter right enough, Ada. And us thinking she was a shy, couldn't-say-boo-to-a-goose kind of kid. 

Salary indeed! And what do you think you are worth, I'd like to know, except as an ornament. I could get fifty girls like you the market's flooded with 'em-for five bob a week and their keep." "Now then, Charlie," remonstrated Mrs. Jaconelli; but she herself was startled at Ann's question. Charlie had made it sound so attractive. A house was always well patronised, he had said, when there was a handsome woman, pleasant-spoken and well-dressed, to make the customers welcome. Neither she nor Ann need soil their hands. They could have everything they wanted. All they would have to do would be to keep their eye on things and take the money. He didn't believe in trusting hired girls with cash; it was more satisfactory if the family handled it. 

How explain this to Ann? Ann was so young and proper, wrapped up in her own childish pursuits, with no idea of how difficult life could be and no idea of the compromises one had to make to get comforts and money. She had fought with all the arts she knew to win Charlie and make herself safe for the rest of her life. She was not going to allow Ann to upset everything. 

Her mouth hardened and her blue eyes went cold. She took up the reins again. Until she was safely married it would not do to relax a moment.

NEW BED 

33 "It will be all right, Ann. Surely you trust me to do what is best for us both?"

Ann went to the door. She stood holding the curtain trying to make her departure seem natural. She could not bear to stay in the room any longer now that she knew --what she had always known deep down in her heartthat her mother was not on her side. Her mother was fencing with her, putting her off, and if she went to live at "Ye Old Thatched Inn" she would be doomed to stay there for the rest of her life. Whatever they might say about talking affairs over with her, their minds were made up. The uselessness of argument against the two of them banded together stupefied her. She did not know what to say or how to plead for the life she wanted. "Very well, Mother," she murmured, with her eyes on the floor, for she could not bear even to look at them, so acute was the feeling of betrayal. Without waiting for them to answer she slipped away.

She would have to strike out on her own, Ann decided, after long hours of self-communion. To live with her mother and Charlie Dibbs and make herself agreeable to men in bar parlours, which was what she imagined it would come to in the end, horrified her. 

Would it be possible to persuade Miss Farthingdyke to give her a salary instead of pocket money? A very small salary would suffice, Ann thought, aglow with the idea of sacrifice for the sake of her ideals. She went over in her mind the various duties she performed at school, and was hopeful. Miss Farthingdyke was kind; her "dear child" could sound quite maternal and comforting, but, thought Ann without cynicism, that was when Miss Farthingdyke wanted some extra service from her student teacher, or when Ann had been successful in taking over an absent teacher's duty. Miss Farthingdyke had a way of promising

NEW BED 

things without actually committing herself, and she had often suggested how pleasant it would be when Ann was promoted to an official status in the school with her name on the list of teachers in the syllabus. Ann felt Miss Farthingdyke was fond of her. She had been especially pleased this last term, since Ann had taken over a good deal of the music, including games and dancing; this was Miss Farthingdyke's own work, for which she was highly qualified and which she advertised as one of the school's attractions. 

Miss Farthingdyke had kept up a fiction of supervising her lessons, of course, but Ann had done most of the work. 

Ann knew the children enjoyed her lessons, for she had had no trouble with discipline, which was more than the other teachers could say in a school where self-discipline and the latest educational ideas were carried to the extreme. 

In Miss Farthingdyke's school the children ruled. They did as they liked-it was advertised so, worded a little more scientifically-and attracted the fond mothers of the district who were willing to pay highly so that their young children should have a good time. In addition to the music 

Ann told stories to the tiniest ones, shepherded the children who took swimming lessons to and from the swimming baths, helped to dress and undress them when there, supervised the tiny gardens and the raffia work, which meant doing most of it herself; drew charts and diagrams, guided baby fingers over the alphabet, handed round the milk at lunch-time and watched that each child drank its own; tidied the cloak-room and looked after slipper bags, pacified nursemaids whose charges had put most of the modelling clay on their clothes and hair, quelled riots in the French class, which was taken by an anæmic mademoiselle always sighing for a cane, by removing the ringleaders and playing ball with them on the modern principle of wearing down their superfluous energy, and as many

NEW BED 35 other jobs as Miss Farthingdyke could find her to do and could be fitted into the day. Ann enjoyed her work and loved the children, from the terror of the school, a stout boy of eight who kicked indiscriminately when he was bored and whom Ann had mastered by apparently playful but really purposeful smacks when he was obstreperous, to the tiniest lisping toddler. She liked to think she was indispensable. Hadn't Miss Farthingdyke herself hinted the same thing? Ann told herself valiantly: "I'm worth a salary."

Some days later she endeavoured to introduce the subject and discovered Miss Farthingdyke thought differently.

Ann's confidence had weakened a little since the idea had first come to her and she made a mistake in the opening move. "You said, didn't you, that the tinies got on just as well with me last summer when Miss Brown was away ill?” "You were really wonderful, my dear, when I think what little experience you have had and your qualifications. 

Of course, if Miss Brown had stayed away longer, I should have had to take a hand myself. Let me see . . it was the time little Roma Rice caught a bad chill. I remember her mother complaining we let the child sit on the grass to watch the games. Miss Brown would have noticed more carefully, perhaps, being experienced. One has to be very careful in this profession. There are mothers and nurses, particularly nurses, always ready to blame us for anything that might happen to their children. I noticed a day or two ago you let Marjorie Jones share the biscuits she brought for lunch with little Ida." "But Ida had left her biscuits at home, and Marjorie

Jones had more than she could eat," protested Ann. "It didn't seem right to refuse to let her perform an unselfish act. She is such a selfish child as a rule."

NEW BED "You should have fetched Ida a biscuit from the kitchen.

Marjorie Jones probably told her mother she gave Ida a biscuit, and it is very likely her mother will ask me to be sure Marjorie eats her lunch, because otherwise she is not getting her proper amount of food. There is a great deal to learn about young children. I am always learning," went on Miss Farthingdyke, very much the principal. Ann was intimidated and went away sadly, deciding to wait a more favourable opportunity to plead her cause. Miss Farthingdyke called her back. "My dear child, don't imagine I'm criticising you." She put her hand on Ann's arm affectionately. "We must all take a hand with Miss Brown's class until I find a new mistress. If you will take them this morning .. the tinies must go without their story. Find them something to do and take both classes into the big room, and then you can keep your eye on them both at the same time." 

Ann's second attempt was not more successful. "I can get student teachers-girls from the university -who would be glad to come for nothing for the sake of the experience. Of course I shouldn't allow that. I think a little pocket-money well, it is an encouragement; and then there are books and overalls to buy.. .. I like my student teachers to have the latest books on education." "My mother is leaving the neighbourhood. If I can't earn a little more I'm afraid I can't stay on here after the summer term. She is unable to give me an allowance to live in diggings." 

Miss Farthingdyke was sympathetic. "My poor child!

Let me have a chat with your mother about an allowance. 

Come and take tea with me on Sunday. I'm sure I could talk to her and show her how necessary it is for your career that you should go on with your music under my care."

Ann felt unequal to explaining that it was impossible,

37 and Charlie Dibbs would never allow her to live in lodgings at his expense. Miss Farthingdyke, without saying so, had made it quite clear she did not mean to change 

Ann's pocket-money into salary. "Mean old devil," said Prudence, to whom Ann confided her desire for independence. Prudence had no illusions about Miss Farthingdyke. "Why on earth did you take up music for a career?" "I suppose it is—well—a kind of family failing," sighed 

Ann. "Mother only thinks in terms of marriage for women. 

When I left school Miss Marble implored her to let me go to college and train for something-money wasn't so tight then-but Mother wouldn't. She wanted me at home, she said; I could find a light, ladylike occupation, if I must do something. I just went on with my music, and it was rather decent of Miss Farthingdyke to give me lessons for nothing. NEW BED of the romance of living when one had a comfortable job in an office and adoring, well-to-do parents. Ann began to see that in this matter of earning a livelihood she depended on herself alone. Her mother was to be married at the beginning of August, when "Ye Old Thatched Inn" would be ready to receive her. She had the whole of the summer term in which to find something suitable. Her mother thought Ann was reconciled to the move, but they did not discuss it. There was a shyness between them now. Mrs. 

Jaconelli was busy turning out cupboards and boxes, destroying rubbish and packing treasures. Every day Mrs. 

Pollard went home laden with things saved from the dustman or the old-clothes woman.

Ann said she would turn out the music cabinet and burn any old music they did not want. It was a good opportunity to see what was really hidden away there. At the bottom of a drawer Ann discovered a set of verses wrapped round an old photograph. It was so faded Ann could barely make out a white house set on a wooded hill overlooking a large stretch of water; a wild romantic scene, made all the more strange by the foreign name of the photographer stamped on the mount. Ann recognised the verses as the words of one of her father's songs. She showed the picture to her mother. "It was where your grandmother lived-your father's mother-when she was a girl. Somewhere in Italy. Your father used to say he was going there, but he never did. 

Fancy it turning up now!" She stretched out her hand and put it on the fire with some other papers she was burning. 

Ann watched it burn before going back to her work. 

That was the end of her father's secret longing—to be burnt as rubbish without a kind thought going after it except her own. He had probably cherished through the discomforts of winter-touring in England a hope of visiting

NEW BED 

39 that white house set on the hill; maybe he had thought how beautiful his songs would sound sung beneath its windows on a gracious summer evening. And had it comforted him? Ann hummed the song of the picture to herself as she sorted the music into piles. 

Over the water 

Framed in the sky, Grey in the morning 

Ships passing by; 

Tall towers soaring, Domes underneath, Trees the town circling 

Green in a wreath; 

Chiming bells echo 

Over the bay- 

Golden the sun comes 

Calling the day. 

Over the water 

Framed in the sky; 

All the town gleaming, Ships passing by.

It was a croon, rather than a well-defined melody. She wished she had known her father. He would have helped her fight against Charlie Dibbs. It was true he did not look a fighting sort in the pictures she had seen of him, nor did his verses suggest a fighting spirit; no-they would just have run away together, perhaps to that same white house. 

They would have wandered in the dark woods that were shown in the picture and she would have told him about

NEW BED 

Bill and his wood-carving, and he would have made a song about it. She was sure he must have been a delightful lover in his youth. Ann wondered what her mother had been like in those days; pretty, she knew, with round cheeks and high breasts, pouting lips and a little curl on her forehead like the girl in the nursery rhyme. She had seen photographs in which her mother's prettiness had been triumphant, in spite of the weird clothes. Couldn't her mother feel the melancholy of giving up her home, which must be more full of memories for her than for her daughter; this turning out of hoarded fragments of the past, had it affected her also? Ann, filled suddenly with sadness, felt affection for her mother bubble into the desire to speak to her, and ran into the kitchen, where her mother was burning papers on the kitchen stove. "Aren't you sorry, Mum, leaving here? The things you are taking won't look the same in any other house," she said impulsively. "It will be all different and horrid, shining with new varnish and silly imitation quaintness." 

Mrs. Jaconelli straighted her back and sighed. “I'm not so fond of leaving as you think. I've been someone here-Madame Jaconelli-a name which meant something once-but it's better to move on than to stay and moulder away in a backwater without any life or company. I'm not getting younger." "Oh, Mum, you don't look old. When you are dressed you look younger than Miss Farthingdyke and prettier.

Your complexion is marvellous now. Couldn't we stay as we are in a year or two I'll be getting a decent salary, enough to keep us in comfort, anyway. We might find a pretty little flat; something modern that you'd like. 

Couldn't you sell something-silver, jewellery-to keep us till then?" "The silver's mostly electro-plate and my rings and

NEW BED 

4I brooches are paste. The real stones went long ago. This place and this neighbourhood give me the horrors now, anyway. It's like a tomb; all past and no hope for the future. Where are the families who used to live round here? Gone, and their houses made into flats. Whoever comes to call but a few old has-beens or the curate wanting me to sing for nothing at his mother's meeting? The best thing is to clear out; a new life, new surroundings. . . ." "How things change," thought Ann. "Only a few weeks ago I was as happy as could be, looking forward to Easter Monday with Miss Marble, and now everything is sad and ugly and tangled-up." She sensed that her mother, also, felt the desolation of change and was fighting frantically against it. "I didn't mean to upset you, Mother. I only thought · • Mrs. Jaconelli shook the last of the papers into the fire and took off her overall briskly. "Put your things on and we'll go to the pictures. We've been getting the pip over this turning out. I'll treat you to a Neopolitan ice at the Corner House afterwards-all the colours of the rainbow with cream and nuts—you'll like it."

She laughed girlishly as she busied herself powdering her face and curling the ends of her yellow hair under her smart hat.

Ann asked herself sternly what right had she to criticise her mother's hankering after colour and gaiety? As they set off she felt very much older and far more baffled by the complexities of life.

CHAPTER V 

BILL sent Ann a small figure of a shepherd boy playing a pipe. It was carved out of a single bough, the natural curve of the wood making the upward slanting line of the body, which was raised on tip-toe and leaned forward a little as though the shepherd boy were piping to someone in the distance. Ann cherished it. Every day as soon as she came back from school she ran up to her room to look at it, and often it would be in her thoughts, pleasing them. Bill seemed rather startled at the ardour of her appreciation, but he liked it, and his letters became more frequent and less formal. He was seeking, he said, on one of the rare occasions when he wrote about his work, a female figure, a Phyllis for his Corydon, but not of the buxom shepherdess type; his idea was a figure with the immature and tender limbs of girlhood and with features showing no signs of worldliness and sex. Ann wrote and asked who was his model for the shepherd boy. The figure, diminutive as it was, had become more real to her than many of the people with whom she was surrounded; the long lean arms, the narrow boyish chest, the thin face with its curly hair growing well off the high forehead, the deep eyesockets, and the lips pursed up holding the pipe thrilled her as no human being had ever done. She would look at it yearningly until she could almost imagine she could hear the thin high tune of his pipe. 

Bill replied that a young cousin of his, Paul Darwin, had given him the original inspiration. Paul was a musician who, when he played the violin or even on ordinary oc- 42

NEW BED 

43 casions, fell naturally into attitudes so graceful that Bill could not resist the desire to put them down. From odd remarks in Bill's letters Ann made up pictures of them together; Bill, solemn, rather formal, with his heavy Roman face and clumsy figure and broad, strong workman's hands, that were scarred in places where a tool had slipped, and Paul, slim and fine, with his narrow chest and long, lean arms and thin, delicate fingers; Paul would be acting as Bill's model under the apple trees in the orchard, or, another time, Bill would be chipping at his wood while Paul played the violin in the old barn which served Bill as a workshop; pictures of an idyllic world, they seemed to Ann, where the commonplace or petty never entered and life had masculine breadth and proportion.

She did not write and tell Bill of her troubles at home, but when he wrote to say he would be coming to town soon she answered: "I am going to ask your advice about something when you come, because I think you are wise and know a tremendous lot." Bill was disinterested, she thought; he would say what was best for her, whereas her mother and Miss Farthingdyke could not help thinking what was best for themselves as well. She wanted to have something definite to tell him about her endeavours to find a new job, so she began to study the advertisement columns of the papers. She made an attempt to tell her mother about her plans for the future, but Mrs. Jaconelli was busy furnishing "Ye Old Thatched Inn," and when addressed answered abstractedly, with a tolerant smile that showed plainly she did not or would not take Ann seriously. Charlie Dibbs, however, scored a triumph in bringing an Airedale puppy for Ann to take charge of until the new home was ready. Ann was so pleased with it and gave 

Charlie so many bright glances and smiles of delight, that he congratulated himself for hours afterwards.

NEW BED 

The puppy was the one joyful thing in Ann's life at that period, for at school also was upheaval and disorder, Miss Farthingdyke being seized by spasms of doubt, owing to a new book she had just read deploring most of the methods of education used in her type of school. Ann was first harassed but willing to alter her ways, then bewildered because Miss Farthingdyke did not seem quite clear as to the changes she wanted and expected Ann to know without being told, and finally annoyed when she discovered the other teachers carrying on exactly as before with an occasional gesture towards the new ideas when 

Miss Farthingdyke visited their classes. Ann was astonished at the cynical views on children and their education which came out in the confabulations amongst the staff over the new methods. It seemed as if the world she knew was falling about her in ruin. She took a few days off and turned her attentions to the "situations vacant" columns in the papers, remembering Prudence and her advice about the more unusual kinds of jobs. 

Ann decided she did not want to be a clerk, a governess, a shop assistant, a domestic worker, or a nurse. She could not be a hairdresser, a dispenser, a secretary, a chauffeuse, or a lady gardener, because she had no training. She had a vague idea that it would be jolly to be a welfare worker or a factory inspector, but she could not quite imagine what their duties would be except to get up concerts and rambling clubs amongst the employees, and she felt that something more must be needed. She began to wonder, as she went down column after column of advertisements and found nothing suitable, if Charlie Dibbs had not been right about untrained girls.

Then at last she found a job which promised to be both interesting and remunerative. A fine art society wanted a lady assistant for their showrooms. The address was in the

NEW BED 

45 neighbourhood of Bond Street. Ann had seen such shops, with one picture in a massive frame in the window, a piece of porcelain, perhaps, or a bronze, while inside was a marvellously carpeted floor, polished wood, a chair or two that were works of art in themselves, all making up an atmosphere deep and rich as of old mellowed varnish and hushed as though in deference to the antiquity and expensiveness of their wares. In such a place one moved with dignity and grace; one's voice changed from the accents of everyday to rarer, more refined tones; one's hands grew fine and tender touching precious things. Ann wrote her application with great care, thankful she had always taken pride in her handwriting and that her mother's notepaper was good.

She had a reply making an appointment for an interview at a private address in South Kensington after six o'clock. When she entered the block of mansion flats and was directed to the lift by the commissionaire she was trembling with excitement. The flats looked very expensive, with a severe, aloof, almost intimidating elegance that made Ann painfully aware of her own insignificance. 

The liftman had taken on the colour and atmosphere of the place; she had to screw up her courage to tell him where she wanted to go. She was afraid he might refuse to take her in his lift, and she felt that if she were found wandering on the grey marble stairs she would be arrested, silently and without fuss, as an intruder. 

She reached the door she wanted, however, and was admitted by a maid and told to sit down and wait. It was a beautiful room, with only a few pieces of furniture in it, its colouring a delicate mingling of silver-grey and light ash-brown. There were Japanese prints on the walls. A small table covered with a printed cloth was in the centre of the room, and it held an enamelled tray on which were

NEW BED 

several opened letters. Ann imagined her own letter was on top, but she could not be sure, and she did not like to look closely. Everything in the room was so light in tone and delicate in treatment that she felt clumsy and heavy; she was almost afraid that if she moved her flat-heeled brown shoes would disturb the silvery bloom which shone from the thick grey silky carpet and show her up as a hobbledehoy. She sat on a chair and let the beauty and quietness of the room sink in, and so forgot her nervousness. When Mr. Levi came in she was quite self-possessed.

He was disappointing in appearance, but his Semitic face had no grossness and was fastidious, almost tortuously refined. His voice was colourless but compelling in a remote way, for each word he spoke was given its full meaning, so that he did not appear brusque though his words were few. "How old are you? I gathered from your letter you would be older," he said. "I'm really quite responsible. I shall be twenty next year," answered Ann, adding a year and a few weeks to her age in desperation, but realising at the same time how young it sounded in the presence of this man with the ageless face. "What training have you had for a post of this kind?

It is essential you should know a certain amount, not detail, perhaps, but general information about pictures, periods, porcelain. . . . Take this for example." He lifted a green bowl decorated with dragons. "This is a rather rare specimen. Can you tell me- 

Ann looked at it wonderingly. "It's very lovely," she sighed, and then as that answer did not seem to satisfy him, risked it and said: "It's Chinese, isn't it?"

NEW BED 

47 A faint glimmer of a smile passed over the creases in

"To love them all suggests indiscrimination," he answered gravely. Ann tried again. "I know something about pictures-old masters and all that. I often go to the art galleries, and I have a friend, an artist-he carves wood-who lends me books about art." 

Another faint flicker of a smile passed over Mr. Levi's face. "You imagine you are to sell Turners, Rubens, Titians, Botticellis, Correggios?" he asked. "I suppose some minor artists as well," replied Ann with an air of magnanimity. "Most of our work consists of selling the work of minor artists, as you call them."
