"Unfortunately we need someone with more experience older. In the show-room you would be charming, but my clients have no confidence in youth alone."

Ann began to walk to the door. She did not want him to say any more. He had made it quite clear how incongruous and impossible it was for her to do the work. She felt as innocent and as inexperienced in the ways of Bond Street as one of her own tinies. If, magically, he had offered her the post she would have refused it; not out of pique, for beneath her humiliation she was grateful to him for letting her down so lightly, but because she knew she could not have performed the duties. He became more human as he stood by the door, waiting to open it for her.

NEW BED "Your expenses? I always pay applicants' expenses. It is rather unusual asking them to come to my flat instead of to town-rather out of the way." "It is twopence on the bus," said Ann. 

Mr. Levi took fourpence out of his pocket and gave it to her gravely. Ann accepted it in the same grave manner. He walked down the corridor and pressed the button for the lift. When it came he shook hands formally and left her. The handshake in full view of the liftman compensated a little for the disappointment she felt.

Once in the street, the adventure seemed fantasticalmost unreal. She bought two twopenny bars of chocolate with the fourpence and walked home through the back streets, munching them thoughtfully.

The next unusual job Ann tried for was a direct contrast to the first one. The advertisement was enigmatic— "Well-known artist wants a temporary lady helper. Must be refined." With the optimism of her youth Ann set off to call at the address given. She was quite willing to undertake anything, even to filling in the dull bits in his pictures, or making toast for his tea; and the fact that the advertisement said "temporary" did not deter her, for surely if she made herself indispensable he would keep her always, or recommend her to another artist in need of a refined and charming lady helper.

The studio was on the first floor, a long room crammed with imitation Persian rugs, Turkey carpets, brass-ware, statuettes, small tables inlaid with ivory, a few massively framed oil-paintings on easels of polished wood, tapestry panels, pieces of armour, antlered heads and a large wood carving representing the Crucifixion. Leading from it was a smaller room furnished with trestle tables, two wooden chairs, a typewriter, a grocer's calendar on the wall and other signs it was used as a workroom. The velvet curtain

NEW BED 

49 which served as a door between the two rooms was pushed aside.

The artist's name was on the studio door above a bell which said "Push" and a notice which said "Come in." The door was slightly ajar. Ann pushed the bell several times, but received no answer. She knocked vigorously, and still no reply. So after staring boldly at the notice which said "Come in," she pushed the door wide open and walked in. 

An overcoat, a silver knobbed stick, a broad-brimmed black hat, were on a chair, but she could not see the owner. He must have slipped out for a moment, she told herself, and went exploring gingerly amongst the bric-a-brac. Then she noticed the pictures on the easels and went up to them. 

Mr. Levi, she thought, would never have survived a glance at them. The largest one was a monumental work, a picture of the Holy Land, in the most hideous of greens, purples and sandy yellows, showing, so the inscription beneath it read, fifty places of pilgrimage and reverent interest to all readers of the scriptures. Ann studied it carefully, trying to make out the fifty points of interest; she thought the mountain in the background might be Mount 

Olivet. He must have brought back all these sandalwood boxes and carpets and other curios when he went to Jerusalem to paint it, she mused, and suppressed a giggle at the idea of the lady helper dusting the trophies with a feather duster. "I don't really think I could live with that picture, or in this place. Bill wouldn't let me. It's like a studio in a film." She turned to go. As she reached the door a middleaged man wearing a pointed beard and a velvet jacket came up the stairs. "Have you come in answer to my advertisement?" he said. “I just had to pop out a minute. Come in here.” His voice was brisk. Ann followed him through the studio into

the smaller room. The artist wasted no time in preliminaries. He pointed to a stack of small leaflets on which were printed a diagram of the large picture Ann had been studying, and a key to its numbered points. On the table was a pile of mounted coloured reproductions of the picture, balls of string, sheets of cardboard, brown paper, and a large pair of scissors. "I want these packed and sent off as the orders come in. 

One leaflet to each picture. In your spare time leaflets to be sent off to church organisations. I've been round the country lecturing with my picture. I should sell a thousand or so reproductions in the next few weeks. Sunday schools buy 'em. Come and have a look at it."

Ann followed him into the studio, unable to speak. He did not seem to require an answer, which was fortunate, for she was afraid that if she opened her lips she would giggle and disgrace herself. Mr. Carmen Howarth stood rapt before the easel. It was plain the wonders of his picture never palled for him. "Wonderful spot! Fifty places mentioned in the Bible all there! Figures imaginary, but costumes taken from life. And the asses! And the palm trees! And the mountain! Two of the happiest and most solemn weeks of my life I spent in that spot. Wonderful!" "It is," said Ann, and turned her giggle into a cough. 

Mr. Carmen Howarth frowned at her coldly. Had he expected more enthusiasm? Ann did the best she could without perjuring her soul. "It's very large," she ventured. "Had to be to get it all in. Best thing I've ever done." "Yes," agreed Ann meekly.

"Is it about my advertisement?" The girl replied that it was. Before either of them could speak Ann slipped to the door. "Excuse me," she murmured, and ran swiftly down the stairs. She did not breathe freely again until she was well away from the building. She had recurring attacks of laughter during the day. It was not until night that she saw the incident in the more serious light of a failure to find a congenial post.

She would change her plans and see if she would be more successful at finding a commonplace job. She would be a lady clerk. All the tales she had heard and read of smart young lady clerks winning their way into highlypaid and confidential secretaryships, marrying junior partners, and other such romantic endings to very ordinary beginnings came to her mind. The world was large and 

Ann was full of hope.

She went to Prudence with her tale of how she tried for a post as lady clerk. "The man who finally came to me was one of those very self-possessed young men. What I objected to was his curtness. He spoke to me as if he were pressing a

NEW BED button and expected a sharp buzz in reply. He told me to fill in a long form all about myself, which I did. After that he gave me a printed paper with examination questions on it and some sheets of foolscap and said that it was necessary for me to sit for a slight examination—which was not so much to prove the applicant was well educated as to prove her intelligence. He said it had been proved that all the questions could be answered by a normal adult. I was broken in by then and didn't say a word. I just sat down and chewed the end of my pen and felt all my intelligence fading slowly away. After a few moments of staring at the questions they began to look familiar. I almost whooped with joy when I remembered

I had seen them before at school. Miss Farthingdyke had lent the book to the staff to pick out some of the easier problems for the children, and we had amused ourselves at lunch-time asking one another questions out of it. I polished them off with gusto and handed in the paper triumphantly. I believe I caused that young man to look surprised for the first time in his life. In story-books, or in America, after such an exhibition of intelligence the very progressive employer, always on the look-out for talent, would have offered me a junior partnership at least; but all the young man said was that he would let me know officially, yet that in the meantime I could feel pretty certain the job was mine. 53 "And how long does it take before one becomes a senior'? "Well, some of them have been with us anything from ten years upwards,' was his reply. I felt like saying that I should have probably faded away with starvation by then, but I just thanked him and left. And there it ended." 

Prudence looked grave; perhaps she did not approve of Ann's light treatment of the office world. "Fifteen shillings isn't bad for an inexperienced girl.

Charlie Dibbs was not triumphant yet, though Ann's feelings were not so bitter against Charlie since he brought her the young dog. Rover, Charlie Dibbs had called him, but Ann had renamed him Boodle-Dibbs's dog Boodle. 

Charlie Dibbs had been suspicious of the name at first, without understanding why, until Ann, her eyes wide and starry with suppressed laughter, but looking altogether innocent and delighted, had said: "But he is rather Boodleish, the way he bounds about, you know, as if his feet had india-rubber pads on them. 

And then his silly fond ways—rushing up and kissing me all over. Boodle's his name, and I love him." Boodle pranced up at this, carrying his slipper for Ann to play, excited by the laughter in her voice, and the two of them gambolled on the rug until Ann was breathless and had to hide her face to escape the rough wet tongue eager to lick her. "Lot of good he'll be as a watch dog when you've done with him," grumbled Charlie, but his grin was broad and he was plainly enjoying the fun. "Take the dog into the kitchen. You know he's forbidden to come in here," said Mrs. Jaconelli tartly. She

did not like to see Charlie playing with Ann and Boodle.

It made her feel old and stiff. 

Ann returned to school after a fortnight's holiday to find her place taken by an efficient young student teacher who had just left college and was looking about for experience before beginning her career proper. A fortnight of unsuccessful job-hunting had left Ann sadder and wiser. 

The glamour had faded from the unknown world, once fondly cherished in her imagination as a romantic place where anything might happen. Only the knowledge that she would see Bill at the week-end and that, if the worst came to the worst, there would be Boodle at "Ye Old 

Thatched Inn" kept her from sinking into melancholy.

Ann found it impossible to keep up with the enthusiasm and energy of Miss Ball, the new student teacher. She was supposed to be showing Miss Ball her work, but after the first few days Miss Ball was showing Ann how everything should be done according to the latest methods and with the greatest expenditure of energy on the part of the teacher. Miss Ball was cheerful and bright, with unflagging zeal, never ruffled by the naughtiest child or the most uproarious class. She had, apparently, no desires or interest outside the school; she would stay late and come early to coach backward children; she gleefully drew charts and diagrams in her spare time; she would make costumes for the dances and little plays, also in her spare time; she wanted to organize Saturday afternoon entertainments for parents; she started a botany class, visits to the Zoo, and in all these things overflowed with exuberance and was untireable. She was more full of schemes than 

Miss Farthingdyke herself, and several evenings a week, so rumour went, was closeted with the headmistress discussing the betterment of the school. Ann felt that even if she had been able to stay on it was very unlikely Miss

55 

Farthingdyke would keep her now. NEW BED 

"Yes, I do know what you mean. It takes time, I think," said Ann, as enigmatically as Miss Farthingdyke herself.

Ball had mastered her job, Ann could see that Miss Farthingdyke was not going to have it all her own way. She wished she might have been there to see Miss Ball's bright, clear intelligence trying to pin down her principal to a definite statement.

CHAPTER VI 

BILL came to town for the week-end. The greater part of his time he spent with Ann. He stayed to lunch and came back with Ann each evening to supper. Mrs. Jaconelli quite definitely disliked him, while Charlie, when Bill was gone, compared him unfavourably to all the wealthy young men in dashing two-seaters who would come to woo Ann when she lived in "Ye Old Thatched Inn." Mrs. Jaconelli was annoyed because Bill accepted her hospitality without making any return or doing anything to ingratiate himself with her. She wasn't used to men who were mean, she said. Ann argued hotly in Bill's defence and showed the books he had bought her, the wood-carving, the fine collar studded with brass he had bought for Boodle, who showed a tendency to fight with strange dogs. "Did he take you to a show? Did he buy you as much as a box of chocolates? Or even bring in a drink for supper? He'll be no good to you." "You always estimate people by the flashy things they do and the money they waste. Bill doesn't drink, anyway," said Ann, who was ready to cry with vexation at her mother's sordid reckoning. "Your mother's right," boomed Charlie Dibbs. "You want a chap that knows how to spend his brass and who'll take a drink like a man. I've nothing to say for teetotallers." "I know that," replied Ann, in such a cutting tone that 

Charlie winced and rubbed himself ruefully, as if he had been stung. 57

NEW BED

Her mother was angry now, for she felt this dig at 

Charlie touched her as well. "Where's the new job you've been trying for, or have you found it is not so easy for a girl to earn a decent living? You can sneer at me and at Charlie, but we'll have to keep you." "Go it, old dear," said Charlie Dibbs. "If you don't want to keep me, you needn't," retorted 

Ann. "Why didn't you let me have a proper training when Miss Marble asked you, then it wouldn't have been like this at all." Ann broke down into tears as the futility of argument came over her. "I suppose you wanted me to let you grow into a dried-up stick of a school-marm. Where's your sense, Ann?

Here you are with a nice new home, nothing to do but look pretty and make yourself agreeable to the customers who come in, and you are behaving like a spoilt child." 

Mrs. Jaconelli was quite sure of Ann. Her husband had had these little tantrums, but she had always won in the end.

The mention of customers and behaving nicely to them increased Ann's sobs. A glorified barmaid was what she would become, surrounded by people who made fatuous jokes and smelt of drink. All the pent-up disappointments of the last fortnight-her failure to find a post; the weekend with Bill, which had not been so helpful as she hoped; 

Miss Ball's triumphant progress at school-came flooding out. She stumbled to the window and hid her head in the dark curtains, trying to stifle the sound of the great tearing sobs which shook her. 

Charlie Dibbs was remorseful. Her genuine distress touched him. "Now then, Annie, don't take on so. Anybody else would be glad of the chance you've got. It isn't as if your

NEW BED 

59 mother was doing badly for herself marrying me. I'm a soft-hearted chap. Anybody can get round me with a kind word. It's your sarcy remarks I can't stand. Come on now, don't cry. I'll buy you a nice new dress-there's an offer for you-if you'll give us a kiss." "He thinks he can get all his own way by giving things; he thinks he can make people like him, even. What a hopeless idea! Still, it worked with Mother. What a voracious woman she must have been to Father, never happy unless he was giving her things. No wonder he died!" Ann's thoughts ran on down these bitter ways as she struggled with her sobs. "I don't want a new dress. I don't want anything you can give me. You don't know what the things mean that

I like," she said tragically and went slowly out of the room.

Long after she was in bed occasional sobs shook her. 

One particularly violent one woke her from a doze with a start. She could hear a curious snuffling noise coming underneath the door, as of someone smelling hard to find out if she were within. It was Boodle. She remembered now that she had gone to bed so engrossed in her miserable thoughts that she had not played her usual good-night game with him, which consisted chiefly of his pushing his nose into her tightly-closed hand to extract one by one the puppy biscuits she held. Her mother must have left the kitchen door unlatched, and now the house was quiet he had stolen upstairs to find her. Boodle was forbidden in the bedrooms but Ann let him in. He seemed to understand the need for silence, for he padded softly across the floor with the prancing movements Ann loved so much, wagging his tail gently and looking about him with interest. Ann slipped into bed again. "Lie down, Boodle," she whispered, and pushed him down on the floor where

NEW BED 

her hand, trailing out of bed, could pat his curly forehead. "Nice Boodle, go to sleep," she murmured, and turned out the light.

She heard him breathing heavily as he fidgeted about the room, but finally he turned round several times and threw himself down on the rug beside the bed with a violent thump. In the early morning when Ann woke she heard faint snorts and murmurs coming from him and forgot to feel forlorn as she smiled to herself wondering what his doggy dreams could be.

She did not want to go to sleep again so she lay on her back with her hands behind her head, thinking over the week-end with that peculiar clearness of mind which comes in the early hours. She had to confess the much-hoped-for talk with Bill about her affairs had been unsatisfactory. 

He had been horrified at her adventures, and showed himself surprisingly old-fashioned about the things girls should and should not do. He had disapproved of

Prudence's advice about seeking the unusual job, and 

Ann's stories of Mr. Levi and Mr. Carmen Howarth had received in reply a stern lecture on the dangers of going about London and entering strange houses alone. Bill had made her promise not to go after any more jobs for a week or two. He said he hated to think of her going round to offices and shops. She was much too young and pretty and innocent. According to Bill disaster in the form of unscrupulous men and women waited to pounce upon her unless she was very careful. Ann complained that the people she had seen were entirely the opposite, and had been only too indifferent; whereupon Bill had answered coldly that she was talking like a child and was no more capable of earning her own living than Boodle. 

On the other hand he agreed that she could not go and live with her mother and Charlie Dibbs at "Ye Old

NEW BED 

61 

Thatched Inn." Tea-gardens, he pointed out, existed only for a very short period of the year; what would she have to do the rest of the time? Ann knew his fears were her own. He had been grave for the remainder of his visit, speaking so little that she was frightened lest she had offended him, and her natural gaiety and high spirits, which had never entirely forsaken her before in spite of her anxiety, deserted her. His heavy face, unlightened by geniality, was forbidding.

She went to the station to see him off and it was there he made her promise not to do anything further until she heard from him. The air of brooding he had worn since she had told him her story lifted a little and he looked less like a stern father and more of the dear friend and adviser she wished him to be. "Have you found a nymph for the other figure?" she asked. "You would do. All the other girls I meet are too sophisticated or else silly, not only their faces but their limbs. It's this wretched modern dancing." "But I'm not a nymph," laughed Ann. "You should see me trying bare-foot dancing with the children and coming down like a load of bricks after each jump through the air." "A nymph doesn't know she's a nymph-not the kind 

I mean. A self-conscious one would be terrible," replied Bill.

These few moments before the train went were the only happy moments of conversation they had, decided Ann.

She found school a burden the next few days. She could not share her home affairs with the people there; the story was too humiliating to be told in detail. She knew the code of what one could and could not do was rigidly

NEW BED 

fixed. Genteel poverty was allowed and understood, but to keep a public-house, even when it was called "Ye Old Thatched Inn," would not be tolerated. 

The fact that she was going to leave them at the end of the term was already making her of less importance. 

She seemed to be slipping out of the intimate circle which united the members of the staff and which made of absorbing interest the tiniest happenings in their daily work. 

A small incident helped her to discover this fact and added to the sense of isolation. Ann was talking to the visiting gym mistress, who had just finished her lesson. 

All the children had changed their shoes and were waiting by the door, except two little girls who were chasing each other round the room with wild shrieks, while Mademoiselle implored them to stop and come with her for their French lesson. She held their shoes in one hand and kept trying to stop them as they passed her, entreating as she did so: "Now, Joan darling; Barbara, my dear, you really must come. The others are waiting; Joan, darling, listen to me. . . ." She caught Joan as she dashed past but the child shook her off rudely. "I hate French. Miss Farthingdyke told Mamma I should not be forced to learn anything I did not like." "But darling, you really must. .." Mademoiselle followed Joan with suppliant outstretched hands. "What that child wants is a good spanking," said the gym mistress feelingly. "I'd make her want to learn."

A month ago Ann would have been horrified at the barbarity of the suggestion and would have talked sternly about the dangers of suppressing the child's natural instincts; but now she smiled in agreement and discovered she was looking at the school from the outside instead of from within.

She had received only a short note from Bill since he

NEW BED 

63 left, saying he was busy but that on Sunday he would write her a long letter. On Sunday evening, finding the flat desolate in its half-denuded state, she took Boodle and went to call on Prudence.

Prudence, always a confident, self-possessed young person, looked particularly so when she took Ann's arm for a stroll round the garden and a confidential chat. She fluttered her fingers about continuously, but Ann was dullwitted that evening and instead of congratulating Prudence on the diamond engagement ring she wore, only wondered why her friend was so fidgety. Prudence was obliged to point it out to her. Ann praised it as enthusiastically as Prudence could have wished, and they continued their stroll with their arms about each other as they were used to do at school. "But Pru, have you forgotten? We were to get engaged together." 

Prudence laughed. "Ann, you baby! Why, we were only kids playing. This is serious. Of course, I'm not going to get married for years. I'm going to make George save up and buy a car. I'm only going to let him see me three times a week, Saturday, Sunday and Wednesday; on 

Tuesday and Thursday I'm going to take cookery and housewifery classes at the Tech." "That only leaves you Friday for yourself," suggested 

Ann, not liking to add "and for me" because Prudence had suddenly become inaccessible in a superior, grown-up way. "I have to get my hair done on Friday, wash my stockings and jobs like that. Of course I'll be busy. Still, it's worth it. It's all very well idling when one hasn't any responsibilities." "Yes, I suppose so," agreed Ann, and added involuntarily, "Never mind, I've got Boodle."

NEW BED 

Prudence flushed. "George will be very glad to meet you. I told him he's got to know all my friends and treat them as his own. You must come round on his evenings. 

Come and be introduced now. I left him talking to mother."

Ann demurred. She could not imagine that George would be glad to see her on the evenings Prudence put aside for him. "I'll come another day," she said. "I do think it's a perfectly lovely ring." "You'll have to be a bridesmaid in rose-pink chiffon. 

I'm going to have round posies of moss-roses and forgetme-nots," consoled Prudence.

Ann walked home solemnly with Boodle on his lead, trying to remember a mournful poem she had once read which began: "Change, change everywhere." She tried to finish it herself but gave it up. She had worked herself into an exquisite state of melancholy by the time she arrived home. 

Her mother had not returned. Ann went into the larder and cut herself a large piece of cake and two slices of bread and butter spread liberally with honey, for melancholy, strangely enough, had given her an appetite. She shared her supper with Boodle. Boodle had a sweet tooth; he managed to make his whiskers drop sweetness on quite a small allowance of bread and honey. Ann's melancholy faded at the sight. She left him on the hearthrug licking himself with noisy enjoyment and went to bed, not to weep as she had intended over her lonely lot, but to sleep dreamlessly until the noise of the postman next morning woke her and she ran downstairs in her dressing gown to fetch Bill's letter. 

Mrs. Jaconelli was very pleased with her breakfast some two hours later. Ann had taken special pains over

NEW BED 

65 it; the tea was hot and strong, the toast browned to perfection and richly buttered, and Ann, as she presented it, was a sight to restore a jaded appetite, so fresh and pretty she looked, as if a secret pleasure bubbled up within her, making her cheeks bloom and her eyes shine. 

Mrs. Jaconelli was surprised and rather touched and told herself that Ann had made up her mind to sulk no more. She had not taken Ann's unhappiness seriously, but it had made her uncomfortable, and Mrs. Jaconelli detested being uncomfortable with all the strength of her pleasure-loving nature. As usual she expressed her appreciation in a practical manner. "Would you like my flowered voile? It would make you a pretty dress if we altered it. Bring it from the wardrobe. That parchment shade suits you. I imagine it makes me look sallow at times."

Ann brought out the dress and, holding it against herself, pranced up and down the room. "I shall look like a nymph," she murmured. "Keep it long and full in the skirt and rather highwaisted. . . . Can I alter it to-night, Mum? I'd like it for next week-end very, very specially.” "And what's happening next week-end?" condescended her mother absently as she sipped her tea. When she sat in bed she could see her head and shoulders in the wardrobe mirror and she was studying herself now, wondering if Charlie Dibbs would give her enough money to buy new lingerie of pink crêpe de Chine or whether it would have to be muslin. It was time she bought the material and cut them out. "It's a secret till then." Ann blushed, then her colour faded leaving her dark eyes abnormally large and bright in a white tense face. "That girl is a bundle of nerves. Some silly school-

NEW BED 

party, I suppose," thought her mother, and said placidly: "We'll have a sewing night to-night. Now be off or you'll be late," and resumed her preoccupation with the problem of pink crêpe de Chine or mauve muslin and lace for her trousseau.

Ann walked to school with her hands in her coat pockets, because Bill's letter was in one of them and she liked to feel the paper in her fingers. The extent of her relief and glowing gratitude to Bill showed her how deep had been her horror of being forced to live at "Ye Old Thatched 

Inn." Bill was going to save her. He was going to take her away and give her a home of her own, with a garden, an orchard, even a little stream at the bottom of the orchard where ducks might live. He would teach her about art and wood-carving, and she could practise her music whenever she liked, and perhaps there would be friends coming to see them who knew all about these things and she would entertain them and be very charming and clever, so that Bill would be glad he had married her.

As exhilaration flooded her mind with happy images of a new life, she sped along rapidly, only to change into slower step as she dwelt affectionately upon Bill's gravity and strength, his solidity contrasting well with her mother's gimcrack world of good sorts and good times; once she stopped altogether in sudden consternation as she asked herself: would her mother give her consent or would she be obliged to run away?

The children found her bewilderingly inconsistent that day. One moment she would be sharp-eyed, seeing the mischief they contemplated before it was begun, the next moment her abstraction was so profound that they might have been without a mistress in the room at all. At lunch she knew she had never been so brilliant; she was keyed

NEW BED 

67 up to such a pitch of excitement that her sensibilities were acute almost to painfulness. Miss Ball seemed quite colourless beside her, and upon her contradicting Ann on some minor point in the discussion Miss Farthingdyke intervened and supported Ann firmly. There was a time when such an occurrence would have gone to Ann's head, but now she regarded it only as an aid to living through the days until Saturday, when Bill was coming to make arrangements for their marriage. Suppose she told them, now Miss Farthingdyke had left them and they sat round the staff-room table sipping coffee and gossiping idly in sluggish after-lunch mood, that she was going to be married? She was almost tempted to make the experiment. 

First she must answer Bill's letter, and thank him and tell him how happy he had made her. She knew there would be no opportunity all the afternoon, and in the evening there was the sewing to be done; also she dreaded her mother's sharp eyes and sharper intuition if she wrote her letter at home. She took paper and pen and, as a screen, a pile of homework books into a desk in the corner of the room. "Look at Jacky being industrious," drawled Miss Maidstone, who gave the ten-year-olds their first Latin lessons and considered herself entitled to be sarcastic to the other mistresses. "I'm not moving till the bell rings. I believe in starting my afternoon classes fresh," commented Miss Tring, who was clever at handwork but inclined to indolence, which she imagined she concealed by giving elaborate and entirely unconvincing reasons for it. "Humbug!" said Ann gaily. The words came out before she could stop them. It was unpardonable impudence for a junior to address a senior with such freedom. There

NEW BED was a surprised silence, a smothered giggle or two, a tiny squeal of delight from Miss Ball who found Miss Tring's sluggishness irritating. "I have noticed a difference in tone, in morale-in-er, well, you know what I mean, Miss Maidstone, in this staff-room of late. When some young women I could mention have had my experience" Miss Tring rambled on in a high-pitched voice, with Miss Maidstone nodding agreement at frequent intervals. It was lost on Ann, who was writing to Bill to tell him she would love to be his nymph and live in his house, and that she would always try to make him happy. It was not lost on Miss Ball, for she was pondering deeply what kind of complex or repression Miss Tring could be described as suffering from, and trying to fix the complaining words in her mind so that she could discuss the matter with a lecturer of psychology who was a friend of hers.

CHAPTER VII 

STRANGE to say, it was Charlie Dibbs who was the most surprised and hurt by Ann's news. He had been reckoning on displaying Ann as a pretty daughter amongst his customers and acquaintances, and he had hoped to gain her affection that he might have taken a father's liberties with her, pinched her round soft cheeks, embraced her, and, in return for presents and privileges, had arms flung round him and heard soft cajoling words in his ear. Ever since they first met Ann had been on her dignity-an absurd childish dignity which was so pathetically frail that he would never let himself believe how it upset him. 

Charlie Dibbs had always stretched out his hand and taken whatever he wanted, and if it proved good business more often than not, that was because he considered it just as well to assist Providence and not depend on good luck alone. He had wanted Mrs. Jaconelli and he had won her, and it was likely to prove good business, for if she was physically indolent she had a sharp eye for comfort and would not let her maids scamp their work. She understood good food, and the virtues of linen, silver and glass in setting it off so that the pleasure of eating was enhanced. Apparelled in her best, she was magnificent to look upon. She was pleasant-tempered and a good companion where there was no shortage of money. There were still people who remembered her singing and her name was an attraction in a hotel, where personality and past celebrity always attracted custom. He had stretched out his hand for her and she had fallen into it like a ripe plum from a twig. Ann had been different. Apparently possess- 69

NEW BED ing nothing of value except her youth and its tender unconscious beauty, which was more likely to be a drawback to her than an aid in the world as he knew it, she had beaten him. With no weapon to withstand the strength of his position she had won. There was something poignant in the sense of disappointment he felt when he knew he had lost her. He had never met a girl like Ann before. 

His opinion of her sex was deplorable, for his experience was wide amongst the types of women who frequent public-houses. He had known them as drabs or hussies, or fine bold women like Mrs. Jaconelli, who knew what they wanted; never as gentle, charming creatures, fine-spirited, with a refinement which was more than a veneer and an innocence calm and unspotted.

Mrs. Jaconelli was not unrelieved that the problem of her daughter should have solved itself in this way. It would be much easier for her to begin a new life without Ann. For one thing, she always tried to keep up before her daughter the appearance of refinement and sensibility which had been hers before she fell upon bad times. Ann was out all day, Mrs. Jaconelli was mostly out in the evenings, so that Ann saw very little of her mother. Ann did not know, for instance, that her mother frequented hotel lounges and was quite at home in them. 

Mrs. Jaconelli considered Bill Darwin quite a good match, under the circumstances. He had shown respect for the formalities of the occasion and had answered Mrs. 

Jaconelli's questions about his affairs without hesitation. "But if your mother lives in your house? You can't expect Ann to be happy in her married life if she has to share you with your mother and three sisters." "My sisters have wanted to move into the town for a long while, but I would not go and my mother would not leave me alone," answered Bill. "Mother will be de-

NEW BED 

71 lighted to go. As a matter of fact they are inspecting houses this very week-end. My sisters find the country dull, and it is a long train journey from their work and their friends. 

They regard Ann as their saviour. My father left everything in my hands. He was old-fashioned and did not believe in letting women handle money. My mother and sisters want to please me." "Naturally," agreed Mrs. Jaconelli drily. There was something dour and weighty about Bill when he was after something he wanted which made her also anxious to please him. She wondered suddenly if he would prove a tyrant to Ann, who was soft and yielding, like her father, unless some mysterious spot of pride or loyalty was touched. In her late husband it had been his music; plague him as she would, she could never get him to alter a note of it, though he had given in to her on every other matter. "Ann is very young. I have kept her young for her age, and you are sixteen years older and fond of your own way, I fancy. You would have to be very careful she is sensitive." She wanted to do her best for Ann. She herself had revelled in marriage and love. She liked men, had always liked them. The poor child didn't know what she was in for-probably a baby every year . . . well, other women bore it. . . . Ann would have to get used to it. 

She'd know in a very short while that married life wasn't all kisses and reading books together. She found it so hard to talk to Ann, to warn her, to tell her things. If only she would be sensible and not wrap herself up in high-falutin' ideas.. 

Mrs. Jaconelli looked up from her thoughts to find Bill blushing red and looking uncomfortable almost as if he knew of what she had been thinking.

NEW BED "Ann will be all right with me. We understand one another," he said coldly. "I still think Ann too young to get married," she was saying when Ann came in with the tea-tray. Ann put the tray on the table and turned her back on them to get the cake from the sideboard. Her voice came muffled but quite unconcerned from the depths of the cupboard. "Don't you worry, Mum. Bill and I aren't really getting married; not how you mean, anyway. We like each other for different reasons." "That's right," agreed Bill, flinching a little under Mrs. 

Jaconelli's sharp scrutiny. "Can we have the last pot of apple jelly?” said Ann, turning round and holding the jam jar towards her mother coaxingly. "If you like," answered her mother, not at all interested in apple jelly. "I'll go and tell Charlie tea is ready."

As soon as she was gone Ann came swiftly from the table and stood in front of Bill. "It will be like that, won't it? You promised, didn't you? Mother keeps getting me in corners and making me uncomfortable. I think she wants to tell me things about the mysteries of marriage. I couldn't really hear it from her, somehow. It doesn't concern us, does it? You are going to take me to Cheet because you love me for myself, my soul, I mean, and because you need a companion and because there are so many things we love doing together. 

I am going to be a great help to you?"

Bill took her hands and pulled her to him. "Of course. But you do love me, don't you?" 

Ann blushed and smiled. "I think you are the nicest person I've ever met. No one else has ever cared what became of me but you. I wish, though, I could come to 

Cheet and live with you without being married. I don't

NEW BED 

73 mean because I should ever want not to live with you, but because I feel we are being deceitful. You see, we don't mean by marriage what the rest of the world seems to mean." 

Bill nodded gravely. "Your mother would never allow it. You don't understand, my dear. It wouldn't do at all.

People wouldn't be nice to you. It's the world, and convention and the laws of the land. We are not strong enough to go against them, even if I wanted to do so." 

Ann gave him a sudden brilliant smile. Her face was illuminated with radiance; her whole being glowed with the intensity of her feelings and her voice went rich and deep and brought tears to Bill's eyes. "Whatever you say is right. I trust you absolutely.

Didn't the damsel in the old stories trust the knight who sallied forth to rescue her?" 

Charlie Dibbs came in at that moment. He was not quite wakened from his afternoon sleep and looked sulky. 

He sat down heavily in an arm-chair. Ann took him a cup of tea. She was not afraid of him now, and her fear gone, she saw their relationship in a more kindly light.

She wanted to be nice to him to please her mother, and for another reason she was keeping tightly inside herself until the right moment came. She smiled as she put three lumps of sugar in his cup. "Cake, or apple jelly?" she said with an air of wanting to make him comfortable. 

Charlie Dibbs waved the food away and drank the tea in a noisy gulp. He put the cup and saucer on the mantelpiece and wiped his moustache with the back of his hand. "Come here, Annie, I want to ask you something," he said, and winked at Bill.

Ann stood in front of him and he put one great hand round her waist and drew her between his knees, the

NEW BED other fondling her limbs as if she were a young child whose tender body he loved. Bill scowled alarmingly, but 

Ann, though she felt uncomfortable, did not move away, but took the straying hand and held it tightly in her own. 

His caresses did not offend her. There was no lust or evil desire in him; only a kind of wonder at the delicacy of her girlhood and a regret that it could never belong to him. "What do you want to ask me?" she said, still smiling at him in simple friendliness and locking her fingers in and out of his great hairy ones. 

Charlie wanted to delay the moment. "What do you think?" "I don't know. Is it something nice?" "What would you like me to give you for a wedding present? Here's a chance for you. Whatever you like as long as you don't break the bank, eh, Ada?” "You promise?" "Well, give us a kiss first." 

Ann kissed him hastily on his whiskers, shrinking from the large wet lips which reached out for hers. "I want Boodle. To take him with me," she said solemnly. 

Charlie Dibbs let out a great laugh. "If you aren't a cough-drop! I expected a pianner at least." "I don't want anything else," said Ann firmly.

Her mother followed her into the kitchen when Ann took the tea-tray away. She looked angry, but Ann did not care. She had been afraid Charlie would refuse her the dog. "And where do you think your trousseau's coming from? I suppose you are going away with hardly a rag to your back. Dog, indeed! You ought to have more sense. 

Why, he would have given you anything! What do you

NEW BED 

75 think Bill's mother and sisters will say when they see your things?"

Ann's high spirits evaporated, but it did not change her determination to take nothing from Charlie Dibbs, particularly as she felt more kindly towards him now. The thought that Bill's mother and sisters might despise her meagre belongings was depressing, nevertheless. "Perhaps they don't worry about things like that. They won't if they are like Bill," she suggested. Her mother sniffed in reply. 

Tears came into Ann's eyes, and her mother, seeing them, reconciled herself once more to her daughter's lack of business sense. "I'll see what I can do," she said kindly. "I shall have a lot of new things. It shouldn't be hard to squeeze in a few for you."

When they were alone later in the evening Bill said: "I shouldn't let that old beer-guzzler buy you presents. 

I can't bear him to put his hands on you. It's profane." 

Bill was frowning. His thick dark eyebrows met and made his face sombre and forbidding. Ann felt a shadow of apprehension pass over her. She flushed and reached out for his hand. She could not bear that he should look at her with disapproval. "No of course not! I asked for Boodle because I love him. He wouldn't be happy at 'Ye Old Thatched Inn.' 

Mother dislikes him. She'd make him into a yard dog and he'd grow savage and brutal." 

Bill's face cleared quickly. He took her hand and kissed it. He could feel how swiftly she reacted to his frown, and he wanted to make her happy again. "He'll have a lovely time with us. You'll be glad of him during the day when I'm in town, because it can be lonely at Cheet. He shall be your guard when I cannot

NEW BED 

be with you. We must train him not to chase chickens or sheep."

Ann laughed, her eyes warm with delight, her cheeks rosy with anticipated pleasure. She could imagine herself and Boodle chasing one another down grassy slopes and along green lanes in a frolicking ecstasy. Bill, watching her, was filled with a wild aching passionate love for her which was never to leave him. She had so little understanding of it, giving him bounteously of her affection, but even as she caressed him she was thinking of Boodle, living a girlish life of her own he could not share. He felt he could never bear to let her out of his sight any more, lest harm should come to her and she should be lost to him. The snares and pitfalls that lay in wait for innocence assumed monstrous size in his over-sensitised mind. It seemed to him that in spite of her tall springing figure and long slim legs she was small and fragile, to be enfolded and kept inviolate from the brutal world. He did not think many people were aware of her prettiness, because she herself was not yet conscious of it and it was not displayed. There was still a good deal of the tom-boy in her, mixed oddly with the earnest student and docile child. 

When she thought of herself it was not of the effect of her looks on people, but the effect of her mind, or her love, or her friendliness. She still thought ideas meant more than anything else. Perhaps it was this which gave her pretty face its air of cool remoteness, as of a flower growing in a wood which had never seen the sun. She was his wood-nymph come to life, the nymph he had always been seeking and had never dared hope he would find.

CHAPTER VIII 

ANN was married at ten o'clock on a fine August morning. 

Her mother had been married the previous day, but "Ye

Old Thatched Inn" not being ready to receive the new 

Mrs. Dibbs, Ann was married from her old home, as she desired.

Ann had announced it was to be a quiet wedding-no finery, no carriages, no confetti. They were walking to church, which was only a short distance away, and afterwards there was wedding-cake and wine for any of her friends who cared to call in for a few moments. Bill and she were catching a train at noon, and almost on their heels. the removal men would be pulling up carpets, packing china, taking away from that neighbourhood for ever all traces of the late Mrs. Jaconelli and her daughter. Mr. and Mrs. Dibbs were going on a honeymoon to Ostend. 

Ann and Bill were going straight home; they were not having a honeymoon. 

Charlotte, Miss Farthingdyke's old cook, had made 

Ann a wedding-cake, revealing an affection for Ann and an unexpected talent for confectionery she had kept concealed for years. There was to be in the church a gathering of all the pupils of the school who were not away on holiday, the tiniest, whom Ann loved most, accompanied by their nursemaids. Miss Ball had arranged this, and with an instinct for pageant and display on the school's behalf had told the children to carry bunches of marigolds, the flower the school had taken for its symbol and wore embroidered on its badges and blazers. 77

NEW BED

It was a brilliant summer morning when Ann, her mother and Prudence set forth. Bill and Charlie Dibbs had gone on before. Bill's mother and sisters did not come for the wedding; they preferred to greet the bride in their own home, they said; and Ann was glad, because she wanted to surround herself at this last hour with her own girlhood friends. She saw this act of marriage and going away symbolically; an act of parting from the things and places and people of her youth. What chance was there that she would ever return to this place, once her mother went to live at "Ye Old Thatched Inn"?

There was a flutter of summer frocks-her mother in pale grey chiffon, Prudence in rose, Phil and Amy, Prudence's young sisters, in flowery voiles, Miss Ball taking the occasion seriously in blue silk. Ann herself in the pale-green coat and frock she had worn for her mother's wedding, a sheaf of red roses the children had given her on her arm, and a strange excitement, half-joyful, half-sad, flushing and paling her cheeks. All about her was the nervous twittering of girlish voices, mingling with shrill cries from the children as they surged round holding their posies in tight, hot hands—and then as they came from the hot sunshine and glaring pavements into the cool church the hushed whispering of the verger as he showed them their places was the only sound. The smell of marigolds made itself felt, and its rankness caught in Ann's throat with a sharpness that came back each summer afterwards whenever the same scent came to her. "Nothing will ever be the same again," she told herself. 

Bill was a stranger in his new suit and stiff, upright bearing; his voice as he answered the responses was as formal as his figure. Her mother, now Mrs. Dibbs, was a stranger also, although she surreptitiously picked pieces

NEW BED 

79 of cotton from Ann's coat, occupied as usual with appearances. Prudence, holding Ann's red roses and gloves, was cool and self-possessed, engrossed in the pleasure the prominent position gave her, and thinking, no doubt, of George and the day when she would be a bride herself. 

Prudence had said earlier in the day, "You look sweet in green; but isn't it unlucky?" And she had answered "What rot! I always wanted a green suit like this." Suppose it was unlucky, after all? The droning voice of the clergyman dropped, then gathered strength and swelled again like the humming of a bee going backwards and forwards.

She could not concentrate on the words he spoke, for her mind was moving too quickly, and holding nothing. She felt utterly alone. She was going out into an unfamiliar place, as a new person, with a new name, even. Panic came over her, and a wild rush of fear. Suppose she ran away now before it was too late! She lifted two frightened eyes to the clergyman reading from his book and he took her hand impersonally and without expression on his solemn face-it was the moment when the ring must be put on her finger. Panic fled. Bill smiled at her; her mother gave a tiny cough of nervousness. Soon it was all over, and Bill and she were walking back to the house together arm in arm. The others fell back as they passed and let them walk alone, as if by the act they had just performed they had become isolated. Ann could hear chattering and laughing behind her. "Are you cold?" asked Bill, for she was trembling. "Never mind, it is all over. We'll soon be in the train." 

Phyllis and Ann came flying past them, their thin, blackstockinged legs lanky and awkward as the legs of young foals. They stood one each side of the gate, holding handfuls of paper rose petals ready to throw, laughing as they

NEW BED jigged up and down excitedly. They pelted everyone indiscriminately, shaking the empty boxes over themselves at last to catch the last few fluttering wafers.

The strangeness passed now the solemnity of the church was left behind. There was no air of a nuptial feast about the cakes and wine and lemonade. Charlie Dibbs was too embarrassed by the cool, almost disdainful glances of the young girls and their excited high-pitched chatter in a language he barely understood, to make the jokes on marriage for which he was celebrated amongst his own friends. 

He sat there in silence like an old black hulk surrounded by fluttering gaily-coloured flowers which took no heed of him, except perhaps to use him as a foil for their own buoyant freshness. His wife was out of place also amongst these gay young creatures. She had become possessed in this last hour or so of a fierce maternal tenderness. She was releasing some of it on Bill in the form of complaints because she could not get at Ann, who was surrounded by girls, showing them her presents and distributing pictures of herself and Boodle and pieces of cake in silver boxes. "I'm surprised your mother and sisters didn't come," she said for the tenth time at least that day.

Bill patiently went through their excuses-the journey, the expense, the desire to welcome Ann in their own home. "I should like to have met your mother. You are taking Ann so far away." Her eyes stared painfully. She looked over-dressed and over-powdered amongst the sleekheaded girls in their sleeveless summer frocks. "Ann will be all right. I shall take care of her," Bill answered gravely.

There was bustle and commotion, Prudence to be kissed again, the children to be hugged, Boodle to be pushed into the car, for he was terrified at the noise and shrank

NEW BED 

81 under the table and had to be dragged out, her roses to wrap up, for she could not bear to leave them amongst the débris for the removal men to throw aside. Her mother was crying now, and Mrs. Pollard was quite openly comforting her, with Miss Farthingdyke looking on as if she would have liked to do the same, but could think of nothing but the maxims on courage she taught the children. 

Ann hesitated; she wanted to stay until her mother recovered, but Charlie Dibbs pushed her into the car. "They like a good cry. It does 'em good," he said. "I'll be back in half a jiff, and then we'll be off to catch our boat. That'll cheer her up."

With a jerk of the car they were off. Boodle put his paws on Ann's lap and looked out at the waving group on the pavement. Ann looked with him, waving her hand frantically and shouting out last messages they could not hear. In front Charlie Dibbs was making polite conversation with Bill. "Ever been to Ostend? Rummy place. A chap can have a bit of fun there if you know the ropes. I can give you a few tips if you ever want to go.

He did not like Bill, but there was a fellow-feeling between them that day.

The country was calm and bright under early evening sunlight when Bill and Ann and Boodle got out of the train some five hours later and walked along the road from Cheet station to Cheet Barn. Bill had sent the taxi ahead with their luggage. The strange sensations of railway travel had subdued Boodle into a docile young dog who padded along close to his mistress, though occasionally his ears lifted and his customary prance returned as new and fascinating smells came to him from the hedgerows. Ann imagined she felt not unlike Boodle. The long journey coming after the emotion of the morning had wearied her and her keen interest in her new home and its surround-

NEW BED ings were temporarily overlaid by a heaviness of spirit she could not fight against. It took the edge from her intense apprehension of Bill's mother and sisters, for her mother had managed to convey the impression, without actually saying so, that Ann would find them her enemies.

The landscape around her exposed by the searching rays of the evening sun was pleasantly rural, but undistinguished. The greenery was dusty with the heat of summer and had lost its freshness, but to Ann it was like catching up with spring again after the yellowing plane trees of home. Except in one direction, where a plantation of fir trees topped a rising bank and made a definite skyline pleasant for the eye to look upon, the country was flat, consisting of small square fields sown with cabbages and potatoes, a few skimpy trees in the hedgerows and clusters of red-brick cottages and tin-roofed farm buildings. As they turned into a lane from the road Ann heard the sound of running water. The ground on one side of the lane fell away abruptly, showing between the slender silvery trunks of birch trees in a little spinney a shining brook flowing noisily over stones. There were foxgloves and ferns on the sloping bank, and rosettes of primrose leaves. A robin sang quietly, and as Ann stopped and looked over the hedge a blackbird went clucking loudly through the trees and sang half a stave in fright. "How pretty!" said Ann. "Can we go in there? I love those tall flowers. We found some once on a school-picnic, and the tinies picked the blossoms off and wore them on their fingers going home. When Miss Farthingdyke remonstrated with them one child said serenely: "They are fairy gloves. You wish with them on.""

Bill gave a smile to her story of the foxgloves and dismissed it, occupied with his own thoughts. It seemed incredible to Ann that only a few hours had passed since

NEW BED 83 she left home. Her mother was probably on the water, their old home dismantled and desolate and Miss Farthingdyke's school wrapped in the dusty silence of vacation time. "The spinney is on our land," Bill said. "The brook winds round and skirts the orchard a few yards further on. I used to dream about the spinney when I was in

France; usually when I was almost at the end of my endurance. I used to dream that I was creeping very quietly to the brookside, hoping desperately I might see a wood-nymph between the trees-I think it came from a book of Corot reproductions, which was my greatest treasure when I was young." "And did you see one?" "No. I always woke just as I knew I must catch a glimpse of white limbs at last. It has been a hunger in me. It is still. I have always wanted to carve a nymph. 

I've tried dozens of times and never been satisfied. The models I've seen have always been too old or too coarse -too much of the world-not their features so much as their bodies. There has been no buoyancy in them. I've not found it easy to get models. I couldn't bring them to the house because of my mother and sisters. They think the human body is a sinful thing."

Ann felt that there was purpose behind his words which concerned her. The unsatisfied desire she could sympathise with, for she herself had known many. She hadn't imagined Bill as having tormenting longings; he seemed so self-contained and strong. They stood looking over the hedge into the spinney, each striving to reach the other's mind and realising how little of it they really understood. 

Boodle, after a tug or two at his lead, sat on his haunches and surveyed the landscape, changed momentarily by his resignation into an old and wise dog.

NEW BED "Nymphs don't wear clothes, do they?" she asked at last.

Bill put his arm round her shoulder and pressed it gently. "I wouldn't hurt you, Ann. I swear it. If you could it would be in reverent adoration I would look at you. You know me well enough to believe that? If you'd be my model . . . It's nothing to be ashamed of. It is only convention's foul mind which sees evil in beauty. . . . I am an artist." "I know. I wouldn't be ashamed," said Ann shakily. 

Bill's mood changed. He pressed her to him jubilantly. "Come along, sweetheart. They'll be waiting for us. Just bear with them a little while, then they'll be gone and we shall have the place to ourselves. I've got a holiday and can take you exploring, so we need not spend much time indoors."

The front of Cheet Barn stood firm and square behind the tall holly hedge and the two laburnum trees drooping their fringe of seed pods over the gate, its brass knocker shining, its whitened steps undefiled by footprints. The front was like a house a painstaking child had drawn, a square brick box with a door exactly in the centre and four windows, two at each side, exactly above one another, their white lace curtains looped back with precision. It was at once intimidating in its respectability and naïve in its craving after rigidity. The back of the house was much older and built of smaller bricks whose rosy colour looked, when the evening rays fell upon it, as if it had absorbed the sunshine of years. It was revealed like this now as Bill led Ann round the neat gravel path to a side door which stood wide open, showing a long, stone-flagged passage with hats and coats hanging on pegs on the wall and sticks and umbrellas in a painted drain-pipe.

NEW BED 85 "We don't use the front door," explained Bill. “As a matter of fact the front part of the house is ghastly. The rooms are full of junk. It was added on to please my grandmother, who was a town woman with a mania for bamboo furniture and knick-knacks." 

Ann clutched Boodle by the collar for support, overcome suddenly by the strangeness of this new life and by her appalling ignorance. What did she know of Bill, of his people, of his house? What things must she learn before she became one of them, if ever she did? The thought passed almost before she had taken hold of it. Bill was pointing out the orchard beyond the garden. Red apples and green hung in tight clusters on grey twisted boughs; there were dark plums with a bloom on them that the sunlight lingered over, and brown and yellow pears. The grass under the trees was lush and green, and three white ducks quacked softly in intimate conversation with one another as they thrust their necks forward and guzzled in the grass. White phlox, snowy as the ducks' plumage, and marigolds as yellow as their beaks were in the flower beds. 

Through the trees could be seen an ancient black and white barn, whose stout beams curved fantastically with the weight of years, the natural lines of the timber and the homely conceit of the craftsmen who had built it. "That is why the place is called Cheet Barn," said Bill. "The barn is my workroom. It is the oldest building in the neighbourhood." "Oh! I can't believe I am going to live here,” cried

Ann, all the rapture of a town child for the wonders of the country sparkling in her eyes. Five years at least slipped from her at that moment. That she was a married woman seemed as preposterous as the prim genteel front of the house appeared when one knew of the charm which lay behind it. Bill stood beside her almost grim in his

NEW BED stolidity, but inwardly moved by her delight. Cheet Barn was very dear to him. It was his refuge from a modern world which he detested. He had always felt that the puritanical fervours of his chapel-loving mother and sisters had struck a false note in the place, and he was glad that they would soon be leaving him. He had found a nymph to share his pastoral: his dream was fulfilled and he wanted nothing more. "This is an enchanted spot. Now you have entered it 

I shall hold you fast," he said, attempting to make the words playful, but not succeeding.

Ann was still watching the ducks, with one hand on Boodle, who showed signs of wanting to investigate these strange talkative creatures. She felt his tone jar without knowing why, except that it seemed too portentous for the occasion; but before she could reply a door opened along the passage and she became involved in the embarrassments of meeting Bill's mother and sisters.

CHAPTER IX 

IT DID not take Ann long to discover that Bill was undisputed master at Cheet Barn. Ann had never lived in a house under masculine rule. Her life had been spent with women—her mother, Miss Marble, Miss Farthingdyke, feminists all in their different ways; she had had very little experience of the squaw type of woman. 

Bill's mother and sisters gave the impression that they lived entirely for and because of Bill. They had their own life apart from him, it was true, but they indulged it secretly, almost guiltily, whispering in corners together and scuttling apart when Bill entered the room. 

Maude, the eldest sister, had a sharp tongue, a bustling manner, and a hard, bright voice. She seemed to be always saying to herself: "It is my duty to be cheerful,” and it was noticeable that the more resolutely cheerful she became the more depressed were those around her. Ann was not at all surprised to discover over the looking-glass in Maude's bedroom a large, harshly-coloured text, "Be 

Bright," and another over her bed "Be Thankful for your

Blessings. The world is full of those whose troubles are worse than your own." Maude grumbled occasionally at 

Bill's autocracy when he was absent, but she made up for her mutinous thoughts by a fiercer and more persistent devotion to him when he was present, a form of selfchastisement so unctuously undertaken that Ann recoiled from her with dislike.

Pansy, who was two years younger than Maude, was a pale reflection of her sister. Her voice was softer and her 87

NEW BED 

smile more natural, and her laugh could ring out into a foolish giggle not unpleasant to hear at Cheet Barn, where laughter was rare; and her perpetual gratitude for the mercies of God was without Maude's trenchant resolution to make the best of things. Betty, the youngest sister, was a pale, unhappy-looking child of thirteen, inclined to sullenness and with fury burning in her eyes at her sisters' exhortations and prayers on her behalf. She was a clever child with a biting tongue that could shoot out home truths of devastating bitterness against her sisters. But Maude was well-armoured; she would turn the other cheek, and 

Betty would be reduced to inarticulate rage and tears.

Betty was convinced the world was against her and against her mother, who had long ago given up her personality to her energetic children and who lived now to clean and wash and cook for them, and to find solace in her religion in the brief moments she could spare from housekeeping. 

Bill's mother was sorry to leave Cheet Barn, which had been her home since she married, forty years before, though she would have less work to do in the small modern house they had taken in the town. She was resigned to leaving, because she hoped Maude and Pansy might find husbands—she gave much secret thought to the problem of the girls' futures-and she knew there was no one for them at Cheet but a few farm hands and a railway porter, and because the move would save them the journey to and from town which made Pansy ill in the winter. Pansy and Maude were wild with desire to leave 

Cheet and flaunt a new house and modern furniture. They longed to join a large chapel in town which would give them more life and social activities than the small tin chapel at Cheet, with its makeshift preachers and meagre congregations of poor cottagers. Cheet as a village was mainly church, and Maude in the fervour of her spirit had

NEW BED 89 upset most of the church people. Bill never interfered with his mother's or his sister's devotions; when Maude became too persistently evangelical he would leave the room and have his meal sent into the barn, where Maude dared not follow him-they were not allowed in the barn except to bring him food or a message, and then they never lingered, intimidated by Bill's intense irritation at seeing them there. 

Bill's mother's great distress was in leaving Bill to his heathenish ways. All day long she fretted and fussed about the house, for in her tired brain her household cares had become an obsession, a perpetual frown wrinkling her forehead, her restless fingers never still; and in the evening, when implored to rest, she would knit as if life depended on it, her lips pursed up, rocking herself to and fro in the old kitchen rocking-chair. But in the early morning, before the girls were up and while she was waiting for the kettle to boil, she would sit in the kitchen doorway and read a chapter from the Bible, chanting the words quietly as she rocked to and fro, not with the feverish preoccupation of other times, but gently soothed by the music of the words. Only Bill knew of this habit of hers, for he was an early riser also, and he would stand looking over the garden and listening to her husky voice droning to herself until she shut the book and would move to stir the fire under the kettle. In those few moments a look of contentment would steal over her face, her watery paleblue eyes shine mild and benevolent and her work-scarred hands lie at peace, the itch to fidget and worry forgotten. 

They said nothing to each other on these occasions, but Bill's mother took it as a sign of grace that he listened without impatience, and so could smile at her daughter's fears that hell-fire would surely claim him. She cherished, without being consciously aware of it, Bill's smile of affec-

NEW BED 

tion when she put the book down and looked up at him. 

She did not know that it was not the words she read which pleased him, but the serenity on her face. When he was left at Cheet with Ann she feared he would lose his one touch with godliness.

To her bitter regret Ann showed no sign that she would lead Bill the way he should go. When she had managed to get Ann by herself almost her first words had been, "Do you go to chapel, my dear?" and Ann's hesitating reply had told her what she feared from the beginning -Ann, also, was a heathen, for Ann had told her she had been brought up Church of England, which was almost as bad as the Church of Rome to Mrs. Darwin. 

As far as she could take any interest outside household affairs she was interested in Ann and could have soon learned to love her, for she found Ann biddable as none of her own children had been. Ann was gentle and pretty, and her voice made a pleasant sound about the place as she exclaimed her delight at country sights and ways. The house was enriched by her presence, if only because it made Bill thoughtful and kind; a little of the affection and care he bestowed upon Ann falling upon his mother. He was pleased with her because she left them alone and would bring their meals surreptitiously to the barn away from Maude's and Pansy's inquisitive questioning. 

It was accepted that Ann was not to begin her domestic duties until Bill's holiday was over and his mother and sisters had departed. Maude resented this. Ann's prettiness, her summer frocks, which looked almost flippant compared with those of her sisters-in-law, seemed to point to an incapable and useless wife for Bill. Maude was anxious Ann should understand her responsibilities. Boodle was a source of dissatisfaction. Officially he lived in the barn on a bag of straw and an old mat, but actually he

NEW BED 91 lived with Ann, and when driven out he would creep back and hide under Ann's chair, and silently Ann would aid him. "I suppose God had a good reason for making the lower animals. He meant them to serve us and for our protection but-" said Maude pointedly, "I do not think He meant us to use them as playthings and indulge them as if they were human beings." Betty and Ann were playing with Boodle on the grass. They were trying to make him dance like a bear on his hind legs, and Boodle was trying to understand what they wanted, and not succeeding very well. He looked serious and rather ashamed when Ann said: "Boodle, I don't believe you are doing your best."

Betty's usual pale face was flushed and happy. She gurgled with pleasure each time Boodle licked her when she thrust her face against his whiskery chin.

At Maude's words Betty made an ugly grimace and sat up, pulling her frock over her knees as if expecting a reprimand for indecorous behaviour. "I might have known you would come and spoil it,” she said bitterly. She turned to Ann. "That's what I have to put up with every day. How I hate it! Must I live with them? Every day for weeks and months and years

I've got to put up with them, until I'm grown-up, and perhaps then."

The morbid intensity of the words startled Ann. "Don't be silly, Betty," she laughed. "And we shall spoil Boodle if we play with him too much. If you lived with me I should have to be as hard as Maude." "But you wouldn't be against me; not all the time picking and preaching and praying for my sins to be forgiven. I only wish I'd got some big sins for them to pray for."

Ann got up and walked slowly across the orchard, hav-

NEW BED ing been told at school that the way to deal with hysterical children was to leave them alone. Boodle followed her, as if he disapproved also. Bill was in the barn, making a new bookshelf for Ann's books. "Anything up?" he asked, seeing her thoughtful face. "It's Betty. Couldn't she live here with us. I'm sure her nature is getting spoiled with Maude and Pansy. 

Maude preaches at her and Pansy looks mournful and makes her cry, and between the two she's morbid and hysterical. She gets too much notice taken of her in one direction and not enough in another."

Bill put down his plane to give added firmness to his words. "I want nobody here but you and me, Ann. I'm longing to begin my studies for the nymph. I can't bother with Betty. She must do as she's told." "But, Bill, it's serious. She'll be spoilt. Couldn't you send her away to school, then? Can't you see how she'll grow up?" "She's all right. Don't worry, Ann. I'm not having you worried with the family. Come and have a look at this shelf. It's a lovely piece of wood."

Ann sighed and turned her attention to the shelf. She was amazed at Bill's indifference, but she was too loyal to dwell on it. Bill knew best, she supposed, but uneasiness lingered in her mind.

He showed no signs of tyrannising over her in the same way as he tyrannised over his family. He was a devoted lover, quick to think of anything that might add to her comfort, and springing to her slightest wish or call. His sisters and mother were amazed at the thoughtfulness and devotion he showed, and horrified that Ann should take it for granted. "Will you move into Mother's room when we are

NEW BED 93 gone?" asked Maude. It was a large room, facing the front, papered in pale-pink satin stripes, with a white marble mantelpiece and a monumental bed of brass. "Bill likes his own room best. I don't think he cares for the front of the house much." "But I thought you had your own way in everything?" 

Ann laughed softly. "That sounds as if I am dreadfully spoilt, doesn't it?" she said. 

The happy tone of her voice irritated Maude. "I think it is perfectly indecent the way he wants to get rid of us. 

Cheet's been our home since we were born." "But I thought you wanted to live in town," answered

Ann, rather alarmed at Maude's visible bristling. "We are thinking of our duty to Mother. She won't have so much to do in a little house with modern conveniences. She won't have to be up so early to get us off to work. Our mother is not getting any younger. She has to work very hard and we must do what we can to help her."

The unctuous unconscious hypocrisy of Maude's voice nauseated Ann. Surely Maude must know how reluctant her mother was to leave Cheet. Ann had detected it almost as soon as she saw her. "We should be thankful we have it in our power to help our mother," went on Maude, now fairly wound up. "She must mistake me for her Sunday School class," thought Ann. "I'm sure you will find it jolly living in town," was the only reply Ann could think of. She moved to the door to escape, but Maude reached the door first and stood with her back to it. Ann had a strong sense of being cornered. "It might be 'jolly,' to use your word, although it doesn't seem to me to apply to our way of living, if Bill

95 concluded Maude was not pleased, and warily kept out of her way for the rest of the day. 

Ann told Bill something of the encounter. "You aren't turning your mother out for me?" she asked hesitatingly. 

Bill laughed. "Rubbish! I am paying all their expenses -rent, rates, light, coal, but not food. The new house is in Mother's name-Maude wanted it in her name, but I wouldn't allow it. Mother works her fingers to the bone for them, so I don't see why they shouldn't find her in food. They are doing very well out of it. If I gave her more-and I can't afford to-she would only spend it on the girls or chapel."

He was unexpectedly harsh when Maude began to collect small articles about the house and pack them for the new home. Ann felt it was because of what she had told him, and felt also that Maude guessed the reason for Bill's unusual watchfulness. Maude bore it all with patience and high charity. "I forgive you," she seemed to be saying as she brought Bill his slippers or fussed over his laundry. "There is One above who sees all and the pure in heart must triumph in the end."

Ann had only one more encounter with her. It was the day they moved-a Saturday. Mrs. Darwin and Pansy had already gone on. On the following Monday Bill returned to work. Maude was picking flowers to take to their new home and Ann and Betty were helping her. Ann felt it was ungracious to let Maude pick them alone. "What a pretty colour these violas are," she said conversationally, to break an awkward silence into which all three had fallen after one of Maude's tart remarks. Maude straightened her back. "Do you know what they are called?" There was a threat in her voice, but Ann put it down to the general air

of discomfort which had existed all day. Betty made a grimace, as if the opening was familiar and she guessed what was coming. "No," answered Ann. "Tell me.”

Maude detested Boodle. She collected the remainder of the flowers in a silence which was full of meaning. She felt the odds were against her at the moment. "You and I together could make old Maudie sit up," confided Betty to Ann when they were alone.

Betty looked at her owlishly, apparently considering the advice and finding it a trifle absurd. "It was a great mistake your marrying into this family.

97 as you were coming Paul gets packed off when he doesn't want to go any longer and has got fixed up with a good master here." "But what has Bill to do with it?" "He's got the money. Father left Aunt Rose, that is
