It was not the answer Ann desired. She could not understand Bill's lack of understanding about Betty. Wilfully, it seemed to Ann, he would not let himself think about it. He was so devoted and thoughtful to herself, anticipating her wants, guarding her from his mother and Maude's inquisitive questionings. He would not let her undertake the work of the house and had found a woman in the village to come in each day. He had swept the cobwebs from the barn because she had expressed a horror of the spiders which lurked there, and had installed a comfortable chair by the side of his bench because he wanted her to sit with him while he worked; he had had the piano tuned and overhauled and made her a cabinet for her music; in fact he had done everything possible to make her feel happy and comfortable in her new home.

Ann loved the barn with its curly shavings and smell of sawdust, its curiously-shaped tools in racks, the piles of rough logs still wearing their bark and the polished slabs, some dark as wine, others glistening yellow as amber or pale

NEW BED 

99 as ivory, and the sheets of precious woods stacked in corners with markings as luxuriant as the tracery of tropical foliage. 

She loved the kitchen also, because of its deep dim coolness when she entered from the garden whose gaudy clusters of asters and marigolds burned under the August sun. The worn floor was covered with slabs of local pinkish-coloured stone which shone with a pale enamelled lustre when it was washed and polished. The table and dresser tops were scrubbed white sycamore wood, fragrantly white it seemed to Ann, and the window seat was wide so that she could lie stretched out with Boodle at her feet reading or listening to the country noises going on outside. There were two wooden rocking chairs, black with age and years of polishing, by the fireside, and round the walls at intervals kitchen chairs, straight-backed and solid. Plush and mahogany of the ugliest shapes and colours were rampant over the rest of the house, iron bedsteads belligerent with brass knobs, carpets crudely pink and green, texts framed and unframed, bamboo stands holding plant pots where neglected plants mouldered into decay-a saddening and depressing collection that were treasured no longer and had not even the fact that they were used to humanise them.

Bill's bedroom, which Ann shared, was a large attic going the whole length of the old part of the house, its windows overlooking the orchard and the barn. Bill had made most of his own furniture-a chest, a carved wooden bedstead, a bookcase and a writing table. It had an oak floor, and an old rag rug by the fireside. Engravings from Claude Lorraine, classical landscape, a ruined temple, a nymph or two, coloured prints from Corot, some studies in red chalk of the female figure which caused his mother much heartburning, were on the walls. There was a heavy oak fireplace with part of an intricate design carved on it and then abandoned. Garlands of vine leaves and satyrs with leering faces and lean

NEW BED hairy shanks holding wine-skins was the principal motive.

In one corner was a tiny kid, its young legs uplifted in clumsy gambolling. When Ann awoke in the early morning she would lie in bed and go over each part of it. The satyrs fascinated her, and if she looked at them too long she could fancy they were moving along in procession. "It didn't come off. I'm going to take it down," said Bill when he first showed the room to Ann; but he never did, and Ann often wanted to remind him, for it seemed to her inappropriate in a bedroom, and she grew to dislike it.

Bill's mother and sisters were astounded that Ann did not mean to move into the best bedroom as soon as they quitted the house. It had always been the bedroom of the mistress of Cheet Barn, where she had slept with the master of the house and where her children had been born. "I shouldn't feel comfortable in it. It is too big," Ann tried to explain to Bill's mother. 

Bill's mother shook her head mournfully. "Maybe in a year or two. You are only young. Maybe you'll fill out a bit. 

I was twice as big as you when I was married. Men must have been different in those days." She was thinking of her own young days, and of the hard work she did, the children she had borne, the husband she had humoured and to whom she had submitted, and of how he had taken her labour and her pain for granted as part of her duty to life and himself. 

She had been glad to serve him with a health and strength that once had seemed inexhaustible. Ann felt something of the old lady's thoughts, and for the moments was chastened and humbled. "She wouldn't understand if I told her about 

Bill and me," she thought. "We'll keep the best bedroom for when you come and stay with us. You will, won't you?" she said coaxingly. 

Bill's mother was gratified. She was glad Ann had asked

NEW BED ΙΟΙ her to come, because Bill had said nothing about her visiting 

Cheet Barn. Her eyes twinkled unexpectedly. "You'll be glad of me some day. There's times when an old woman is handy about the place. You know what I mean maybe." 

Her roguish chuckle pursued Ann as she went down the passage. In spite of her embarrassment she was glad to find a spark of human nature still existed in Bill's mother which had not been swamped by the fierce gentilities of her daughters.

CHAPTER X 

ANN walked slowly to the front gate to see if the postman was coming. Two years had passed to this very day since she first came to Cheet, and it was her mother's custom to remember the anniversary of her wedding day and send her a parcel.

Ann had changed little in two years. She had grown quieter perhaps, and did not laugh so often at the ridiculous habits of ducks as they waddled in procession through the orchard, or balanced, tails up, guzzling at the bottom of the brook. Boodle had definitely left puppyhood behind him and was a serious, mature dog, walking in stately fashion at Ann's side. There were occasions when he forsook dignified ways and disappeared for a few hours to return bloody and triumphant after asserting his mastery over another dog in the village, but Ann felt he earned these dissipations by his devotion and gravity at other times; she liked to think also that he enjoyed himself in doggy ways and was not entirely under domestic control. Boodle was considered a savage dog in the village and he did not add to the popularity of the inhabitants of Cheet Barn.

Ann had made no friends. Communal life in the village centred round the church and chapel, and she belonged to neither.

Cheet was near enough to a large industrial city for it to have lost many of its rural ways. Near the station small new houses were springing up, inhabited by strangers who worked and had their interests in the town. The old country 102

NEW BED 

103 life was dying out and the curiosity of the villagers was so furtive that Ann was hardly aware of it. 

Except at week-ends and holidays, Bill went to town every day. The exact nature of his work in the warehouse Ann could never discover, for Bill was reluctant to talk of it. To him it was a means of making money which enabled him to live and buy the wood for what he considered his real work; but habit had made it not unpleasant to him. The days could be very long at Cheet when Bill was in town; singularly enough, the spring and summer days seemed harder to endure than the winter time. In the winter she got up later; there was more cooking to be done; and early in the afternoon she could light the lamp and draw the curtains and settle down to practising her music or to sewing or reading by the fire; but in the long bright days when her household duties were finished she could not stay in the house. She would go wandering down the orchard to the brook, through the fields and into the spinney, out into the lane and home again, to find the day still young and many hours to go before it was time for Bill to return. Boodle was a companion to her then. He would lie at her feet in the orchard while she sat sewing, or, as the habit grew on her, musing idly over old days at home when life had seemed always full of interest and things to do, and she had not been compelled to manœuvre to make the time pass by.

In the spring and early summer mornings the birds sang vociferously at Cheet; blackbirds and thrushes, willowwrens and chaffinches all clamorous with the ecstasy of living. Ann had never imagined bird song could be so loud and compelling. The urgency of the ringing notes of a thrush on a tall pear tree in the orchard could become maddening in its insistence, and Ann would turn to the piano, hoping to relieve the indefinable longing and melancholy they produced, yet find little relief.

NEW BED 

This year such moods had been frequent, and when the songtime of the birds had passed and the orchard was quiet under summer sunshine, a bank of flowers, or any other glimpse of beauty had the same power to make her heart ache with a sadness none the less heavy because the reason for it was hidden from her. It was a desire to still the beginnings of such a mood which had taken her to the front gate to watch for the postman. The postman came along and 

Ann took her parcel and ran indoors to open it. It contained a pair of silk stockings, a length of her mother's favourite flowered silk, a packet of chocolate, a bottle of cherries in brandy taken from stock, and a letter on thick rough-edged paper on the top of which was a black and white picture of "Ye Olde Thatched Inn."

Ann envied her mother's facility of so arranging her letters that the meagreness of their contents was concealed by the large important-looking handwriting and the thick opulent notepaper. This letter, however, ran to several pages; apart from the usual grumbles about Charlie, this time because he insisted upon buying his wife a new summer outfit through a friend of his in the trade instead of giving her the pleasure of a day's shopping in town, it contained news of Prudence. Her mother's vitality came through the limited range of phrases and words she used. "Do you know Prudence is married, but not the model

George, who was everything a young man should be, so her mother told me, and buying her a house and a car, but to a young curate as poor as one of his own church mice. Very 

High Church, too, and nothing to do it on. I'm surprised at 

Prudence, she was such a sensible girl and used to the best of everything. I met her in the bargain basement at Selfridge's, and she told me very proud of herself she was marrying for love and didn't care if they'd nothing to start housekeeping on, and she'd rather starve with Aubrey than

NEW BED 105 live in luxury with George, who was only a coarse moneymaking business man. Think of that! Did you ever hear such nonsense? She was buying one of those steamers that cook three things at once, on one gas jet. Very economical! I never saw a girl so changed. She sent her love to you, and I was to tell you she was having the time of her life. The young man came up-all brown eyes and no chest, thin as a rasher of wind and a saintly look on him due to undernourishment. Fancy Prudence getting off with an object like that. You did better, I must say.. Ann smiled for a moment at her mother's acknowledgment, for Mrs. Dibbs had never ceased to bewail that Ann had thrown herself away on Bill. Ann's correspondence with 

Prudence had been excessive for the first few months, and then had died away, as such correspondences usually do when they are based almost entirely upon items of news. 

Ann had little to say when she had written about the barn, the orchard, the ducks, and anecdotes of Boodle growing up. 

She could not discuss Bill with her friend; it meant continually explaining him, and she did not feel equal to it; and since Bill's mother and sisters had left Cheet her life had become a routine of little things-walks with Bill in the evenings or posing as his model while he worked, household duties, remaking her clothes and struggling with her music.

They seldom went to town to a concert or theatre. Bill was quite satisfied with his life at Cheet now. Contentment was making him almost urbane. He was filling out, and he looked more than ever like a high-minded Roman. Ann still hoped that when he finished his wood-carving of the nymph and had exhibited it fame would come to him and people would call-interesting people Bill would like-for when she had discovered in the early days of their marriage that Bill was indifferent to human society except her own, she put it down to the fact that he never met the right people.

NEW BED

This news of Prudence set her thoughts tingling. That 

Prudence should, after all, choose the romance of being poor instead of a comfortable middle-class life with George filled Ann with warm admiration for her friend. It made her think deeply about her own way of life. She had taken Bill to escape from Charlie Dibbs and his hideous proposals. 

Would they have been so intolerable after all? She was ashamed of her disloyalty at once. Bill was so kind, so good; always thinking of her. He never lingered a moment in town when he might be at home; he watched over her always. Though they slept together, it was as two comrades might do; and men, she knew from remarks her mother had let fall, were not often like this. Bill never wanted anyone but her, and to suggest she would like other companionship sometimes, younger companionship, must sound ungrateful. She could not do it. Was it the atmosphere of 

Cheet, where the women had always been afraid to voice their desires, where the men had always dominated and their wives and daughters blindly and obediently followed? 

Once or twice Bill had said: "I must take you to town. We will go to the theatre"; and then a few days later: "I don't think we'll go. It's a noisy, rackety place. I'd rather walk in the spinney with you or sit in the barn. I exhausted the theatre years ago," and never seemed to think she might not agree with him. Loyally she hid her disappointment. It was easy to understand that Bill, having town life all day, preferred to spend his leisure time at Cheet. "I could go by myself one afternoon?" she had suggested. "Oh, don't do that, sweetheart! I'd never rest. I'd imagine you run over or getting lost or something dreadful like that happening to you. I couldn't really bear to think of you in town alone." "Anyone would think I'd lived in the country all my life.

I always went about alone at home. I'm a town girl." She

NEW BED 

107 resented the implication which made of her a helpless creature fit only for a sheltered life; in one illuminating second she saw herself gradually being made into a useless parasite by Bill's care and affection. She did not know how to put up a fight. She compromised with herself, saying, "One day when I really want to go somewhere I'll go." "You are my own precious little girl and I can't bear to think of you alone in town. I shudder when I think of those days in London. Aren't you happy at Cheet? Is there anything you want?" 

What could she say to that? What did she say? Just told him that of course she was happy at Cheet, and if he did not want her to go alone she would stay at home, and turned her attention to fresh ways of passing the time. She started to learn Italian by herself, with the vague idea of one day persuading Bill to take her to Italy and search for her father's home. She must have something in the future, some hope of adventure for which she could work, and something with which to occupy her dreams. 

Her mother's letter stayed in her mind all day. She brooded over it and had worked herself into a mood of home-sickness by the time Bill came in to tea. She wept a little, thinking of Prudence who was lost to her; the children she had loved at Miss Farthingdyke's; the old days at home before Charlie Dibbs became too prominent and spoiled it all. Bill was amazed at her sad expression and her red eyelids, and though she told him about her mother's letter he did not seem to find the information particularly significant. His voice was heavy with displeasure and pain. "But, Bill, don't be tragic. It's only a day or two I want.

She had hurt Bill and done her own restlessness and heartache no good. She began to sob, quietly at first, then a great noisy one escaped her. The pain was unendurable; she turned and ran into Bill's arms to be comforted. "You don't really want to go, sweetheart?" he asked pleadingly when, later in the evening, they sat before a fire of larch boughs Bill had kindled. A chilly rain was falling and a fitful wind blew mournfully through the orchard, sending the apples thudding to the ground, but in the kitchen was the glow of the lamp and leaping firelight.

Boodle lay stretched on the hearthrug, his nose in the fender, grunting sleepily when Ann tickled him with the toe of her slipper. The room was enfolded in a warm domestic peace and cosiness. Bill was very fond, very tender.

CHAPTER XI 

THE meeting between Paul and Ann was decisive. They knew even as their first glances met and their hands clasped that each had found the other, and nothing that had happened before or might happen afterwards could make any difference to the bond between them. 

One does not, perhaps, in falling in love at first sight, go out haphazard and fall irrevocably in love with a stranger.

For Ann the readiness was there, and subconsciously the desire. Lonely, dreaming days at Cheet, dimly associated in her mind with Bill's nymphs and shepherd boys, had made her supersensitive to intangible feelings and desires. Beauty had played upon her as Paul played upon his fiddle, calling forth a response that was both discontent and taut expectancy. She had waited eagerly for the day when she should see Paul. She knew she must like him, but she had imagined nothing like this strong sensation of affinity which passed through her as their hands met. He stood silently before her while Bill introduced her, yet she knew the same wild exultation was flooding him as flooded herself. 

Paul was bewildered by the depth and brilliance of her brown eyes, the fairness of her silky hair as it curled back from her cheeks, her grace as she smiled and came towards him. There was a bloom on her. She was young, virginal and sweet, and the grossness of the world had passed her by. His surprise and delight at her beauty was almost more than he could bear. She was jolly, too, and the greeting she gave him was warm and friendly, which moved him, because he had experienced no feminine kindness at Cheet 

IIO

NEW BED 

III before. He did not get on well with women, in spite of his handsome face and figure; young women thought he was conceited, because shyness made him abrupt and cynical, while older women who understood this air of rudeness tried to mother him, which Paul resented; they could not hope to compare with his own mother, who had made an art of it.

Never had Paul been so brilliant or Ann so charming, or Bill so tolerant, for Bill also was affected by the undercurrent of excitement and felt his blood quicken and his mind more fluid. He was astonished at the change in Ann, her animation, the light in her eyes, the sparkling nuances in her voice, which had lost some of its cadences since she came to Cheet, because the people about her used flat, monotonous tones and unconsciously she had modulated her voice to theirs.

When they had talked their fill Bill suggested music. 

Paul declared he could not play his violin in the house; he preferred the barn, where his tones could get lost in the high-raftered roof. He suggested Bill should chip away at his work as in the old days, and that Ann should sit with them in the barn. He did not want Ann to play with him, and Ann felt that with riot and sweet confusion in her mind she could not follow a difficult accompaniment. What Paul wanted was that Ann should sit and watch him as he played, that he might feel the exhilaration of her presence mounting his brain and into his fiddle.

If Bill had a monotonous job on hand he liked working while Paul played; he would look up at Paul's swaying, graceful figure and watch the curve of Paul's arm and, contemplating how the muscles would go, dwell on the idea of a life-size figure of a fiddler out of a block of his favourite rosewood.

"It's those silly old ducks. I couldn't live without ducks.

Life would be incomplete. I'm sure that's why a lot of people in towns are bad-tempered and discontented. My cure would be give 'em ducks and a green field and a brook.

Bill was hunting about for his socks. "Old as Methuselah." Ann jumped out of bed contritely. She took some clean socks from the drawer and threw them to him. “I didn't think of that," admitted Bill. "In my experience the older one grows the less sense one has," retorted Ann roguishly.

In company with Mrs. Gordon, Ann worked busily about the house all morning. Ann supposed Bill was annoyed because he wanted to work and they were hindering him. She felt the joy oozing slowly out of her day; it was coming to such a dismal end. Paul was chilled also, and looked sullen and subdued. Tears came into her eyes and she wandered slowly across the garden to fasten up the ducks for the night. After she had coaxed the last duck to enter she lingered awhile, wondering why it was that Bill would not be friendly to Paul and let him share their life. 

Paul was so proud of Bill's work, and devoted as a young brother might have been.

NEW BED 119 Paul came up to her. He leaned against an apple tree and they stood and looked at each other wistfully, like two children who had been reproved and felt guilty, yet did not know what they had done. "I'm going now," he said. "Yes," answered Ann, but did not move, nor could she find anything to say but: "I'll practise that sonata.” "I should," said Paul politely, and still stared at her. "Well, I must go. Here's Bill coming. Good-bye." "Good-bye," murmured Ann, almost too faintly to be heard. She heard him shout to Bill and watched Bill turn and go into the barn again, and still she stood by the shed, choking back tears of disappointment, her hands clenched at her sides. A mouse came out, sat on its haunches a moment sniffing her, then scampered away; a large white moth brushed her cheek as it went by; she felt gossamer threads pass before her face; she still stood waiting. There was the noise of running footsteps on the path and sounds of heavy breathing as Paul came panting across the grass. "I left my music on the garden seat," he said, breathlessly. The depression had gone from his face. "Have to sprint for the train, but couldn't go without seeing you again." He was smiling at her. She held out both her hands. "Run for it," she said, animation sparkling on her face and her melancholy swept away at the splendid surprise of his return. He pressed her hands, held them a second, then put them to his lips and was gone, running and leaping down the lane, waving his parcel wildly, his heart singing even as Ann's. "That silly boy left his music," said Ann gaily to Bill as she ran into the barn. "I'll take you to some of the concerts when they start," 

Bill offered.

Ann suspected for one second that this was a kind of

NEW BED peace-offering because he had been harsh earlier in the evening, but she dismissed the thought. "I'd love to go," she said fervently. "Wouldn't you?" "I'm not particular, but I'll take you if you want to go. "I wish you'd want to go as well," murmured Ann into

Boodle's ear, as she bent over and stroked his curly forehead. Boodle paid no attention. He was spoiled enough to know the words were not meant as endearments for him.

CHAPTER XII 

ONE or two afternoons in the following week Ann walked in the spinney, but each time she met no one on her walk and returned disappointed; but disappointment did not make her melancholy; it was almost sufficient to know Paul lived only a few miles away and that before long she must surely see him again. Bill was pleased her contentment and merry ways had returned. Nearly every day he would bring her gifts-a book, a piece of music she had mentioned, chocolates, fruit; and one day a padded blue silk dressinggown embroidered with sprays of apple blossom and tiny crimson-breasted birds. Ann feasted her eyes on it, her voice hushed with joy; never before had she owned such a beautiful garment. "Bill, it's the loveliest thing" "It's because you've been so good about the nymph." "I'm glad to help," murmured Ann shyly.

That evening when she got down from the model's throne in the barn Bill called to her. "Don't dress," he said, as she pushed her toes into her slippers. He folded the blue dressing-gown about her and took her in his arms. "Now you are as snug and soft as a bird in a nest," he said. "I want you to do something for me, will you?" 

Ann nodded, trembling as she caught some of the excitement mounting in Bill which the gentle way he held her and his quiet voice only intensified. "I want you to go out into the orchard like a nymph; I want to see you among the trees in the moonlight as I have often dreamed. will you?" .. 121

NEW BED 

"Yes," said Ann. "But-" "You will come to no harm, sweetheart, I'll promise you."

Emotion made his face look strange to her. She had never seen him so transformed; there was longing in his eyes and a kind of subdued wildness. She did not know what to say or how to look at him. Without a word she slipped the dressing-gown from her and fled into the orchard, wanting to please him, yet wanting to get away from the strange look on his face. The air was full of the scent of apples. It struck cold and sweet on her limbs, so that she gasped at first and walked carefully, but seeing Bill following her, she ran between the trees.

The moon made bright and dark patches on the grass, and she did not know which were the more terrifying, the streaming lines of moonlight shedding an uncanny brilliance on bough and twig, so that they were utterly changed from their familiar daylight form and colour, or the sinister patches of shadow where unknown creatures of the night might lurk in waiting. A withered leaf fell on her back, a moth struck her on the breast with a soft thud of its woolly body, a low bough trailing a tuft of leaves brushed her bare shoulder. Behind her she heard thud thudding of apples falling, and they sounded to her like the thudding of tiny hoofs on the turf as she ran to and fro in distress. A horror of the night and of what she was doing came over her. 

She became afraid as she ran and began to sob for breath, a wild unreasoning terror in her heart. "Bill, Bill," she called. "Why, sweetheart, it's all right," comforted Bill, and put the dressing-gown round her and led her into the house. 

Ann began to cry piteously, her nerve gone.

Bill carried her upstairs and put her on the bed. He lighted the fire of dry twigs and apple wood laid on the hearth, lit the candles and pulled the curtains to shut out

NEW BED 

123 the moonlight. He could not do enough to show his love, and his eyes were soft, his hands tender as he went to and fro, making hot milk for her to drink, filling the hot-water bottle, building up the fire, so that the room was drenched in firelight and drowsy with warmth. Ann gradually recovered her poise. "Was it how you thought it would be?" she asked shyly, when he came to bed. "It was almost unbearably beautiful. I hadn't dreamt of it quite like that. Not the unearthly radiance. . . . One moment you were like ivory, then a dim white shadow, and the next moment shining silver under the moonlight. And when you ran and stretched out your hands the light rippled down your arms. ." Bill stopped. He came and knelt by the bed. His eyes were haunted, and they did not lose that look for several days. · • In the morning when Bill was going about the usual routine of getting ready for work Ann said: "Could you ask Paul to come one evening soon? I've practised the music he left. I'd like to try it over with him.” 

Bill could not refuse her in his new mood of tenderness. "Why not?" he said. "Now the nymph is practically finished we will show it to him, shall we?" "That will be very nice," answered Ann placidly.

Whether it was the adventure in the moonlit orchard that had shaken him out of his moroseness or because he was filled with a warm glow of satisfaction about his finished work-he knew it was the best thing he had ever done-Bill was genial to Paul when he came. Imperceptibly Paul slipped into the habit of coming several times a week, insinuating himself into the routine of the house, helping Bill, and climbing the trees in the orchard and shaking down the apples and pears to the accompaniment of squeals of delight from Ann and Boodle's mad rushes to and fro as the fruit

NEW BED 

fell all around him and he tried to catch it. When the time came to send in Bill's work to the autumn exhibition it was Paul who helped to carry it to the station, and Paul who was most full of hopes of its success. 

Bill took an hour or two from work to take Ann to the opening of the exhibition. Ann met him in town, and they lunched grandly in a restaurant. Then they walked to the art gallery, Ann holding Bill's arm as he presented his card to the attendant and they passed in. She wore her new flowered silk frock and was afire with pride and delight, her brown cheeks flushed with excitement at this unaccustomed excursion into social life. The smartly-dressed women walking to and fro, chattering as they glanced at the pictures and statuary, the groups of earnest young men in corners, the groups of older men not taking it so seriously, the unmistakable figures of the local artists in large black hats and beards, spinsters in Roman silk scarves and handwoven skirts, an indescribable smell of pictures newly done, a faint aroma of China tea and cake from the refreshment room, the range of colours and lights and scents intoxicated Ann after the seclusion of Cheet. How full the world must be of people interested in the arts, she told herself, and the thought made her glad.

Bill's nymph, the statuette of the shepherd boy, and a panel on which he had carved two hares boxing were grouped by themselves and attracted a good deal of attention. Bill eyed them from a distance, and while he could not be persuaded to go right up to them so as to get a good view of the way they had been arranged, yet his glances at the other rooms were perfunctory, and he was back again at the edge of the group of people who looked at his work. 

Ann thought the nymph looked very lovely on her pedestal; she did not identify it as herself, but regarded it as 

Bill's creation entirely, and could look at it therefore with

NEW BED 

125 an impersonal eye. The nymph had her hand stretched out, holding the trunk of a birch sapling whose branches and leaves streamed over her head as if the wind blew them.

The curves of her limbs were tender with youth and more pliant than any sapling, and there was only a hint of womanhood in her figure, and no desires of the world on her face. 

The wood was smooth and polished, the close grain giving the feeling of firm flesh. "I don't want to leave it," sighed Ann, when Bill began to lead her away. "Aren't you proud of him?" asked a voice behind them.

It was Paul, looking romantically in keeping with the place, in a large black hat and a flowing necktie. "How did you get in? I didn't know you had a ticket," said Bill.

Paul grinned and pointed to his tie. "There are more ways than one of getting in. 

Ann laughed gaily, then as suddenly looked grave again. "Isn't it too bad Bill has to go? He says he can't stay a minute longer and I haven't seen half the things or had tea." "I could take you for some tea and show you round if

"I've always wanted to take a girl to one of these affairs. a pretty girl, like you . . . the

NEW BED other chaps who've only got maiden aunts or dowagers in tow will look so sick."

He fetched buns and a dish of cakes with an amazing rapidity and deftness. An elderly man glared as Paul apologised and whisked away a plate of sandwiches from under his nose; he watched Paul wind his way persuasively through immobile groups, and his glare of annoyance turned to unwilling admiration and regrets for his own lost youth.

The table was small, the crush around them great; sometimes they were obliged to lean forward so that their faces nearly touched. There is something in the ritual of drinking tea that makes for intimacy. Voices are tuned lower and take on caressing intonations; there are sudden quick glances and little smiles; the slightest change of expression can be revealed and answered; a conversation goes on of which the least important part is the actual words spoken. "Do have a bun," said Paul, holding out the plate as if he were offering himself upon it. "Won't you have some more tea?" asked Ann, and smiled as if there were no greater pleasure in life than to hand him a cup and saucer and hold a lump of sugar poised above it. Words do not matter. They are transmuted from the commonplace into something rich and strange. They are like the leaves in the fairy tale that are changed to gold when the right people, at the right time, handle them. Ann and Paul had no consciousness of the surging of men and women about them; their whole awareness was confined to a space about a yard square, into which themselves and their table were squeezed.

The clamour began to die down and the room to empty. 

There was a desolation of crumbs and used china on the tables. Paul looked at his watch. "We'll have to step it out to catch your train. Unless

NEW BED 127 you'd like to wait and catch the six o'clock. That's Bill's train, isn't it?" "Oh, no! I want to be home first," decided Ann. Paul picked up her bag and gloves and presented them to her. "Come along then."

The braided and gold-buttoned commissionaire at the door saluted and murmured "Good evening, sir.” Ann in the fullness of her delight gave him a wide smile which left him fingering his moustache. In the street trams went clattering by and newsboys called; yellow lights glimmered, and there was a feeling of autumn in the sky. Ann loved it all. "I'd like to come again," she said. "Why not? It's not such a bad place," answered Paul, forgetting his diatribes about the city's dirt and hideousness when compared with the beauty of foreign cities. 

The guard was just going to wave his green flag as they hurried along the platform. He opened a carriage door for them and shut them in and waved the train on. "But, Paul, you mustn't come." "I couldn't let you go abruptly. A day like to-day wants to come to an end with a dying fall, you know, a gentle rounding off. Don't you think so?" He waved his hand to illuminate his meaning. Ann's eyes were laughing; they bubbled over. "I think it's lovely. How far are you coming?" "All the way. Can I?" 

Ann nodded. "I'd be glad. Did you like the nymph?" she asked after a moment's silence. "Yes. The work is marvellous. Talk about a genius for taking pains! And he loves his wood. But it's cold. Bill got it bad, and then the war came and took him away, and instead of forgetting the old stuff and getting the after-the-war spirit, he still holds on to the ideas he used to have. CHAPTER XIII 

AFTER the opening of the exhibition Bill's friendliness to Paul faded away again. Ann had the suspicion that although 

Bill had consented to Paul taking her back into the art gallery for tea after they had seen him to the door he had not enjoyed it. Thinking it over, she was remorseful that they had sped him on his way so gaily and then had forgotten him; it had been thoughtless, even unkind. She was learning now how sensitive he was to anything which concerned the relationship between him and herself. He did not want her to have any life without him. She brooded on the idea and found herself regarding him critically for the first time. 

The whole countryside around her was heavy with autumn. Ann had never felt the fall of the year in quite the same way before, nor had it made her contemplative, brooding over this thing and that in her mind. Paul's explanation of Bill's work haunted her. She had had until now a clear, unquestioning faith in Bill's future. She had regarded herself as the wife of an artist potentially great, trusting that when his genius was recognised they would be able to travel and see the world and that life would open out for them. She had regarded the monotony of Cheet as a period of apprenticeship; she had never imagined in the old days a quiet domestic life with no adventures or interludes until she grew old and died. She loved Cheet Barn; she did not want wealth or an ostentatious life; what she craved was something to occupy her mind and her energies, something beside the petty routine of household duties; it was a longing undefinable yet very real. Before she had married Bill 130

NEW BED 

131 and for some time afterwards life had appeared to be crammed with possibilities. There were so many things which might happen-delightful, unexpected, interesting things; now when she looked ahead she felt cheated and betrayed. It had given her so little for her mind and spirit and heart to feed upon and when she did find a companionship which excited her Bill frowned upon it, and what made it more depressing, she feared he would frown upon any endeavour of hers which meant more freedom for her. Bill was content to remain as he was. He had not shared in her surprise that the exhibition of the nymph had not made more difference to their way of living. He had what he wanted out of life, and thought she should be content also. 

He was afraid for her to be free; he wanted to keep her shut up in himself. Possession was the strongest instinct he possessed, she felt. He held doggedly to his own and would not share with anyone. Such thoughts led inevitably to the pondering of how far one person could belong to another. 

She questioned many things as she went about tidying the garden and planting her bulbs for the spring. 

The autumn was making Boodle restless also. Ann had been obliged to watch him because there were rumours in the neighbourhood of a dog seen worrying sheep, and Bill had warned her that if Boodle were seen in a field alone a farmer might shoot him in a panic, even if there were no sheep near, because they suspected and disliked every strange dog at these times. Ann could not imagine anyone shooting a dog, and particularly Boodle, and said so; nevertheless she watched him closely, and when he went sniffing in ditches and through the hedges, pretending absurdly he had only to sniff hard enough and a rat or a rabbit would appear, Ann chided him and made him walk by her side on the path.

Boodle put up with this for several days and even pre-

NEW BED 

tended to enjoy it when Ann patted him and told him how sorry she was to spoil his fun; then, about a week after 

Ann's visit to town, his desire for investigation and adventure was too much for him and he succumbed to a fascinating trail and forgot his mistress. Afterwards Ann confessed to herself that it was her own fault. They had been walking together companionably enough until they met Paul. 

Ann had not hoped to see him, and in her surprise and delight she forgot to watch Boodle. Paul and she had wandered a mile or two before she missed him. She whistled softly at first, watching the gaps in the hedges, expecting to see him come struggling through, but no Boodle appeared.

She called down the lane, but no Boodle came thundering along panting with eagerness, ears shaking and tail wagging. She walked back the way she had come, calling imperatively now and scanning the fields on either side as she went, but there was no sign of him. "Perhaps he's gone home," suggested Paul, who did not want Ann to worry about Boodle, but to talk to him. "I've never known him to go home from a walk before it was necessary." 

"Some love affair perhaps. You want to give him a little freedom. Even a dog likes to go his own way sometimes.” "Yes. . . Poor Boodle. 

Poor Boodle. . . . It's not been much fun lately. Bill warned me. I should have kept him on the lead," said Ann, fighting down her fears as she thought how she longed to escape from guardianship occasionally, tender and loving though it was.

They came to the house and went in. Ann called halfheartedly about the garden and orchard, but she did not hope for response. "Perhaps I'd better stay. I really only meant to see you for a little while and go back to town. Bill seems tired of me this week."

NEW BED 

133 "Don't go," said Ann. "I'll get tea ready, and then we might walk down to the village. He may have gone after another dog." "I'll go and look down the lane while you are busy, shall

She hurried about her duties, stopping every few moments to go to the door and look across the orchard, hoping to see Boodle sneaking in with a guilty look and perhaps scars of battle on his ears, but there was no sign of him. The orchard and garden looked desolate and empty. In her anxiety she cut the bread in jagged slices, spilt the jam on the cloth, and then in a rush to be finished and out again she knocked over the milk jug and had to fetch a clean tablecloth and start afresh. She had nearly finished when she heard voices outside. It was Bill and Paul talking together. 

Bill was reassuring. Boodle was probably engaged in a love affair, and when it was over he would come home. Infected by Ann's anxiety, however, they hurried over the meal, and when it was finished Bill and Paul set off to the village together. Ann wanted to go with them, but Bill persuaded her to stay at home.

Ann cleared away the tea-things, then went through the garden to the front gate and stood looking down the lane. 

A few farm labourers went cycling by on their way home, and a couple passed so engrossed in each other they did not see her staring away over the gate. Yellow lights sprang up here and there in the distance; she could hear a slow train chuffing its way into the station; there were far-off farmyard noises, ducks quacking, a belligerent cock. A sense of twilight loneliness deepened and her anxiety became acute. Why didn't Boodle come home? She could not imagine anything could keep him away from her for so long. 

Then she saw Paul hurrying down the lane.

NEW BED "Do go in," he said. "Where's Bill?” "He sent me for something. Do go inside, Ann. Bill will be cross if he finds you here in the cold mist." "What have you come for? Where's Bill?" demanded 

Ann, scrutinising Paul's pale face. "Bill's in the spinney." Paul took her by the hand, but

"Go in, go in," came floating back to her in a thin high wail. Ann hurried after him, stumbling over twigs and fallen fruit on the ground, fear in her heart. Paul had just handed Bill one of the tools. On the ground between them was Boodle with his legs stretched out straight and taut as she had seen him lie many times on the hearthrug, abandoned voluptuously to the warmth of the fire. But she knew he would never lie by the fire again on a winter evening, twitching in what seemed like exciting doggy dreams. "A brute of a farmer shot him," said Bill. "I found him in the corner of the field. It was a straight shot and must have finished him at once. He would hardly have felt it.” 

Ann knelt and looked down at Boodle. Pictures of the old life came rushing over her-her mother, Prudence, Miss Marble, Charlie Dibbs, with his generosities and vulgarities. "Oh, Boodle, Dibbs's dog Boodle. And now I shan't have you any more," she cried in desolation and dismay, tears streaming down her cheeks.

NEW BED 

135 Bill pulled her up from the ground. "We'd better bury him before it gets dark," he said gently. "And there's not much time."

White mist was curling through the trees and the air was cold and damp. Autumn became suddenly bodeful and spoke of decay and dissolution. Dying leaves from the birch trees floated down and fell quietly on Boodle's plump side. "I'd like to help," said Ann, and wiped the tears roughly from her eyes that she might see the place to dig. Paul handed her his fork. "I'll fetch another," he whispered, and vanished into the mist.

They dug for some time in silence, the only noise the gurgling of Bill's pipe and a clink when their tools met, for it was growing dark rapidly and they worked more by feeling than sight. "That'll do," said Bill.

They lifted the body with difficulty and laid it in the hole. 

Ann covered it with dead leaves, yellow and brown like Boodle's own coat; then they shovelled the earth back again as quickly as they could, Bill patting the mound into shape. Their faces and hands were white blurs, wan in the dusk, as they walked back silently through the orchard; occasional unseen boughs whipped them, but they took no heed, only bowed their heads lower and walked on. 

Ann poked the fire and set the kettle singing on the blaze.

She made a hot drink and poured it out, and the strong aroma of coffee filled the room. Bill and Paul sipped theirs gratefully, for they were cold, but when she went to put the cup to her lips Ann broke down and ran quickly out of the room and up the stairs. The bedroom door slammed behind her and silence fell upon the house. 

The two men looked at each other. There were tears in 

Paul's eyes, though he was not fond of dogs and had no

NEW BED 

particular affection for Boodle, but Bill was calm and undisturbed. "I told Ann to keep the dog up. I knew Wilkes would shoot at sight any strange dog he found on his land. He's crazy because his sheep have been worried."

Paul nodded sadly. Ann had suddenly become a stranger to him. He did not want her to be weeping for Boodle; he wanted her to be thinking of him. "I'd better be off for my train," he said, half-heartedly.

He felt unwanted and unhappy and wished desperately that 

Bill would show some signs of wanting him to stay.

Bill was giving all his attention to a supper tray for Ann. "You might put the spades away in the shed as you go," he said, and turned his back on him to watch that the milk heating on the stove did not boil over. 

Paul walked to the station, brooding mournfully over the fact that Ann had been unaware of him all the evening, and even now was weeping alone in her room and had let him go without saying good-bye.

CHAPTER XIV 

EARLY in the afternoon of the following day Ann heard a timid knock on the kitchen door, and going listlessly to open it she found Paul standing outside, holding a bundle of books and magazines which he thrust into her hands without speaking. He looked pale and dejected, and when she asked him to come in he stood fidgeting uncomfortably on the doorstep. "No, I don't want to come in. You come out. It is lovely in the sun. Let's have a walk," he said, and gave quick, embarrassed glances at her red eyelids. Ann ran upstairs to put on her hat and coat. "We'll go across the fields, not down the lane," suggested Paul. "I know a rather different walk, which will take us to Tavor. It's about three miles." "I've never been that way," said Ann. 

Carefully avoiding the spinney, they walked quickly across the fields until they came to a narrow road Ann had never walked before. The hedges were high and untrimmed and full of autumn colour, thorn and briar red with berries, bramble leaves turning crimson, elderberry bushes heavily laden with clusters of black fruit and spangled chattering starlings, young beech trees with yellow-brown leaves fluttering like flags at the ends of twigs, and mellow sunlight over them all. "It's jolly here," said Ann, but she did not lift her eyes to Paul's face as she usually did when she spoke to him. 

Paul stopped with such vehemence in the middle of the road that a singing robin took fright and flew farther down 137

NEW BED the hedge, where he continued his song louder than ever, as though in defiance of his fears. "Don't be unhappy, Ann. I can't bear it," said Paul passionately. There was such distress in his voice and such misery that Ann stopped also and put her hand on his arm. "I can't help it. Boodle made such a difference. I'm all alone here now. . . . I feel I have lost part of myselfthe me that used to be. It's not only Boodle-more and more of the old me has been disappearing lately, and I'm not becoming anything else. It's a kind of fading out, if you know what I mean." 

Paul faced her. "I think I do," he said heavily, and Ann knew they were both thinking of Bill and Bill's strength and how powerless they were. Out of loyalty to him they would not put their thoughts into words. "I shall miss Boodle for walks," she said, and tears came into her eyes. "I don't suppose I shall ever go out now by myself." "I'll come walks with you," offered Paul. "Couldn't we see more of each other in the daytime? We must keep it secret, so nobody can spoil it. Let it be something between you and me ... I like secrets," he went on boyishly. "I've always had them, even when I was a tiny kid. When one's affairs are common property the bloom gets rubbed off and the charm goes."

Ann hesitated. She knew Bill would not care for her to go walking alone with Paul. She wanted to keep faith with him. But what harm would it do? Was it fair of Bill to forbid her an innocent companionship with one of her own age and interests? Suppose she asked him? She thought of the heavy gloom of Bill's displeasure and was afraid. "I don't see why not," she said almost defiantly, then softened: "I suppose we couldn't tell Bill?" 

Paul disagreed with a violent nod of the head.

Die Liebe muss bei beiden allzeit verschweigen sein, Drum schliess die grossten Freuden in deinem Herzen ein." "What does it mean?" "I'll translate it for you word for word." He looked at her strangely. "Only not to-day. One day when we are very happy together." "I'm happy now being with you." The words came huskily and faint. Ann looked at him, then looked away, caught up suddenly in a pain that was not sadness or happiness or exaltation, but a mixture of all three, strange to her and terrifying, for she felt she had no control whatever over it. 

She could not meet his eyes, but unconsciously their hands met and they stood, overwhelmed by each other, in the middle of the road, with the autumn sunshine around them and an autumn robin singing in the hedge. They did not hear him, for their ears were muffled, and in their own breasts a wild, troubled music was beating itself out. How long they stood in silence they did not know. A farm cart came rattling by, holding under a net a tottery black calf which called weakly to its mother, who trotted with anxious eyes by the cart. Her heavy udders swung from side to side as she strove to keep pace. Ann and Paul drew into the hedge to let it pass.

NEW BED 

"Poor thing!" said Ann. "Sometimes I hate the country. 

It is so cruel." "Town will be best now the winter's coming. There are lots of things we can do. I know cafés and tea-shops with shaded lamps on little tables where we can sit and talk while the fog and drizzle and noise go on outside. I've got such a lot of things to tell you!" "Oh," said Ann, catching her breath with desire. “If I could. It would be an adventure, and I never thought to have adventures any more." "They come if you want them. It's not wanting them that's the awful thing. When I think of the wonders in the world, the things one might see and do and hear, I get terribly impatient. If I lived to be five hundred I could never hear all the music I want to hear-not sufficiently to know it well. It's the same with people. One so seldom gets to know them. They are hedged round with stupid barriers. 

They won't be honest and frank about affection, and then when a chap's dead they are sorry and wish they'd showed him how much they loved or appreciated him. What's the good, then?"

Paul's fists were clenched, his eyes glowed, his face was pale with the vehemence of his passion. 

Ann was humble and earnest. "We'll be honest with each other, won't we?"

Paul listened and his passion dwindled and died. It seemed so much empty fury beating the air. He knew that to be honest with Ann he would have to go down on his knees and worship her. "Yes. I don't know. . . . Is it possible? Can one be honest ever? Life is so extraordinarily difficult . . . the compromises one has to make. . 

Ann laughed merrily. "Oh, Paul, don't be so hopeless about everything. Why, it can be a lovely world. Look at

NEW BED 

141 to-day how nice it is walking along together and talking.' 

She wanted to reassure him about life as she had never wanted to reassure anyone before; she wanted his eyes to lose their burning, intense bewilderment and distress. He was too sensitive, she felt, and these times when he went about with his quivering nerves exposed life must hurt him.

She had never felt the desire to cherish before, and it filled her with a warm gladness. Bill never wanted to be cherished and comforted. His egotism made him the one who must do the comforting and cherishing. 

Paul threw off his doubting mood as quickly as it had come. "Come on. Let's see if we can get a meal at Tavor. All this talking has given me a thirst." He grinned at Ann and she laughed back at him. They walked on and came to the village, where a garrulous old woman in a cottage gave them tea. She was pleased to have visitors and kept trotting backwards and forwards suggesting different things they might have to eat, ranging from boiled eggs to tinned salmon and cold beef. She was not daunted when they protested that tea and bread and butter and cake was all they could eat, and she spread the table with several kinds of jam, a dish of tomatoes, a large home-made tart, rock buns, plates of bread and butter, and as a crowning triumph a monumental fruit cake, on a glass stand, which must have weighed several pounds. Ann's eyes grew deeper with laughter at each addition the old lady put proudly on the table, until by the time the old lady brought the teapot Ann was in pain with suppressed merriment. They discovered an appetite, however, and made a good meal, and it was the old lady's turn to laugh at them, which she did good-naturedly. "Where's the sense in clemming yerself, I always say," she reproved.

She leaned over the garden gate, smiling in benediction

NEW BED as they set off on their homeward journey. "Enjoy yerself. 

You're only young once," she cried shrilly.

Something of the thoughts which caused the words, the thoughts of an old woman who watched enviously, thinking of her own youth, two young lovers in all the pride of health and good looks, came to them. It made them conscious of blood running high and free. They strode along, invigorated and refreshed, heads up, deliberately enjoying the physical act of stepping out together on the homeward road which stretched out invitingly before them golden with afternoon sunlight, with a misty autumn bloom on distant trees and a milky blue sky banked with softly-lighted clouds. "I won't come in," said Paul, as they came near Cheet 

Barn. "I'll cut off to the station. I'll come again to-morrow.

We must enjoy these days while we may." 

Ann agreed. What was there in life and what after death that she should cut herself off from things which brought delight and happiness? Soon she would be old, and then she would be sorry, thinking of wasted days and adventures refused.

She turned into the house. It was close on the time when 

Bill returned from town. The kitchen door was open and smoke puffed from the chimney. Bill appeared in the doorway. "How early you are!" she said, alarmed. "I came away earlier, thinking you'd be lonely to-day," he answered, surprised to see her cheeks glowing from the walk and her eyes clear and bright again. He pointed to the books and papers Paul had brought. "Where did these come from?" "Paul brought them. He thought I'd be miserable. I was. Then we went for a walk and I felt better." "What time was that?” "This morning. Or rather, early this afternoon."

NEW BED 143 "Have you been out all day?" "Well, hardly all day. We walked to Tavor and back." "That's barely three miles. It couldn't have taken long. 

What did you do there?" "We had tea in a cottage with a funny old woman. Such a pretty walk, Bill," she chattered on insincerely, but Bill interrupted her. "Where's Paul now?" "He ran for a train. I asked him to come in, but he wouldn't." 

Ann became uncomfortable and self-conscious. The conversation had changed into a cross-examination. Bill's voice, which had begun suavely enough, had gradually hardened until now the words snapped out at her in short angry phrases. "I suppose he's gone slinking home. Ashamed to face me." "You've got it all it all wrong. I was desperately unhappy about Boodle, and then Paul came and took me out so that 

I'd not be so sad. It was kind of him. Don't be horrid, Bill." 

Her words faltered. Was it quite truthful to say Paul had taken her out because he was sorry about Boodle? She weakened. The exhilarating idea of the afternoon of finding joy where she could and living her own life was in danger. 

Bill had his opportunity then to win her, but he was too engrossed in himself to take it. If he had been generous and kind, she might out of loyalty have turned from the idea of Paul and herself against a stupid, cruel world. "I'll not have him hanging around here in the daytime.

I don't want him taking you out while I'm working and keeping the home. Either you belong to me or you don't," he said brutally. His eyes were hard and unhappy, his face set and ugly in a harsh imperviousness to any mood but his own. Ann stiffened, and her resolution to go her own way

Ann became aware of a great depression and a great weariness. How make him understand? It was impossible.

Only his own frustration meant anything to him. After all the emotion of the day this was too much. She could not cope with the situation. Bill was unhappy, she was unhappy, and she was afraid Paul must always know more unhappiness than joy because he exposed a quivering and sensitive spirit to a harsh world. Bill rushed to her, and she allowed herself to be comforted and her tears dried as if she was the clinging child he would have her be. As she lay in his arms she told herself she would not go out with Paul to-morrow. Bill loved her so. It was easiest to give in, to sink quiescent in his warm protection and leave the outside world to itself; but next morning when Paul came to take her out her resolve faded away.

CHAPTER XV 

THERE began then in that golden October the happiest time Ann had ever known. Resolutely she pushed away all self-reproach about deceiving Bill, arguing to herself that the life she led when he was in the city was her own affair and took nothing from him; in the evenings and week-ends she was his, to do whatever he wished and go wherever he pleased. Not that he ever wanted to go anywhere; he was content at Cheet Barn with Ann docile and without the restlessness which had formerly troubled him. Ann found the days so full now that when evening came she was glad to rest and dream.

Sometimes out of nowhere a great fear would sweep over her of what would happen if Bill found out she was spending most of her time with Paul; sometimes she woke up at night, startled by an ugly dream of Bill rampant, Bill unhappy, Bill reproachful; and she would lie awake with her heart beating painfully, seeing her whole life as something spoiled and dishonest, remembering bitterly how high her ideals had been; but when morning came it would be forgotten and she would rush through her work to get out with Paul. She was ecstatically happy, filled with a satisfying sense that life was rich and full and that time laden with interest was rushing by, instead of the old stagnant process of one dull day after the other.

Their friendship was innocent enough. They tramped the countryside for miles, eating extraordinary makeshift meals in country inns and cottages; they sat in cafés on wet days and never knew how dreary the town was under cold 145

NEW BED 

drizzle or fog, once they were together; occasionally they went to a matinée or the cinema, where lights were dim and the atmosphere sympathetic to romantic undertakings. The wonder was they found so much to talk of, for they talked all the time. There was so much to tell, and as they talked their hearts and minds were eased, and they began to see the world, not as an enemy, inimical, antagonistic, but as a poor, hapless, battered thing knowing too little love and too much frustration. They learnt that all the world is kindly to lovers; bus conductors, train officials, waitresses, proprietors of country inns, all seemed to look on them with friendly eyes, and performed many a little manœuvre, humorously or sadly, according to their dispositions, for their benefit. 

Paul and Ann were aware of them as pleasant but vague presences in a world fantastically rosy-hued. 

Ann's eyes shone brightly nowadays; her voice was quick and merry, and she walked with a kind of lilting hurry when she was alone, so that an experienced observer could have told a lover's meeting was ahead of her, for she had a blessed and happy look. She became aware of her own prettiness and cherished it, and could have laughed with joyperhaps she did unconsciously-each time she looked in her mirror and realised how she was blossoming into beauty. 

Her clothes caught a reflected glow from this knowledge; she wore them with an air, and the girls in Cheet, seeing her hurry by, would wonder what it was that made the stranger different from themselves.

So far Paul and Ann had not questioned what they were doing and where it might lead them. They would not believe so exalted a feeling could be anything but noble. They knew they were the richer and finer because of it, and that it made shining radiant days out of monotony. It influenced their whole mode of living, and the people with whom they came in contact benefited by the glow in their hearts. Ann

NEW BED 

147 was more understanding and affectionate to Bill; not because her conscience was uneasy, but because her nature had deepened and a sense of life rich and strange and perplexing had come to her. She now felt a great tolerance and kindness towards everything, for her happiness welled up and overflowed, so that it embraced, not only Paul, but the whole world.

The fits of moodiness which had used to trouble Paul, and his one-time bitter outbursts on the value of an artist in a world that cared only for commerce, changed into a pleasant irony and leaping high spirits. His ambition was spurred onwards by Ann's adoration of his work. He played better than he had ever played before, and already he was being picked out by the conductor and committee of the municipal concerts as a player of promise good for promotion. When he was not with Ann he practised hard. When he left her he would go swinging home and upstairs to the attic away from the distractions of the house and his mother's plaint that she never saw him nowadays, and he would get down to serious work, enjoying it with the whole of his being. Fits of exultation came over him of such fierceness that he had the desire to run and leap and jump; he startled his mother with wild rhapsodic outbursts of singing and with silly practical jokes. "What has come over you, Paul?" she asked him one day, when energy bubbling up within him demanding an outlet, he had pounded the piano triumphantly until the china in the house rattled on the shelves. She looked at him, puzzled, uneasy; she had known these wild rushes of ecstasy and delight herself. Because of one of them he had been born to her. 

Paul shrugged his elegant shoulders and smiled. "Don't frown," he commanded. He loved his mother when she was not worrying about the future or how long

NEW BED 

Bill would continue her allowance, for the money her brother left her was nearly used up, and though Bill had never said anything about stopping it, she was fearful. 

When her face was placid and serene some of her former beauty returned, and her tall plump figure gained graciousness and poise. It was then Paul loved to sit and talk to her and make absurd plans for the future and tease her about having such a clever, handsome son.

She relaxed her tense frown at his words and put a hand on his hair. "I can't help worrying about you. You're all I've got." "It's all right, Mother. I'm enjoying myself just nowwork and everything. You shouldn't feed me so well, then

Paul pulled himself up. The conversation was becoming painful to him; it would have been so delightful to have poured out his tales of Ann into sympathetic ears, yet he felt he must restrain himself and deceive. He knew how deep was his mother's intuition about himself, and he dreaded it. She loved him so much that he could not bear to hurt her. "I've seen her a time or two. She's very pretty and gaynot like the girls here. Of course, she's only a child, a terribly simple and earnest child sometimes," he answered. His mother made no reply, and her hand slipped from his head and clutched his shoulder. She knew Paul had fallen in love with Ann; his eyes, his voice and manner told her. She saw his life unhappy and thwarted through love, as her own had been. The things she had been told in her early life by an evangelical father and mother at Cheet Barn came back to her. Paul was to be punished for her lapse from virtue by having the same misfortune in love as she had had. The sins of the fathers. .. Hadn't she often been made to read that text on Sunday when she had wanted to play in the barn with her brother? She thought she had paid penance with the humiliations and sufferings of Paul's childhood days, and of late had almost rejoiced at the way life had treated her because it had drawn Paul to her in an intimacy she might never have known if he had had a father and an ordinary family life. Now she saw Jehovah had only been sleeping, and was awakening again to show his displeasure. What could she do? What could she say? Would prayers avail? She remembered the prayers and bitter tears of her girlhood. They had not brought back her lover or softened her father's heart. Those years of suffering in her most impressionable years had made her a fatalist. She would have worn out her knees with prayer

NEW BED 

and her eyes with supplicating tears if she thought it would help Paul, but she had no faith. She could not see how Ann could help falling in love with him-his intense emotions, his dark eyes burning with enthusiasms, his handsome face and figure and, above all, his fiddle, were a sure way to the heart of a young, romantically-minded girl. She knew Bill's tenacity, a family trait, and Bill's ruthlessness. He would hold what was his and never let it go. If she could only warn Paul-but Paul, like herself, had never listened to warnings. It would probably do more harm than good and make him aware of things which perhaps had never entered his head. She bent and kissed his cheek, and against her will the words came out: "Be careful, darling."

Paul bent over the piano and drowned her departure in a brilliant variation of an old song she used to sing to amuse him when he was a small boy and had so excited himself over his games he could not go to sleep. "As long as I remember I never shall forget,

To take my um-ber-ella when it turns out wet." 

Her words made him uneasy when he thought of them later on. He felt that she knew something of what he had been doing these last few weeks and that she was afraid. "If only I had met Ann first," he said to himself, and was startled into a panic at the thought. He was suddenly aware of complications and distress ahead, not in any definite form, but in vague ominous warning. He had the feeling of having received a shock and that his life had advanced a step forward. Ann and he had been so happy together, taking the pleasure of each day as it came and submitting without thought to the circumstances which made them part each

NEW BED 

151 afternoon, their only uneasiness knowing they deceived Bill by not telling him that they spent the days together, but satisfying themselves by saying it made no real difference to him; that, indeed, he benefited because of Ann's renewed cheerfulness. 

A terrible fear came over Paul. What would happen if 

Ann were ill and could not come out? What would they do if Bill suddenly decided to stay at home? What would happen when the winter was over and the orchestra disbanded until the next autumn? He would have to seek a fresh job for the summer, and it would probably mean going to a pleasure resort, because the talkies had caused a glut of violinists in the city, and most of them were experienced players who would naturally come before himself. 

He was haggard and forlorn next morning when he met Ann, his fears had so tormented him all night. Ann was sensitive to the change at once. A chill came over her eagerness and joy as soon as she saw him. "What should we do if one of us were ill? We shouldn't be able to see one another. Or suppose Bill decided to give up work and stay at home?" he asked.

Their eyes met, startled, as each acknowledged the fear. 

Their hands met also and remained clasped together. They seldom embraced more than this. They had not felt the need, for their eyes caressed one another, and just to walk side by side and talk had been enough; but now Paul put his arm round her and kissed her cheek awkwardly, desperately, as if he were ashamed. With a swift movement Ann rested her cheek on his. "What should we do?" she murmured. Her tone changed almost comically. "I know what I should do if you were ill; I should come and see you, and nobody should stop me," she said emphatically.

NEW BED 

"And I should come and see you. I should force my way in if I wasn't allowed to come. I'd knock the door down. 

I'd trample on anyone who stopped me." 

Ann smiled at him triumphantly. "Of course," she said. 

Then she laughed quickly as the idea came to her. "What sillies we are, frightening one another for no reason at all. We are not ill, and everything's just as it was yesterday." 

Paul was still serious. "It is not the same as yesterday. I love you more to-day. 

I shall go on loving you more every day until I'm just nothing but an aching for you. I know. I feel it coming."

Ann looked at him shyly, and was overwhelmed by the intensity of a gaze full of glowing rapture and deep melancholy. "It's like that with me," she whispered. "I'm getting so that nothing else matters in the world but you.' 

He kissed her cheek again, with more assurance this time; and as she turned her face to look at him he kissed her lips and, finding sweetness in them and a drowning pleasure, kissed her again and again. "It's a kind of pledge we'll never forsake one another," he said as they broke apart. "We are never going to be happy again," thought Ann, the quickening of her emotions, the sense of being more spirit than body giving her a prophetic insight into the future. She forgot her prophecy instantly, borne on by the sweetness and the immense possibilities of his words. "I'd fight the whole world and be glad," she answered proudly, and wondered why her ecstasy should be so much nearer pain than joy.

After this confession of love they entered upon days lived at high tension; days of joy and days of apprehensions as vivid as the delights. The experiences of each day as it

NEW BED 

153 passed, however small and insignificant, sharply etched themselves upon Ann's mind and senses; colour, sound, emotions, understanding were heightened and intensified. 

She was amazed when she considered her former life and remembered how she had lived complacent, with half her wits and emotions sleeping. She discovered within herself unexpected powers to deceive and contrive, and after a few struggles her conscience succumbed and it became habit. 

Lie to Bill she could not; she learnt to evade, imply, even to ignore his continual questions as to what she did when he was in town. She spent so much less time in the house than before that when she was at home she worked at high pressure, and there were times when she had to cover up the deficiencies of her housekeeping with subterfuge. Never before had the days been so full. Time became her enemy, for the hours sped by quickly, and it seemed that as soon as she and Paul would meet they must part again. At home in the evenings she was still and subdued, her restlessness appeased by the adventures of the day. There were times when the desire to tell Bill of something which had happened, some small amusing incident perhaps, or one of Paul's witty sallies, would be almost unbearable. She would lean forward and put her hand on his arm to make him look at her, and then, seeing the tender, possessive look in his eyes, she would grow afraid and stifle the confidence. 

Her conscience, ignored all day, took its revenge at night. 

She would waken in panic, her heart thumping, her throat dry, from dreams which left her worn and exhausted. Sometimes she would find herself sobbing, her face wet with tears, the horrors which had caused her distress already fading away. One night, after stormy, broken dreams in which she renounced Paul for ever, she found herself shaken into wakefulness by Bill. She was aware through the heaviness of sleep that Bill was not treating her as gently as

he usually did. She rubbed her arms ruefully where he had held them. "What's the matter with you?" he said roughly.

NEW BED 155 mance, a source of strain and irritation. She wished she could tell Bill what she did and how she spent her days. 

But could she tell him truthfully that her love for Paul was that of a friend and sister only? Could such mad wild joy, such complete oblivion of the world when she was with him, such intimacy of mind to be a sister's love only? What was it Paul was so fond of quoting?
