Secretly, that was it, or the world would take them away from each other, or, what was perhaps worse, spoil their joy in love with its rough, coarse hands.

There was in Bill such a stubborn lack of imagination where she was concerned that she was obliged to admit Paul was right. Bill would take this love and wipe it out brutally, leaving them nothing but sore hearts and wrenched and battered memories. He would see it as treason against himself and as wicked, idle philandering, instead of something which made the days glad, enriched her life, and made her happy to devote herself to him in the evenings and weekends without any of her former heartburnings and discontents. When she was with Paul she felt young again; not that they did not talk seriously for hours with an earnestness that is youth's own, but essentially they were gay, and laughter flowed. Cheet Barn was a solemn house; laughter did not come naturally there. Paul's mother, before she fell in love, was the last merry soul to live in it. Bill had no capacity for thoughtless laughter. More and more, it seemed to Ann, unconsciously contrasting him with Paul, whose volatile spirit changed several times a day, he was growing ponderous and middle-aged, positive and dogmatic in his opinions. His heavy face, unlightened by humour,

NEW BED made her timid when she would have argued with him. 

Her own ideas of right and wrong were fluid, whereas Bill's were solidly fixed; she knew only pity and desperate sadness for those whom the world declared wrong, for she had found out now what a cheat life could be, and how it trapped the young and ignorant into actions which could ruin their later life when they had become wise. 

Her friendship with Paul could be interpreted as wicked and deceitful, but she had no sense of guilt. She was tortured and unhappy at times because she must deceive Bill, but she did not feel she was doing anything wrong. Shorn of its secrecy, it was an innocent boy and girl love affair, romanticised and heightened into beauty because Paul was a musician and emotion was his daily food, and because both had a strong sense of the sadness and loneliness of their position. They walked the lanes and by-ways, or in wet weather sat in cafés, choosing those that were tucked away in cellars or hidden in odd corners, talking all the time, satisfied with a fugitive kiss or a tight clasp of the hand. The world did not exist for them when they were together, and its indifference, which they had bemoaned before they met, became a matter of congratulation. 

Paul talked a good deal about his work. He had his heroes, a modern composer or two, a world-famed violinist he had met abroad, and at whose feet he sat. "My master," 

Paul called him, when he spoke of him to Ann, as if he rejoiced to use the name. One day Paul brought Ann an emerald-green silk handkerchief carefully folded and wrapped in tissue paper. "See this? It was at rehearsal once-I shouldn't have been there at all, but one of the second fiddles was taken suddenly ill and I took his place—and this handkerchief was in my breast pocket; you know, sticking up a bit to show the colour. The Master had come without his pad

NEW BED 

157 to tuck under his chin. He looked all round him, frowning, then he made a dart to where I was sitting and pulled out this green handkerchief. 'Ah! this will do nicely,' he said, and his eyes twinkled at me. 'Very pretty.' I was in such a state of excitement I could hardly play. After the rehearsal, when I was putting my fiddle away, he came to me with the handkerchief carefully folded up like this. 

I begged him to do me the honour of keeping it, but he refused. 'Perhaps a lady gave it to you, and she would be cross,' he said, laughing like mad at his own joke. All the other chaps tittered, but I didn't care. I could have gone down on my knees to him. I never dared hope when I went to the rehearsal that he would speak to me. To be on the same platform was enough." 

Ann looked at the handkerchief reverently. "Is he very wonderful?" she asked. "If I could play like him I don't think I could bear it,” answered Paul solemnly. "You will one day," assured Ann.

This was heresy to Paul. He shook his head seriously. "Next time he gives a concert in England somehow you must come with me. I'd like to take you to lots of things, Ann. You've no idea how wonderful it is. You realise then nothing else matters. All the silly little things-you worrying about Cheet Barn, or me worrying about my chances as first fiddle, just fade away into nothingness."

When Paul was deeply moved the blood drained from his face, leaving it pale and transparent, so that it seemed only spirit that glowed through his skin. Ann was filled. with tenderness for him. "Darling Paul!" she murmured. "Do I fade away also?" she teased. "No! You are part of it now. Wouldn't it be wonderful if we hadn't to leave one another any more? If you could

NEW BED 

come abroad with me and we could wander about going to concerts and operas and plays, and perhaps Bordsky would take me as his pupil, and then in time let me play in his quartet, and we would be famous and I could give Mother enough to make her independent and we need never come to Cheet any more." 

Ann began to tremble. "Oh, Paul. What about Bill, and the Barn and everything?" 

Paul laughed quickly. "It's a fairy tale," he cried. "It's rather nice pretending, though?" "It's a lovely pretending," said Ann energetically. “As long as it isn't serious," she added in a softer tone. "How can pretending be serious?" mocked Paul. "I almost wish it could be," answered Ann wistfully.

There was silence between them for some moments; then Paul began to talk vigorously about another matter. 

The conversation lingered in their minds and made a faint barrier between them for the rest of the day, for they both wanted to go back to the theme yet were afraid, and it was the first time they had not been completely frank with one another and spoken out their hearts.

CHAPTER XVI 

It was inevitable that they should be seen. A friend of 

Maude's who knew Ann by sight, though Ann did not know her, saw them in a tea-shop. It happened that she patronised the tea-shop several times, and each time Ann and Paul were sitting at the same corner table engrossed in each other, and the light in Ann's eyes, the devotion in Paul's leaning figure was unmistakable. Their behaviour was quiet and modest; but that they were lovers was as obvious as anything could be. 

Miss Mayflower was a tolerant and for the most part kindly woman, whose eyes regularly watered at the sentimental rose-lighted ditties in the best musical comedies, and whose heart strings responded to the throbbing love songs of the talkies, and whose favourite fiction consisted of stories of love thwarted and finally triumphant; but now, faced by a love scene in real life, she bridled and was affronted. Their absorption in each other seemed to make her own solitariness conspicuous, and a waitress uninterested in a middle-aged spinster's fussiness about hot water and the amount of butter on toasted buns completed 

Miss Mayflower's sense of injury. Ann became a minx, an idle married woman masquerading as a young girl, leading a handsome young man astray.

Miss Mayflower had often said hard things in confabulations with her colleagues about the Idle Married Woman with no children. Such women sapped the emancipation of woman movement at its very heart; they were little better than women who sold their bodies. For them bridge 159

NEW BED parties, tea dances and such-like ways of passing the time existed, with the attendant evils of gigolos and gambling, while their husbands worked to supply them with pretty clothes and pocket-money. Secretly excited, Miss Mayflower pondered her discovery of the lovers, faint qualms of remorse pricking her as she furtively watched them and pretended to weigh up whether she should use her information or not. Maude Darwin was not popular, for her air of determinedly making the best of things and being bright was not always gratifying to her friends; it suggested too often that she was making the best of them and their endeavours. Miss Mayflower had suffered from this irritating quality in Maude, and she felt it would be an achievement to tell a story which would cloud the atmosphere of superiority Maude carried about with her like a protecting aura. The haughtiness of the waitress, the deficiency of butter on the toasted buns worried her no longer.

She rose quietly and departed, casting a long look at the lovers, which she imagined was the apotheosis of subtlety and of which they were quite unaware. She was filled with such an exhilarating sense of importance that she bumped into a pretty girl in a fur coat and a young man who were coming down the stairs together.

The young man put his arm about his companion solicitously; on Miss Mayflower, who had suffered most from the collision and whose hat had been knocked to the back of her head, for she had been charging forward head down, and whose toes had been trodden upon, he cast a look of dislike and annoyance. "Really, you might look where you are going," said the pretty girl tartly. "Another of them. Idle, dressed-up doll," said Miss

Mayflower savagely to herself, as she cast a withering glance at the couple and went her way.

NEW BED 

161 

On the following evening Betty appeared just as Ann was urging the kitchen fire to light by a handful of sticks liberally soaked in paraffin. Ann had missed the train she generally caught from town and was rushing desperately to make the house look as if it had been inhabited all day. "Where have you come from?" she asked. “I didn't see you on the train." Paul, in spite of her protests, always insisted upon taking her to the station, and Ann wondered if Betty had seen them together. "I biked it," answered Betty. "They don't know at home. They think I'm playing hockey, though I expect

Maude will find out. She always does when I tell lies. Her eyes and ears and nose are twice as sharp as those of any other person I know." "Oh, dear," said Ann, feeling, as always, helpless to check Betty's blunt speech. "You put the kettle on and the tablecloth while I go and take my things upstairs. I've been out and got back rather late." "There's no hurry," said Betty. "Bill's going round to see Mother, and I expect she'll make him stay to tea. He won't be able to catch his usual train." 

Ann looked puzzled. "He didn't tell me. He always does. How do you know? Is anything the matter. Is your mother ill?" "Maude sent him a note at work to call round if he could. She said it wasn't anything too important, but if he could spare a minute. . . . You know Maude's style, pretending Mother's a martyr because Bill married you." 

Ann nodded, then dismissed the subject. It was useless to reprimand Betty, who had a flair for truth where her sisters were concerned. "We'll have a cosy tea together then, shall we? What

NEW BED would you like? And you can tell me how you are going on." "It's not so bad at home now we live in town. The girls" -Betty always spoke of her sisters as "the girls," in a tone which would have made a listener who did not know them imagine they were much younger than Betty-"have more to do and less time to improve me. But I came to-night for a purpose," she said dramatically. 

Ann looked down with a kindly tolerant smile for

Betty's childish intensity. Ann was feeling older and wiser nowadays; the way she was manipulating life for her own purpose had given her a sense of power and self-assurance. 

Emotion had changed from simple black and white to a complex mixture of interchanging greys, and whereas when she first came to Cheet Betty's childishness had been not unlike her own, now she felt removed to another plane, where she could look down and smile at the simple pleasures and despairs of youth. "It sounds thrilling," she said lightly. "It's worse than that," muttered Betty, flushing dull red and poking the fire vigorously to hide her discomfort. 

Ann was feeling remote from Maude and her texts or the worried eyes of her mother-in-law; she had something hidden within her which transcended everything else in interest, and she could not imagine being at all moved by their affairs, for so she thought Betty's news must be. She ran upstairs humming, relieved Bill would be away another hour or so, and pleased to have Betty alone, for in the company of her family Betty could be silent almost to sullenness.

When she came downstairs again she could see Betty was offended because she had not been more curious about the news Betty held insecurely bottled up. "My dear, what's the matter?" she said gently.

"I was doing my homework in the kitchen and I went into the dining-room for a dictionary. Maude had gone out I got at Mother and made her tell me. I can always get things out of Mother if I keep on long enough. Maude is going to tell Bill to-night. A great reluctance to speak to Betty was upon her. It was as much as she could do to take her to the gate and bid her good-bye affectionately. She was afraid to praise Betty for her loyalty, because it was admitting herself in the wrong with Paul and might give Betty the air of an accomplice; she did not want to let anyone into their secret; it cheapened it, reduced it to a commonplace love affair of the kind 

Charlie Dibbs understood and sniggered at.

NEW BED "I couldn't help coming," whispered Betty, as if she guessed Ann's predicament. Don't bother your head about it, my dear," said Ann at last, with a bright smile and a firmness she did not feel.

She was relieved when Betty disappeared down the lane and she was alone and need no longer disguise her feelings. 

The agitation within her would not let her sit down and think quietly. She moved about the house doing odd jobs she had neglected, picking things up and putting them down again as she wrestled with her apprehension. When everything was neat, the table laid, the fire burning brightly, she sat down. The thought of Maude and her sister turning over the gossip, expressing their sorrow for Bill, sickened her. They would make so much out of little. Why couldn't people mind their own business, she asked herself fiercely, clenching and unclenching her fists. What would 

Bill say? He would trust her no longer now. She must give up 

Paul. To-morrow she would tell him. She owed her first loyalty to Bill, who had rescued her from Charlie Dibbs and "Ye Old Thatched Inn," and who loved her so tenderly. Her thoughts went backwards and forwards. She formed tremendous plans for the reorganisation of her whole life, which faded nebulously as other plans came into her mind, which in their turn faded out, leaving her weak and undecided, the only definite emotion in her mind an acute apprehension of how Bill would behave when he returned home.

She was knitting before the fire when he came in, rocking herself in the old rocking-chair in which many Darwin

NEW BED 165 children had been rocked to sleep. The knitting provided an occupation for her fingers which they sorely needed if they were not to show the state of nervous excitement which possessed her.

Bill looked grim and cheerless when he came in. "You are late," Ann said, getting up and bustling with plates and dishes. "I called in to see Mother. I don't want anything to eat," he said. "You didn't tell me this morning you were going," said

Ann, not sorry to have just cause for reproach. "I didn't know. Maude sent me a note." "Is anything the matter? Is your mother ill?" "No . . . Only Maude's fussiness. I told her to mind her own business and not meddle in my affairs." "What has Maude done this time?" asked Ann, trying to speak lightly, but with her heart thumping at her ribs as though it would jump out. She was painfully aware of the insincere tone of her voice.

Bill did not answer, and the gloom on his brows did not relax. Because the anxiety within her would not let her sit still and because of a faint hope that a meal might soften 

Bill's frown Ann hovered about the table. "Couldn't you eat a sausage? They are all hot and brown. I'll make some coffee," she coaxed, and was busily domestic.

The kitchen was warm and cosy with the curtains shutting out the damp mists over the fields and the fire burning brightly, its light reflected from a hundred polished surfaces in the room and the aroma of freshly made coffee coming from the pot on the hob. Ann took the hot plates out of the oven and put them on the table. "Come on," she said. "I waited for you."

NEW BED

Bill took his place at the table opposite to her. He ate in silence, stolidly, pushing up his cup to be refilled without a word. "I suppose this is what it is like day after day when married folk don't get on together," thought Ann, and was appalled. She tried to think of something to say, but her brain was numb. Bill broke the silence as he got up from the table to light his pipe. "I believe the art gallery is going to buy The Nymph. I was asked, unofficially of course, what was the lowest I would take for it."

Ann's eyes went large with surprise and delight. "Oh, Bill! How lovely! How perfectly wonderful. 

Aren't you the gladdest person in the world? Why on earth didn't you tell me before instead of looking miserable." She went round to him and flung an arm round his neck and hugged him enthusiastically. He pulled her on to his knee and held her tightly. "Are you glad?" he asked, looking at her searchingly, the grim lines of his mouth relaxing at her evident delight. 

Ann hugged him again. "Glad! It's the loveliest thing that could have happened. I'm half mad with joy."

She was filled with a bitter remorse and self-hatred that on such a day, when his heart's desire had been granted him, it should have been spoiled because of her. She knew how strong had been his longing for some official recognition of his work, though he had been reticent about it. "Oh, I'm so excited," said Ann, settling herself in the rocker again and rocking excitedly to and fro. Her animation was infectious. Bill smiled and gloom dropped from him. He became unusually loquacious. "I shall be sorry to lose The Nymph. I'm very fond of it. There are other pieces I shouldn't mind if I never saw again, but The Nymph is different. 167 any other thing in the same way. When they are finished 

I usually lose interest in them." "But you can do others," consoled Ann.

Ann looked at him in terror; her apprehensions returned. "But why . . ." she faltered. "You've grown," answered Bill. "You're developing. 

When you first came to Cheet you were a young girl, unformed. I have watched you changing, contours filling out, a gradual, lovely change. Haven't you felt it yourself?" 

Ann nodded shyly. "You are mine just as a plant is mine that I have tended from a seedling. I have a right to you. No one else has such a right as I." 

Ann kept her face hidden behind her hair and hand. "I'm sorry I'm growing up," she said, after a long silence. "It was much jollier before. I don't think one is ever happy grown-up. There are too many things pulling different ways. . . . It is easiest to be young and singlehearted." 

Bill did not mention the reason he visited his mother.

As the evening went by, and under the influence of Ann's delight at his good news, his gloom lightened more and more. Ann wondered if Maude had told him her tale, and then, remembering his grim expression when he first came in, she could not hope that he had refused to listen or that Maude had been frightened off before her story was told. 

Ann did not know how to find out what had been said, and the anxiety affected her with a restlessness it was hard to conceal. When they were in bed she tossed from side to side, unable to sleep. Once a great sigh escaped her, and then she discovered that Bill was awake also, though he had been lying so still that she had thought him sound asleep.

NEW BED 

"What is the matter?" he asked. "Your news, I think. It has excited me," answered Ann. "Does it make you sigh as if you had all the cares of the world on your shoulders," he asked sharply. "Why don't you go to sleep yourself?" retorted Ann fretfully. "Oh, to be alone that I might sigh my heart out," she said to herself, oppressed by the feeling that Bill was watching and listening to her all the time. She remembered a saying of her mother's which had often irritated her in the past. "One has to pay for everything one gets in this world." 

Well, this was how she must pay for her love of Paul and his love for her. From now onward was it going to be a harassed, hunted thing? 

It was morning before she fell asleep. She awoke heavyeyed and depressed. To be in time to meet Paul she must scamper through her household jobs, but this morning there was no lightness in her limbs or mood to aid her. The thought of Paul waiting at the station and of his bitter disappointment when she was not in the usual train, filled her with a dull misery, but gave no impetus to her movements. 

She must not go, she told herself, and pottered about the house unhappily, beginning first one job, then another, and conscious of the futility of them all. She looked at the clock. There was still time if she hurried to change her frock and catch the train. She stood and watched the minute hand of the clock for some time as though mesmerised. 

There was time yet if she did not change, but flung on her hat and coat and ran her swiftest down the lane. She watched the clock tick away another five minutes. It was too late. No matter how she ran she could not get to the station in time. There was a lull now of nearly two hours in the train service to town. Two burning tears came into 

Ann's eyes as she thought of Paul ignorant as yet of her defection.

NEW BED 

169 She strolled desolately to the front gate. The lane was wrapped in morning quiet; mists were lifting from the fields and a few last leaves crisp and yellow floated down. and rustled along to the drift under the hedge. A party of tits in the laburnum trees called sharply as they jerked their way from twig to twig, and a robin clicked scoldingly on the holly-hedge, drooping his wings. In the distance a train whistled as it neared Cheet station. The two burning tears ran down her cheeks and two others took their place, so that she saw the landscape for a moment as through a prism. She stood for some time with no thoughts in her mind, conscious only of an aching misery and unhappiness. 

What a mess she had made of her life? What did it mean? 

What did anything mean? Why couldn't she be content to live with Bill and do whatever he wanted, sinking her own longings and desires to make him happy? There was a kind of voluptuous sadness in the idea which relieved her eyes of smarting tears and sent cooler, easier ones to soothe her.

An hour after, when Paul came knocking at the door, her eyes were red, but her burden of woe had lightened. 

Paul's face was white and drawn, his eyes strained, and his voice husky with anxiety. "Are you alone?" he asked when she opened the door and pulled him into the house. He began at once twisting the brim of his hat round and round with his fingers agitatedly. "I had to come. When the train came in and you were not in it I nearly went mad. I imagined you ill, dying, run over -a hundred terrible things. I knew it must be something important that kept you from coming. Oh, Ann, I've been nearly dead with fear in the train. I didn't know I had such an imagination. Thank God nothing has happened to you."

Ann pushed him into a chair and took his hat away from him, and gave him her hand to hold instead.

NEW BED "It's all right, darling. Do calm yourself," she begged, and smoothing back the hair from his forehead felt it clammy with perspiration. She took out her handkerchief and wiped his brow tenderly. "Don't do that, Ann. Kiss me instead," said Paul sharply. "Oh, Paul,” she whispered, "what shall I do?" and was overcome by a love so compelling that she could say no more, only creep closely to him and stay there, finding a consolation no words could give. "Why didn't you come? What happened?" asked Paul, when they could talk calmly again. “I might have missed the train," suggested Ann weakly. 

Paul shook his head. "One doesn't miss the train when one is in love," he said decisively.

Ann admitted the truth of this. "It's Bill. Someone has seen us. Maude has been making trouble. I felt I couldn't go on hurting Bill. It's so mean. The art gallery has taken 

The Nymph and just when he should have been rejoicing I spoiled it all. Oh, I don't know. Whatever we do someone is going to be hurt," she cried helplessly. "We shall never be happy any more.” "You don't love me. You don't care what happens to me as long as Bill doesn't suffer. You can't expect love to be easy. It's a terrible, devastating emotion. When you didn't come I suffered all the torments of hell. Take all the great love stories of the world. Are they happy ones? Do they not tell of pain and unhappiness as well as joy?" "You frighten me," whispered Ann, thrilled as well as alarmed by Paul's wild eyes and the burning intonation in his voice.

His rhetorical gestures collapsed as violently as they had begun. "It's the frustration. I get maddened by its senselessness and the strength of the others. Forgive me," he said, and

171 smiled at her, looking more like a boy again and less of the haunted poet. "Let's try and think quietly. All I am doing -my music, working for the future-is for you and because of you. Mother's wonderful, but she doesn't understand like you do. She hasn't your sweetness. Because people were cruel to her when I was born she's frightened. She hasn't any faith in life like you have, and she never laughs. Now just think. . . . What would it be like in this place without one another? Can you imagine it?”

NEW BED all the wrong things for their children. I don't remember my father, but I imagine he would have understood. He was a poet and romantic." "There isn't anyone we could go to," said Paul. "It's just us two alone."

His words were like notes plucked on his fiddle-vibrant, shivering on the air. "We've got one another, whatever happens,” cried Ann, and flung her arms around him, unable to bear the sound of her own words.

They had never embraced passionately before. A few kisses, a swift caress of cheek touching cheek, hands moving towards each other involuntarily had satisfied them. Their love had not fed on caresses, but on soft glances and selfexplainings; the happenings of the last few hours had taken away the first bloom of innocence and made them aware that they were lovers, and what was more, illicit lovers. 

What had begun as little more than an escapade could no longer be controlled. But even now they were afraid to take the plunge. "Mother's so decent," said Paul, almost as if he wished she wasn't. "She only thinks about my work and future. 

She's got no one else." "I couldn't leave Bill. I practically asked him to take me away from Charlie Dibbs. Gratitude alone should keep me.

He hasn't done anything to be treated so . . . I wish we could make them understand. If we explained, surely they'd be kind?"

Paul looked at her and his dark eyes were pitiful with disillusion. "They'd never understand in fifty years. Never!" 

Ann knew that he was right.

CHAPTER XVII 

THERE was a change in the attitude of the lovers after this. 

They plotted and planned deliberately to keep their meetings secret, went warily when they were together and learned to disguise their emotions when they were apart, guarding their eyes and their lips. To leave one another as the afternoons grew to an end became harder, and the hours when they could not see one another became time to be got through somehow; a feverishness crept into their companionship that it had not known before, due perhaps to the feeling at the back of their minds that they must gather their rosebuds while they could. All the lovers of history and legend became their companions. 

They found an unexpected haven in a music-shop in town, whose top rooms had been designed for practice rooms, but were rarely used as such. Paul and three other young members of the municipal orchestra who had formed themselves into a quartet shared one of these rooms for rehearsals. They had an arrangement whereby the room was left to each member of the quartet in turn for solitary practice, and on Paul's practice days Ann would come with him and sit and listen as he worked, or perhaps play the old Broadwood grand piano, whose thin, mellow tinklings had a romantic far-away tone in keeping with the dusty remoteness of the room. As the other young men were also in the habit of bringing friends to the practice room Ann's visits were not commented upon. Occasionally one of them would come in for a pipe left behind or a piece of music or just to find someone to talk with, but seeing Ann and Paul together, he would apologise and disappear rapidly. It was a 173

NEW BED 

point of honour between the four not to butt in when the other fellow was with a girl, or to ask awkward questions afterwards. 

It was not necessary to go through the music-shop to reach the practice rooms. There was a side entrance and a lift, in charge of a garrulous old soldier whose watery eyes twinkled as if he were constantly telling himself that boys will be boys, and he was quite prepared to wink at the rules of the place for a consideration. It was a rambling old building. Originally, before the town had expanded, it had been the dwelling-house of a rich merchant. The first and second floors were used as town headquarters by teachers of singing, and pianofortes, grey carpets and hangings and flimsy gilt chairs gave them an air of luxury; but the top floor was dusty and neglected, its corridors uncarpeted, coming alive only in the evening and early morning, when the cleaners came to fetch their buckets and mops which they kept in one of the rooms. Fergerson, the lift man, liked to sit in the lift on the top floor and smoke his pipe away from the turmoil of activity and bell-ringing down below. 

Fergerson was interested in the quartet. They did not complain of his smoking as the teachers of singing below were apt to do, or tell tales of the jugs of beer smuggled in to comfort his solitary meals, which he took in a cubby hole, a linen closet in remote times, which he had made his own. 

The young men joked with him and often supplied the means for beer and tobacco. Because of this he treated their friends with attention and a garrulous respect. He liked 

Ann because she listened to his tales sympathetically and was too shy to reprove him when he took it for granted that she and Paul were lovers. It was thrilling to slip in at the side-door of the shop on a dark winter afternoon and see Fergerson standing at the lift door with a smile of welcome lurking about his meagre ginger whiskers.

NEW BED 

175 "He's just gone up, Miss," he would say portentously, if they were alone; but if there was anyone else in the lift he would content himself with a significant nod and a jerk of his thumb to explain that Paul was already upstairs. As 

Fergerson clanged the lift gate behind her and descended again Paul would open the practice room door and look to see whom the lift had brought, and Ann would run into his arms with eyes shining and cheeks aglow. 

Though Paul was broadening out into manhood, his face was still thin and drawn, as if he were gradually consuming himself by the intense emotions he lived through every day. 

He could never sit still and contemplative as Ann would sit, dreaming with folded hands before the fire, but was constantly moving about. If not practising, he must walk or talk, and when he talked he beat with his hands and tossed his head and swayed his body to and fro. He was either exalted to the skies or buried in gloom so deep that it seemed he would never rise again. There were moods when his love for Ann was a shining wonder and delight, and moods when it was a pitiful frustrated thing defeated by a heartless world; there were days when just to know she was alive, to see her if only for a moment and without touching her was sufficient to content him, and there were others when to part from her was like tearing his heart out -a physical as well as a mental sensation of sickness and desolation. Ann knew these moods and many others; she knew which one dominated the moment she saw him. This knowledge made her older and wiser than Paul; it generally happened, though, that whatever the mood it passed and content took its place when they had been together a few moments. One of the miracles of their friendship was the way time was swallowed up when they were together. 

Apart, an hour could seem an eternity of boredom, but an hour together was gone before they realised it had begun.

NEW BED

It was a shock to find themselves back in the world again when they had parted. Sometimes when Ann had lingered as long as she dared she had to force herself back into ordinary life and to fight against a sense of remoteness which hung over Bill and Cheet Barn and her duties there. 

In spite of the anxieties of their love they were still gay and could laugh at irrelevant things, or go swinging along a country road, forgetting how slender was the thread on which their happiness hung and how at any moment it might be broken by a rough hand. On these days, when a parting kiss or a struggle of hands to separate were the only indications that they were more than jolly companions, Ann would feel no need to guard her eyes from 

Bill's suspicious scrutiny. Well-being flowed through her, and she would feel capable of coping with Bill's jealous imagination; but there were other times when she and Paul would sit raking their hearts and discussing Bill and Paul's mother, and then the desire to stay and share Paul's life became unbearable and the hopelessness of their position weighed her down. It was on these days, with her sensibilities worn thin and fine, that she suffered agonies of apprehension and saw significance in the slightest glance. It may have happened that she had forgotten to take her hat and coat upstairs, or had left her gloves and handbag and thin town shoes on the window seat, and she had noticed Bill looking at them curiously. She would imagine him wondering where she had been and why, reading from these signs that she had been to town against his wish, for when she went to the village to shop or went walking in the orchard she never used a handbag, and wore her old tweed coat with pockets, and brogue shoes. She would be annoyed with herself then for her carelessness and annoyed and ashamed by the necessity for deceit, and her nerves would suffer, making her jumpy and difficult.

NEW BED 177 

One evening Bill said casually: "You were in on Tuesday?" "Yes, I think so," lied Ann. "Most of the time, anyway. Why?" "Mother said she called, but couldn't get an answer, and when she went round to the kitchen door she found it locked." "Perhaps I was in the barn,” said Ann. "I should think she'd look there." "I may have been walking in the orchard. I often go rambling about in the afternoon when I take the ducks their food." "But it rained all the afternoon on Tuesday. It was a real soaker. You don't go walking in the orchard in the rain, do you?" 

Ann remembered Tuesday. The morning had promised well, so she and Paul had taken the train, and then it had turned to steady uncompromising rain and they had sat for hours in a country station waiting-room, waiting for a train back, laughing at times at the almost ludicrous inability of the rain to cease and lunching off bars of chocolate obtained from slot machines on the platform. They had been alone, the rain making a curtain which effectually cut them off from everyone; and if the waiting-room was dreary and forlorn they did not notice it, for they spent most of the time looking at each other. She smiled faintly, remembering the rain and the way they had laughed at it, though she was perturbed by the cross-examination. Bill's conversation so often turned into cross-examination nowadays, and then he would grow quiet and the lines round his mouth harden into grimness, and Ann would shudder inwardly as her imagination ran riot, wondering if she had betrayed herself. "I'm sorry I missed your mother on such a wet day.

NEW BED 

Why didn't she say she was coming, then I should have been prepared?" Ann said after a moment's thoughtfulness. "She told me she'd be coming for apples for Christmas mincemeat and I forgot to mention it," answered Bill, looking rather confused.

He didn't mean me to know. He was using it as a test to see if I had Paul here. The thought came to Ann swift as light, and she knew she read his embarrassment aright. 

Panic rose in her, and at the same time anger that they should so deceive one another. "I'm sorry I missed your mother. I hope she didn't get wet," she said coldly. "She went to Mrs. Gordon, who made her a cup of tea and then she went home.” "A cup of tea and gossip," thought Ann. Would Mrs.

Gordon tell how often Ann rushed her through her work and let her go early? "Many other things as well, I expect," 

Ann said to herself; and her panic returned. Bill's mother was not like Maude, though. She loved Ann and held her son in such respect that she probably would not dare to repeat village gossip to him. But mothers were marvellously wise where their son's wives were concerned, Ann knew, and with a few small facts insignificant in themselves could see a whole situation plain. 

Ann's cheeks burned with shame and dismay. She turned away from Bill and went upstairs and stood in the cold, dark bedroom. Mrs. Darwin wouldn't understand the nature of the love Paul and she had for each other, or how beautiful it was; she would see it crudely, like characters in the stories she read on Sundays in the Christian Herald- Ann as an unfaithful wife, Paul as a treacherous friend to one who had been kind to him and his mother. 

After this incident she was sure that Bill watched her, and that his intimate knowledge of her habits was helping

NEW BED 

179 him. She spoke to Paul about it, but because the signs were too vague to explain in detail, Paul accused her of suffering from a guilty conscience. "You imagine things, darling. You wouldn't believe how dense people really are and how slow to see things." "They may be slow to appreciate music and such like things, but they aren't slow about human nature," protested Ann. "You're growing afraid. And you promised you would never stop loving me." "I haven't broken my promise." "Well, if you still loved me you wouldn't take any notice of this silly talk of mothers and other busybodies. Let them say what they like. We can deny it. They haven't any evidence." "It isn't as easy as that, Paul; they don't want evidence." 

Paul raised his voice excitedly. "It's as easy as you make it. Tell me something concrete they have seen and said and 

I'll listen, but this vague stuff about suspicious looks and the like. It's your uneasy mind, darling Ann." 

Ann sighed. "Perhaps it is.. .. But sometimes I wonder how we are going on, and what will be the end of it. 

I get frightened."

Paul's belligerent mood changed to one that matched her own and he took both her hands and held them tightly. "Must we give one another up?" he said; "and let them win? Be cowards and have nothing at all but peace of mind?" "I don't think I'd have much peace of mind thinking about you," replied Ann slowly. "Nor I," confessed Paul. "Peace is a sterile thing. I glory in our love. It's flowing through me hot and strong, urging me to leap and run and work like mad. Because it is secret and frowned upon only makes it all the more glorious.

NEW BED Don't get moody and regretful. It is a crime to regret anything so lovely as you and me together."

Ann was carried away by his eloquence and his eager, springing figure leaning towards her. "I do love you so," she whispered.

That evening, as she sat dreaming by the fire, she went over the scene again. Bill was working at a panel for a new bed he was making; cupids with fat limbs holding garlands, and lambs leaping over them with ridiculous tottery legs and large heads. Ann liked it when Bill was working, because he became so absorbed in what he was doing that it was as good as being alone, and it was a luxury to sit alone and let her thoughts wander to Paul and the incidents of the day. Paul would suffer most if they were forced apart; he lived their love so intensely, almost violently; his emotion burned him up until there was little but glowing red-hot spirit left. If they had not been forced to intrigue and secrecy it might have been a calmer, less devastating thing, Ann felt; without the fever of parting constantly hanging over their meetings they need not be at boiling point all the time they were together. Paul thrived on this atmosphere, but there were times when Ann's exaltation flagged and she felt tired, her taut nerves craving the rest of normal life. 

She looked up to see Bill staring at her oddly. "What are you wrinkling your forehead over, Ann? You looked as if you were wrestling with the infinite."

Ann smiled and went to the table and took up the panel of wood on which he had been working. "What darling lambs," she said. "But why cupids, Bill?

What a pity they must have wings and quivers and belong to mythology. They'd make the jolliest babies." "Oh, it had to be cupids," Bill answered. "It's a nuptial bed."

NEW BED 

181 "Oh! But I thought you were making it for us," said 

Ann blankly. "So I am."

Ann blushed and gave a little nervous giggle without quite knowing why she did so. She covered up the giggle by saying flippantly: "Funny word, isn't it?"

Bill did not take any notice of the giggle, nor did he look at her hot cheeks. He picked up the wood again and a small tool. "I used to know Herrick's Epithalmie," he said slowly, picking his words. "Come and sit by me and I'll say as much of it as I can."

Ann perched on the table, swinging her legs. She loved 

Bill when he talked and worked. His face lost the expression of heavy seriousness which intimidated her, and became open and frank. The pleasure handling wood and tools gave him was reflected in a mild benevolent expression. "Now, now's the time; so oft by truth 

Promised she'd come to crown your youth. 

Then Faire ones, doe not wrong

Or let Love's fire goe out, By ling'ring thus in doubt: 

But learn, that Time once lost, Is ne'er redeemed by cost.

Bill stopped and applied himself to a tough corner.

NEW BED "Go on," cried Ann; "do go on." 

Bill stumbled over phrases. "There's some of it I can't say," he said. "They were free in their speech in those days."

He smiled at her, a smile Ann had never seen on his face before. It was rather like the smile of one of his own fauns. She blushed and looked away. After waiting a few moments and finding she did not ask for the censored verses, Bill continued his recital: "No fatal Owle the Bedstead keeps, With direful notes to fright your sleeps: 

No Furies, here about 

To put the Tapers out, Watch, or did make the bed; 'Tis Omen full of dread:

Within the chamber here, Then away; come Hymen guide, To the bed the bashfull bride. "That's all I can remember," he said cheerfully, and began sweeping up the litter he had made. Ann helped him, not because it was necessary, but because she did not know what to say. "Give me a kiss for entertaining you." 

Ann bent forward and kissed his cheek sketchily, then repented its meagreness and threw her arms round him and kissed one thick shaggy eyebrow fervently. He pulled her to him. "Do you know you never kiss me nowadays unless I ask you? And never on the lips.' "Oh, Bill, what a thing to say!" she murmured covering her guilty feelings by an assimilated indignation. She wriggled herself free from his arms and stood looking at

NEW BED 183 him, frowning slightly. "One can't kiss to order. It's got to be a spontaneous desire." "Then I take it you've no desire." "Why do you twist my words and cross-examine me like this," she cried, and burst into tears. "I'd like to live all alone," she sobbed, "and be able to kiss exactly when I wanted and be free and not married." 

Bill fondled her tenderly. "Don't say that, my darling; don't say that," he begged, with such pain in his voice that

Ann, for very pity's sake, took back her words and comforted him. She had never seen Bill in a panic before, and the strength of his emotion terrified her. 

The next morning he said, as if he had been thinking of her remarks about the cupids and wished to please her: "I'll put anything you like on the panel at the foot of the bed." "I don't quite know. Only not fauns or goats or cupids or nymphs or vine-leaves, because there are so many already in the house," said Ann. "What about ducks in the orchard?" suggested Bill. 

Ann smiled a little sadly, thinking how her delight in ducks and childish things had been overlaid of late by anxieties and embarrassments. She was moved by Bill's offer, because when he had planned out a piece of work he liked to keep to his plan. "Let's have the orchard with little apples on the trees, like it was when I first saw it. It would be something to look at when I wake early instead of the bacchanalian riot on the mantelpiece." "It shall be as you wish," agreed Bill. "I've been thinking, Ann, would you like to go to the Opera next week? I'll get tickets, shall I?" "Oh, Bill!" sighed Ann to herself, "don't be too good to me, or I can't bear it. Why can't you be indifferent? It would be better if you were cruel."

CHAPTER XVIII 

ON Christmas Eve Bill caught a chill cutting holly in a sleeting drizzle. He was better after a few days, but he seemed reluctant to admit it, and, aided by the doctor, who was a venerable gentleman, clever at reading his patients' desires, he did not hasten his return to work, but pottered about the barn, wearing a dressing-gown over his ordinary clothes to keep up the illusion that he was still an invalid, and working lustily on the new bed when he thought he was unobserved. 

Ann nursed him assiduously. It was something she could do for him at last; and she worked hard at it, so that the doctor congratulated Bill on his devoted and charming wife and exhorted her not to work so hard or she would be his next patient. Paul and Ann had managed to resign themselves to the inevitable separation that must come with the holidays. Bill's illness and the further separation it caused sent Paul frantic with loneliness and frustration.

Ann had no opportunity at first to let him know. Bill would hardly let her out of his sight, and when she said she wanted to go to the village to shop he questioned her with such persistency and provided so many good reasons why she should not go that she lost hope and submitted.

The weather did not help her, for it was cold and wet, with sharp gusts of north-west wind bringing icy rain. Ann was afraid to ask Mrs. Gordon to post a letter for her, nor did she feel it was wise to trust the tradesmen's boys who called at the house. They were local boys, and she regarded the 184

NEW BED 

185 village now as her enemy, and she knew she must give it no opportunity to talk.

She sensed that Bill watched her closely, calculated what she did and where she went when she was out of the bedroom, and knew without anything being said between them that he was conscious of her desire for half-an-hour's freedom. The first morning after the holidays came when she was to have met Paul, and she had been unable to send word to him. Bill had come downstairs after breakfast and Ann had made a big fire in the dining-room, because it was more shut away from draughts and because, also, it gave her the excuse of preparing the dinner to stay by herself in the kitchen. She was hoping that by some means she could send 

Paul a telegram. Mrs. Gordon was cleaning bedrooms upstairs, safely out of the way for an hour or two. As she was feverishly devising fantastic excuses for running into the village a knock came to the kitchen door, and Ann flew to open it. A gipsy woman stood there with an ingratiating smile on her lips, but not in her bold black eyes which searched the kitchen from top to bottom. She carried a basket on her arm and she had a baby slung in a shawl that was pinned across her breast. She was slatternly, coarse of feature, and her manner was impudent. She put one foot across the doorstep, so that Ann could not shut the door, and began a whining tale. Ordinarily Ann would have been rather scared, but there was only one thought in Ann's mind that morning: how to send Paul word she could not see him and how to prevent his coming to find out what was wrong when she did not appear at the meeting place. Paul suffered so terribly from apprehension that Ann could not bear to think of him waiting alone hour after hour.

The door leading to the passage was ajar. Ann shut it and took her purse out of the cupboard. "I don't want any lace,” she said to the woman, who was

NEW BED 

watching her closely, "but if you'll send a telegram for me I will give you a shilling. The post office is just along the lane about a quarter of a mile.” "I'm going that way now, lady," said the gipsy. "This is lovely lace, ma'am; trim a baby's gown or a nightdress. . ." She held up a knot of coarse lace with a leering smile that made Ann uncomfortable. "A nightgown, now, or a baby's petticoat, for it's a baby boy you'll be having later on .. "> "No, thank you," said Ann.

The gipsy's eyes were on the hob where the breakfast coffee-pot rested. "If you've got a drink for a poor woman that's travelling the cold world from morn to dark "" · · "Sit down a minute," said Ann in a flurry, "and drink a cup of coffee while I write the telegram."

The gipsy sat down and sipped her coffee and broke the bread and butter Ann gave to her into tiny pieces. "A drop of milk for the child," said the gipsy. Ann gave her a cup of milk. The gipsy did not sit on the edge of the chair like one on sufferance, but seemed to take possession of the kitchen, rocking herself to and fro in the rocker, her feet in battered boots stuck out from her bedraggled skirts to catch the warmth of the fire. Ann scribbled her telegram, one ear alert for sounds in the rest of the house.

The gipsy seemed in no hurry to depart. She rocked and sipped and fed the baby, while her eyes looked knowingly at Ann and at Ann's barely-concealed agitation. "Here is a shilling for the telegram and a shilling for yourself," said Ann, wishing desperately the woman would get up and go before Bill called or Mrs. Gordon came downstairs.

The gipsy rocked on. "It's hard when you have a little

NEW BED 187 one and no home, kind lady, and no man to give you shelter." "It is," said Ann. "Here is another sixpence for the baby."

The woman's smile broadened and grew evil and intimidating as Ann moved about the kitchen restlessly, not liking to ask the woman to go, yet frantic with anxiety. "The telegram's rather important. If you wouldn't mind," she began.

There was a noise of footsteps overhead. The gipsy got up and pulled the ragged shawl over the wizened face of the child. Except for the shawl, which only half covered her chest, she wore nothing but a dirty white bodice torn where she fed the baby and showing her skin. Outside the wind was thin and the sky grey with occasional falls of sleet that was ice-edged. Ann followed her to the door, but the gipsy was still loth to depart. "Have you got an old coat, lady, or a pair of shoes? 

There's many an old thing a pretty young lady like yourself would be finished with. . . ." She did not supplicate so much as threaten. "I haven't, I'm sorry," said Ann, already repenting that she had chosen such a messenger and only wanting now to be rid of her and the power of her spell. "You're hard, lady, and you with a man that'll buy you plenty and you deceiving him, and wouldn't give a poor woman that has to beg her bread from door to door ..."

Ann was nearly in tears with vexation and despair. Suppose Bill came out of the dining-room? The smile on the woman's face suggested that she would delight in tormenting her. Ann felt the gipsy knew everything—what was in the telegram and why she wanted it sent, although she had not looked at the paper Ann had given her, but had rolled it up and placed it in some corner of the rags which covered

her bosom. The baby, feeling the cold, began to whimper. 

The woman quieted it with a rough shake and an angry word. In a last desperate effort to get rid of her Ann took down a woollen scarf which was hanging behind the door. "Wrap him in that," she said. "And please go." She put out her hands as if she would push the woman off the step. "Thank you, lady," answered the gipsy, and did not put the scarf round the baby, but folded it neatly and put it under the cover of her basket. "The telegram," said Ann, but not very hopefully, for she could see the woman's face wolfish, hard, with a grin that, now it no longer had need to be ingratiating, was more like a snarl.

The woman nodded carelessly, as if she had already forgotten it, and went down the path, chiding the whimpering child. Ann went back into the kitchen, shut and locked the door and sat down on the fender before the fire and cried. 

A noise made her look up. Bill was coming across to her. "Who were you talking to for such a long time?" he asked suspiciously. "What's the matter?"

She saw him look at her purse and the paper and pencil she had left on the table, and wondered nervously what he would deduct from that. "A poor gipsy woman half-dressed with a baby. I gave her something, and then I couldn't get rid of her and she was rude," explained Ann hurriedly. "Why on earth didn't you call me or Mrs. Gordon?” "She kept going and then not going. It was awful," confessed Ann. "You mustn't let gipsies in the house. I thought you knew that, Ann. I've told you lots of times. You might be murdered. Suppose you had been alone and she wouldn't go? 

What would you have done?" "I know," said Ann helplessly. "Only don't go on at me.

Mrs. Gordon relieved the situation by suddenly bursting into the room. As Ann had not heard her steps down the passage it was plain she had been listening to their argument. "That's right, love," she purred, when Bill had retired into the dining-room looking depressed. "Men are proper bad-tempered when they're poorly, and just think what we women have to go through. Now, my husband, it was only last Tuesday—or was it Wednesday-no, it must have been

Ann had been thinking about the gipsy and feeling still more hopeless about the telegram. With a muttered excuse she went into the dining-room. Bill sat hunched-up over the fire, a frown on his brow and an expression of misery in his eyes. "I'm sorry, Bill. I didn't mean to scold," said Ann contritely. "That woman upset me. She was so heartless—evil, like a witch."

Bill continued to stare into the fire. "There are things in my mind which could send me mad," he said heavily.

Ann was afraid to ask what the things were. She pulled his hands from his head and held them in hers. "Don't say that," she pleaded. "You are not well. Don't you think you would be better in bed?" "I don't want to go to bed." "But if it makes you wretched . . .” "It's not that. "All right, darling." Ann's voice was soothing and patient, the voice of one talking to a sick child. She poked the fire, put a log on it, swept up the hearth, and fetched a cushion from another chair for his back. I can't leave it all to Mrs. 

Gordon. Would you rather I stayed with you?"

You are not going out, are you?" "Can I get you anything from the village?" Ann asked eagerly. "Is there anything you want?” "Oh, no! What could I want!" he answered testily. "This is dreadful-dreadful," Ann sighed to herself. "It's all over the house-suspicion, doubt, apprehension. 

Everyone's nerves taut, even Mrs. Gordon feels it, and it makes her talkative." She abandoned then the idea which had been in her mind since the failure with the gipsy of leaving Bill to himself in the afternoon and trying to slip up to town to see Paul and quieten his fears. She could imagine him counting the hours till the time came to meet her and then his disappointment when she did not arrive. After he had waited an hour, perhaps two hours, he would go

NEW BED 

191 round to the practice room, and still she would not come. 

This strain was the inevitable result of such a love as theirs; they paid dearly for the few hours of happiness they managed to steal. She knew Paul had a concert that evening, and if he were worried he would not play well. Paul had room only for one emotion at the time, and disappointment would have laid hold of him and remained in possession, colouring everything he did and saw. He had two solos tonight. He did not get many opportunities to play by himself, and if he were depressed he would lose his courage. 

She could only pray that pride in his work and a sense of the critical audience would win.

The rest of the day passed slowly. Whether it was because Ann no longer schemed and it was felt in the atmosphere, Bill recovered himself and was almost cheerful by bedtime. Ann was not aware of her long silences throughout the rest of the day. She was reluctant to go to bed; she did not know why, because she felt very tired and her head ached. Bill was heavy-eyed and yawning, but he refused to go without her, and having no excuse for staying downstairs, Ann went upstairs with him. Bill was soon in bed. 

Ann pottered about the bedroom pouring out medicine, in case he waked in the night, walking up and down, putting clothes away and finally sitting on the hearthrug before the fire to brush her hair. A fire in the bedroom was a luxury, and she lingered sleepily by its warmth. Bill grumbled from the bed for her to hurry up and turn out the light, so she turned it out and gave herself up to the glowing embers. Bill grunted with disgust and turned away to the wall, as if he would sleep. 

There was a great quietness everywhere. Then afar off came the sound of footsteps. They came nearer, passed the house, and went on. But they did not seem to go very far. 

Ann's heart gave a great jump that almost suffocated her.

NEW BED 

Was this what she had been unconsciously waiting for? She listened again and heard the footsteps returning. The sound was very faint; only ears sharpened by expectation and love could have heard them. She went quietly into the next the bathroom, and looked out of the window. A waning moon with a yellowish ring round it just made a stretch of lane visible but unreal. There was a dark figure in the road opposite the house, wearing, she imagined rather than saw, a wide-brimmed hat well over the eyes, his overcoat collar turned up, but showing a suggestion of white scarf beneath. Ann knew that form better than she knew her own, strange though it appeared in the grey watery moonlight. 

Paul! What was he doing there? He must have walked from town after the concert, for the last train went before ten o'clock. What agony of mind must he be enduring to send him all this way just to stand beneath their windows. 

She went back into the bedroom, her heart beating violently and holding her hands tightly clenched to keep them from trembling. Bill was a long mound on the bed, his face turned to the wall, only the back of his head visible. "Bill," she called softly. "I'm just going downstairs to get an aspirin."

He made no answer. She tiptoed out of the room and down the dark stairs into the kitchen.

The kitchen fire was still red and gave a faint glow. She listened for a moment, then carefully unlocked the door which led to the garden and drew the bolt and went out. 

The raw night air was like a blow in her face, but she paid no heed to it. She ran swiftly and carefully round the path to the front of the house, the wet grass whipping her ankles and moisture soaking through her bedroom slippers at every step she took, but she was burning with a great fire and oblivious of discomfort. Everywhere was very still, almost terrifyingly still, with strange shadows and unfamiliar

NEW BED 193 forms. She came to the front gate where the moonlight flooded the road and where Paul stood motionless. 

She was afraid to call to him, afraid her voice would not do her will and call softly enough. The night seemed enormous and the milky sky was a great sounding-board, ready to throw back with a million reverberations any sound she might make. Then with a faint cry Paul looked and saw her standing there at the gate. He stumbled across the road, inarticulate in his wonder at the miracle of her appearance, his eyes dark shadows, his face drawn and haggard and curiously young in the moonlight, which seemed to reveal. youthful contours the day concealed. "Ann, darling Ann, I willed you to come. I stood there willing you out of the house with all the force of my love," he whispered hoarsely, and in the fierceness of his joy held her shoulders so tightly that it bruised them. do Ann pulled him into the shadow of the holly hedge. "Bill's got a chill. That's why I didn't come. Now go; go, my darling." "I had to come. The concert. I played badly, Ann, and then I was ashamed and so miserable I couldn't go home."

She put her lips on his and silenced his hoarse whispers. 

They clung to one another, trembling with excitement. "You must go, my dear one. Bill will miss me." "Ann, Ann, how wonderful you are! How you understand me! It's all better now. I can go home and sleep. I've been half-crazed with disappointment."

NEW BED moment, then fled back into the house, bolted and locked the door and stood trembling all over. She tried to listen to sounds in the house, but she could hear nothing but her heart beating violently in her ears.

She went to the cupboard and fetched an aspirin and a spoon and walked slowly upstairs fighting desperately against sinking fear as she opened the door of the bedroom.

All was quiet within. The red embers of the fire were turning grey and cinders fell with little clinking sounds, and the warm smell of the room, a mixture of lavender and wood-smoke and camphor was almost painfully strong to her sharpened senses. Bill was still turned to the wall. 

He looked as if he had not moved. Ann undressed quickly, leaving her clothes in a heap on the hearthrug and stole quietly into bed beside him. She lay there stretched motionless until the wild beating of her heart died away and she fell asleep.

She was tremulous and heavy-eyed next morning, but Bill was more cheerful, his cold much better, and after breakfast he got out his drawing materials and was soon absorbed sketching the details for the panel at the foot of the bed. Ann was not restless, nor did she make any attempt to go out by herself, but busied herself in the house, turning out cupboards and baking cakes. "What do you think you said to me in the night?" asked

Bill one time when she went into the dining-room to see if he wanted anything. He was smiling at her in his old kindly fashion, but at his words Ann felt the floor rock beneath her feet. "What did I say?" she asked huskily. "I don't remember dreaming anything. Was I restless?" "No, not restless exactly. You just sat bolt upright and said loudly 'Fortune favours the brave,' as if you were scoring a great point; then turned over and when I asked

NEW BED 

195 you what you were talking about I saw you were fast asleep." 

Ann began to laugh. "What a funny thing. And I detest proverbs and people who quote them." "There's a good deal of solid homely truth in most of them," said Bill. "I shouldn't gibe at proverbs if I were you. The world's been brought up on them." "Words, words,” cried Ann. "And what do they matter? Who can tell what truth is, anyway; honesty, or faith?

It's all relative to be honest to one thing one must be dishonest to another," she went on wildly. 

Bill was displeased. "Honesty and truth are very real things, and so are dishonesty and untruth. You never used to talk such nonsense. Where have you been getting it all from?"

Ann chided herself inwardly for the outburst. Her words had been an echo of one of Paul's tirades which had stuck in her mind. For a moment or two she studied the drawings which were set out on the table. "Not from ducks," she answered quaintly at last, feeling that Bill was still waiting for an answer. "They are simple creatures and only concerned with realities." "The rules of life are simple," said Bill heavily, as if he would make them so, even if they weren't. "Yours may be," muttered Ann, and retired hastily on the excuse of burning cakes.

CHAPTER XIX 

In the night the wind moved round and the cold showers of sleet changed to a mild drizzle which seemed to smell of the spring. Ann thought she heard a thrush singing next morning when she went into the orchard to feed the ducks, and the pale wintry sunlight tempting her, she walked down the orchard to the brook, peering into tufts of primrose leaves to see if she could find a bud or two for Bill. 

Looking across the field to the spinney, she could see the dark shape of a bird on a topmost bare twig. "Nothing but the top of the tree would content him," thought Ann, and something in the wild notes of the thrush made her think of Boodle. How he would have loved a chase across the field on such a morning. How the newly-released scents from the earth would have sent him frantic with excitement. She could see him with his stumpy tail wagging and his tongue hanging out, rushing backwards and forwards, bounding over the grass. Nothing had been the same since 

Boodle died, she said to herself. The thrush's song had turned into a lamentation and she had no heart for further exploring. She hurried back to the house. Bill was standing by the kitchen door, waiting for her. "Where have you been?" he asked.

Ann gave him the two primrose buds she had gathered and two green leaves silvery with moisture. "I heard a thrush singing in the spinney." "Lift up your face. There are rain-drops on your cheeks. 

Is it very wet in the orchard?" 196

NEW BED 

197 Bill took out his pocket-handkerchief and Ann lifted up her face, but as he wiped the teardrops away two others formed and overflowed. "It's nothing-only Boodle, and the kind of day," she explained, wiping her face impatiently. “You should have let me come with you," scolded Bill, as if his presence must be the cure for all her unhappiness. 

Ann's heart sank still lower. How impossible it would be to tell him that she was not entirely his. She could never do it. "I like going by myself sometimes," she said stoutly. "And how could you come if you are ill?” "Wait a few days longer. Then we'll go for some walks. 

I'm not going back to work for a week or two." "Not even if you are better?" asked Ann surprised. "Oh, I shall not be fit for some days yet. Doctor seems to think I'd better stay at home a little longer." "Yes, I should. It will be a nice little holiday for you," said Ann sharply, and began to busy herself at the stove, leaving Bill looking at her with a bewildered expression on his face, as if he were not quite sure she had said the right thing.

After tea Ann began to write letters ostentatiously. "Mother will think I'm never going to write and thank her for the Christmas parcel," she said. "I simply must get it off to-night.' 

"But you don't want to go out in the dark to post it. Can't it wait until to-morrow?" grumbled Bill. "Oh, no. I shouldn't rest if it didn't go to-night," replied 

Ann earnestly. "I'll slip out and post it for you. I'll put my mac on top of my coat and a muffler," said Bill, getting up.

Ann looked at him with her eyes large with horror.

NEW BED "Bill! Are you mad? I've been nursing you and keeping you out of draughts. . . . You'll stay where you are," she said firmly. "Rubbish! It won't hurt me.' "Then there can't be much the matter with you," retorted 

Ann decidedly.

Bill looked rather uncomfortable and went back to his first argument, finding the other unsafe. "I don't see why it should go to-night. One day more or less won't make any difference." 

Ann went on with her writing, sealed up her letter and popped it into her pocket. "I won't be a minute," she said, and without waiting for further protest threw her coat round her shoulders and dashed out into the lane. She walked swiftly to the letterbox, but she hesitated a moment before she pushed the letter into the slot, gazing at the name written on the envelope. I wonder if he knew the letter was for Paul and that was why he made a fuss. How could he know? It's just my guilty conscience imagining things. He would keep me shut up, never away from him. Ann shuddered. How wicked when he is so kind and is only afraid I shall come to harm. 

Her thoughts went to and fro like this for the rest of the evening. Bill was working on the new bed. He looked displeased and barely spoke. Now she had her letter to Paul safely posted Ann was at ease. Paul would be frantic with longing to hear when they could meet again. He did not understand how difficult it was for her to do anything unperceived, without Bill questioning and commenting upon her actions or offering to help her. Paul's mother did not interest herself in Paul's every movement, or if she did, she kept her interest to herself and did not harass him. Paul did not really understand why Ann could not come out for an hour or two, even when Bill was at home.

NEW BED 

199 "Doesn't he think you ever want to go out by yourself? 

Are you a slave or a child? Are you living in a harem?" he would ask. 

That she could not go out to post a letter without resorting to subterfuge would seem incredible to him; in the abstract it did seem incredible; that was one of the reasons why she felt that Bill knew all about her love for Paul and the life they led together. Some instinct born of his love for her and of his strong sense of possession had told him these things, and so he strived with all his powers to circumvent their plans.

It was worse than a nightmare when Ann saw the situation thus; the three of them working underground, concealing their real thoughts, building up an atmosphere of doubt and distrust that was like a fog distorting their motives and desires. And then she would laugh at her fancies, attributing them to overwrought nerves and try and think calmly what was the best thing to do. Not that it was possible to come to a decision. When she was with Paul the joy they had in each other made her forget these things; but alone with Bill day after day with the sense of being watched all the time turned love into something distressing and took all gladness from it.

Bill's illness brought it home to her clearly how dependent upon circumstance was her life with Paul. They had no control over it. If Paul were ill she would be unable to go to him; if she were ill or prevented by some means from getting out she could not see him, though her heart ached and the longing was more than she could bear. This fact hardened her resolve not to miss any opportunity of being with him, though her conscience might reproach her and she must suffer agonies of shame. 

Bill's mother came to see them when she heard Bill was at home with a chill. She brought a basket of good things,

NEW BED mince pies, a rich fruit cake, a bottle of home-made wine, offering them in propitiation for her boldness in coming without an invitation. She was formal at first, conscientiously using the gentilities Maude had taught her were proper on a ceremonious visit. It was as if she had forgotten, or she wanted Ann to think she had forgotten that she had once been mistress of Cheet Barn. Ann was rather touched. 

The old lady could have had no doubt of her welcome. 

Ann petted her, brought out the best tea-cups, insisted upon her having the most comfortable chair and asked her advice about various household matters. In a little while Bill's mother lost her hesitating manner and became cheerful, indeed, almost arch, rocking herself to and fro in her favourite rocker and telling Ann tales about their new home, and the girls, and chapel, leading up to her most exciting piece of news, Pansy's "young man." Pansy, to her mother's great satisfaction, had found a "young man" almost as soon as she joined the new chapel. "Alfred's very musical and plays the banjo. Not comic songs, but accompaniments, rather like a guitar. Which reminds me, I saw young Paul when I was coming. He got in the same carriage," she went on. "He looks a poor thin scarecrow of a lad. I don't hold with music as a living. Why didn't his mother put him to a trade? Now, as Alfred plays, just as a hobby, it's very nice indeed, and so useful to be able to oblige at parties and socials." "Yes," agreed Ann. "Where was Paul going?" asked Bill, taking part in the conversation for the first time. "He said he was going to Tavor to see a friend. I told him 

I was coming to see you, but he wasn't very talkative." "I didn't know he had friends at Tavor," said Bill slowly.

Ann resisted the impulse to join in the discussion. She directed the conversation back to Alfred, fighting down an

201 hysterical giggle as she pictured him serenading Pansy with his banjo. "Does Maude approve of Alfred?" she asked. "The little games I had to get up to at first so they should have an hour together you wouldn't believe, and making everything look as if it happened that way. I did want our Pansy engaged."

Bill laughed, but Ann was sympathetic, enjoying the beaming smile on the old lady's face. She had not looked so satisfied by anything for many years. 

Ann looked at the clock and went to fetch Bill's medicine. "Do you know it's the last dose?" she said when she came downstairs. "I'm afraid I spilled some of it." "It doesn't matter. I don't want any more." "The doctor said you were to have another bottle.”

Doctor's medicine should be taken regularly." "Now then, Mother," remonstrated Bill impatiently, "how many times have I told you that I haven't the slightest faith in bottles of medicine?" NEW BED said Ann; "and if he waits until afternoon he will miss two doses. What do you think?" she said turning to Bill's mother, as if it were a question only a woman of her experience could answer. Bill began to expostulate, but Ann would not meet his eyes, and his grumbling voice alone had no power to intimidate her. She put on her old tweed coat and the selfsame beret she had worn that fateful Easter Monday hurrying to meet Miss Marble when she had found Bill instead.

She put the medicine bottle in her pocket and tried to walk out quietly though her legs ached to run swiftly along the road to Tavor, where Paul would be walking, praying to whatever gods look after the affairs of lovers to send her out to him.

She sped along the lane, grateful for the sense of space and the quiet cold air after the warmth and snug domesticity of Cheet Barn. She peered into the distance, but could see no dark figure advancing to meet her. She tried to reckon up the time it would take Paul to reach the cross-roads, some half a mile along the lane, but though her thoughts were as swift as her feet they would not go in order, but leapt hither and thither from one point to another with a brilliance that dazzled but did not make anything clear. A sense of escape and the romance of flying along the dim lane to meet her lover exhilarated her. Though she had made no appointment, had nothing more to go on than the casual words of Bill's mother, she did not doubt she would meet Paul. He could have been in that train for one reason only, and his going to the next station had been caution. Perhaps he hoped the old lady would tell Ann?

She came to the cross-roads and stood for a moment breathing hard and watching the traffic on the main road.

CHAPTER XX 

SOMETHING rich and rare, Bill had said, and Ann, unable to make up her mind which she preferred, had ordered the three bedspreads to be sent to Cheet Barn, so that Bill couid make the final choice. April sky. "That's good," said Bill, with immense seriousness, stepping back to survey the effect.

I imagine skies in Italy must be as blue and as lovely," sighed Ann, feasting her eyes on it. "Yes," agreed Bill. "All the same, a bed doesn't seem to me related to the heavens, but a piece of furniture belonging entirely to the earth." "Is it?" said Ann simply. "I have never thought so."

Ann was puzzled at the thought and interest he was expending on what seemed to her a simple domestic incident.

It might be explained as pride in his work, but he had made other things for the house and when they were finished had shown little interest in them and had not insisted upon decking them out and spending extravagant sums of money on accessories for them. 

Since the night his mother came he had changed. He no longer watched her or asked question after question about where she had been or what she was doing if she was out of his sight for an hour. When she arrived home after a day in town choosing the bedspreads he had held no close examination into her movements of the day as she had expected. A cheerful activity had taken the place of his tortured gloom and moroseness. He went round the house doing the odd jobs he had so often promised to do, even to putting plain panels of wood round the bedroom fireplace so that the goats and satyrs and vine-leaves went into retirement in a dusty corner of the barn. He was merry and joked and talked as he worked, as if an excitement possessed him which would not let him rest.

NEW BED 

211 "After a period of sluggishness and indecision convalescence is a fine feeling," he said to Ann one morning. "It makes you very energetic," she answered laughingly. "I'm sure you do not work so hard when you are well." "I badly wanted to finish the bed," he confessed; "but next week I must go back to work. Will you be just a little bit sorry, Ann?" In spite of her self-reproach she could not keep down her rising spirits. Some outlet they had to find, for she was reluctant to inflict them upon Bill. 

She went to the piano and pulled out the music she used to play at Miss Farthingdyke's school, dances and nursery rhymes and old singing games. One after another she played, with barely a pause between them, and when the candles on the piano guttered and went out she continued in the firelight, and gradually the twilight crept into the music and the rollicking tunes changed to the sad complainings of "Poor Mary Sits Aweeping," and "London Bridge is Broken Down."

Bill came in quietly and revived the dying fire with an armful of wood that crackled and sent fitful jerky points of light into the room. He pulled up an arm-chair and sat down with his feet to the blaze. Ann stopped. "Go on please," he begged. "Sing as well. Those tunes need the voice." "I have nearly come to an end of them," said Ann, running her fingers over the keys ruminatingly. Of their own accord they seemed to fall into a lullaby. Ann's voice, quavering at first, took up the croon:
