NEW BED 

213 Ann was rather dismayed at his abrupt tone after the tenderness which had gone before, but conversations with Bill had a way of ending unsatisfactorily, she thought sadly. Was there down at bottom any real harmony between them? Bill loved her; and love with Bill, she more than suspected, did not mean that he had any regard for her desires and opinions; he did not want her to have any which did not intimately concern him, and to be fair, what sympathy had she with the formalism which was the very essence of his work and mind. Sometimes it repelled her because it seemed artificial and belonged to an age that was dead. With Paul it was different. When she was with him there was no need to explain themselves; simultaneously both minds would leap in the same direction. Quite ordinary conversations became touched with glamour. Paul was volatile; he did not pronounce judgments which were to be regarded as irrevocable as Bill did; but one moment he would be passionate with enthusiasm or denunciation and the next he would be shaken with doubts, overwhelmed by the immensity of life's problems and the impossibility of solving them.

Monday came and Bill departed for town as usual. 

Tremulously Ann prepared to go out to meet Paul. The usual routine and habits having been broken by Bill's illness, she found herself curiously uneasy; she was afraid the fire she left might be dangerous, and a number of similar small things haunted her. "I must get back early. I want to be sure the house is warm and a hot meal ready when Bill returns. He is bound to find the first day back at work tiring," she said to Paul. "Always worrying about him. What about us? Isn't this our first real day together since before Christmas, and you talk of going early." 

Ann was contrite. "I know. But I must go. Come back

NEW BED with me to Cheet and we'll sit by the fire and talk. We can go through the spinney and up the orchard. I don't see why we shouldn't for once."

The afternoon turned frosty. The dusk came early and there was white mist over the fields. In the spinney the bare boughs and twigs were still, revealing unexpected angles against a pinkish sky, and the brook was silent, a thin silvery streak of ice covering its stones. As Ann and Paul walked through the orchard no bird moved or called; the ducks were sleeping in their shed; there was nothing but quiet trees and still grass blades. Ann and Paul stood for a moment by the barn. "I don't think I'll come in," said Paul. "I don't feel happy about it. Ann, wouldn't it be lovely if we lived together and we were just coming back after a walk? Just you and me. And I could sit with you by the fire until it was time to go to work. Perhaps I'd have a bit to play by myself this evening and I'd try it over and you would listen...." "Oh, Paul!” cried Ann, pressing tightly to him, "why couldn't it be like that?"

She did not ask him again to come in as she had meant to do. "Wait for me a moment. I'll just put the kettle on and look at the fire, then I'll walk back with you to the station."

He watched the light go up in the kitchen window, and after a few moments it was turned down again and flickering firelight took its place. It seemed to have gone much darker in the few moments she had been away. Rime on the garden glistened white, and the pink had gone out of the sky. In the distance lights were twinkling. "We'll go by the road. It is too dark for the spinney," she said. They walked slowly along, holding hands tightly for warmth as well as for love. Paul's words had made

NEW BED 

215 them both sad and they could find nothing to say to one another. They came to the end of the lane; a few yards farther on the lights of the village began. "Don't come any farther," whispered Paul. "Let's say good-bye here where I can kiss you."

There was such a weight of sadness in his voice that Ann could think of no way to comfort him but to hold up her lips. They embraced and their sadness became transmuted into the most exquisite melancholy. Footsteps passing on the other side of the road made them break apart and stand closer in the shadow of the hedge. They could see a dim figure hurrying along in the direction of Cheet Barn. "He never saw us. He was too busy getting home out of the cold," said Paul reassuringly. "It looks almost like Bill," murmured Ann, straining to see if she could identify the man who had passed them, but the mist had already swallowed him up. Apprehension was uppermost now, and they parted. "I'd like to write a song about leaving you, and the twilight, a love's lament," whispered Paul, as if he had made a great emotional discovery new to poets and composers.

Ann ran swiftly along the lane. Now Paul was gone she was conscious of the frost and the chilly mist. "It's much too early for Bill," she told herself. It made her purr like a kitten to come back from an afternoon's shopping and find hot toasted buns and tea awaiting her." "I'll make some toast," offered Bill, and pulling a stool to the fire, turned his back to her and was instantly absorbed in his task.

When at last he came downstairs he was excited but grave, and his eyes had a queer light in them and he did not stop looking at her for a moment. "Come and see," he said, and put his arm round her and led her to the bedroom. "I feel rather self-conscious about sleeping in it," confessed Ann. "Wouldn't you like it for yourself?"

Bill's arm tightened about her and he bent to look into her face with a look that was at once searching and supplicating. She struggled to release herself, but he did not let her go, and after a moment she relaxed and stayed within his arm.

NEW BED 

“Don't say that, my dear. Your place is by my side, whether in bed or out. Didn't you know that is a wife's duty?" There was a suggestion of joking in his manner, but his voice was stern. Ann did not care for it at all. "Is it?" she faltered uneasily, and tried again to break free. He let her go this time and stood smiling at her, his hands in his pockets and feet apart. There was a sense of power, of strength, and a consciousness of her weakness and fragility in his attitude. 

A desire to escape from his strange mood and to be alone came over Ann. She sought in her mind for a way in which it might be done. "I think I'll have a hot bath and go to bed. I'm shivery," she whispered, trembling. "You do, my pet. And I'll bring you a hot drink,” he answered in his old manner, and she was somewhat reassured.

The hot bath and the milk made her drowsy and she lay under the new coverlet and watched the firelight. "You look very pretty, Ann. I think I'll come to bed when I've locked up," he said.

Ann was disappointed. She had wanted to lie alone for a while in the quiet room and sort out her impressions of the evening and try and come to some explanation of its strangeness, but Bill had been so kind and it was unfair not to welcome him to his own new bed. "Yes, do.” Her voice was cordial, but sleepy. She turned over on her side and shut her eyes with a great show of going to sleep at once, to warn him that she did not want to talk; and before she could begin to go over the events of the day her simulation turned into reality and she fell asleep. 

She woke suddenly, startled to find herself held in a grip from which she could not escape. She fought feebly

NEW BED 219 battering her fists on his chest, and it was like battering a wall of iron. "Bill, Bill, you are hurting me. Let me go," she cried, and was astonished when he paid no heed, only held her more tightly and stopped her cries by putting his lips on hers. He had never resisted an appeal before. She forced her lips free and tried again. "Please, please, do let me go. You are hurting me. You are so strong," she moaned, but still he paid no heed and she thought that perhaps he had not heard her rightly and she cried out loudly. Then she began to grow afraid. Panic rose in her and a great fear. He did not mean to let her go. She tried to scream but he held her so tightly that the scream turned to a frightened squeal. Something had happened to Bill. He was changed into a man she did not know and over whom she had no power whatever. For a wild moment she wondered if, indeed, a stranger had crept into her bed, and the scream escaped this time and she fought wildly, frantic with terror; and as she felt how impotent her struggles were her terror increased and she began to cry brokenly. "Hush, hush, my pet," came Bill's voice, and his hand. caressed her and his grip relaxed a little. "You aren't afraid of me? You love me?" he whispered. "No-yes," sobbed Ann, and tried again to struggle free, but his hold tightened and she knew then he did not mean to let her go. She could not fight against him.

It came to her that behind his passion and desire was something calculated, something determined, and she knew she was helpless. Tears and struggles would avail her nothing; his determination was too strong to heed them. Her limbs, tense with struggle, relaxed and her clenched fists opened and one hand sought his. She lay passively in his arms, was, paradoxically, glad of the comfort of them about her, and submitted to his love. Long after he had fallen asleep

NEW BED she lay awake. She heard the milk lorries rattle by and the whistles of the early morning trains coming into Cheet station. Everything was marvellously clear to her-Bill's desire to finish the bed, the ceremony of the bedspreadhow like Bill the ritual of it all-his ignoring of the fact he had seen her with Paul because he knew he would soon possess her in a way that made a fugitive love affair seem child's play. Yesterday when she had kissed Paul she had been a girl; now she supposed she was a woman. Someone should have told her, she thought; and knew there were things one could never learn save by experience. She remembered her mother's hints and smiled wryly. What did it mean being a man and a woman, and what had it to do with love? The question was too much for her. She could find no satisfying answer to it. She supposed one lived the answer, and when she was old she would know, looking back on all the experiences she had had. Slow tears came into her eyes. A sense of emptiness and vagueness, a loneliness of spirit came over her, and the separation of man from man became linked with the loneliness of the spheres, each cosmically insignificant, whirling in infinite space without contact or knowledge of the other. 

Bill's arm was still thrown heavily about her. She shook it off gently and turned to the wall. As she did so Bill moved also and his arm went round her again as if even in sleep he could not let her go. "Poor Bill," she said softly, and pulled his arm closer about her. 

Sleep came mercifully to put an end to her selfquestionings, her doubts and fears. When she woke again the morning was well advanced..She was alone in the new bed and by the bedside was a cup of tea. She looked at her watch. Eleven o'clock. The tea was stone cold with a thin film of cream on the top. It must have been standing there

NEW BED 

221 for an hour or two. Bill had got up and made his own breakfast without awakening her. She listened intently and she could hear Mrs. Gordon in the kitchen. Bill must have left the kitchen door unlocked so that Mrs. Gordon would get in without disturbing her. 

Ann was tired and her head ached. Generally she jumped out of bed the moment she woke, but this morning she felt reluctant to get up. To wash and dress required an effort of will, a goading of weary limbs. Mrs. Gordon was on her knees polishing the kitchen grate when Ann went downstairs. She looked up with the air of one who was resigned to working while another could sleep, but when she saw Ann's pale face she hoisted herself creakily on her feet and her expression changed to a commiseration only slightly more depressing than her moroseness; for when Mrs. Gordon commiserated she was sorrowing hopelessly for her own troubles, which were many, as well as for those of her companion. "The master said I wasn't to disturb you, or I'd have brought you up a cup of tea," she said. She looked helplessly at the cleaning tackle which covered the hearthrug, wondering how to make room for Ann to sit by the fire.

Ann swept them to one side with her foot, took the blacklead brush off the rocking-chair and subsided into it wearily. "Shall I make you some bread and milk?" asked Mrs.

Gordon eagerly. It was her panacea for all ills. A scar on her forehead, the mark of a wound made by a basin of that same nourishing food breaking her head after it had been thrown at her by an irate husband, to whom she had advised it as a cure for chronic alcoholism, had not shaken her faith in its powers. It had been recommended to Ann before as a cure for toothache, food for baby ducks, as an

NEW BED 

appropriate supper for Bill when he was suffering from a cold. Ann had sufficient spirit left to refuse it again, and 

Mrs. Gordon's interest in her faded away. She watched 

Ann put the coffee-pot on the hob. "Have a cup?" said Ann when she had made the coffee.

Mrs. Gordon struggled with her pride for a moment and then succumbed. "It's poison to the system," she murmured, sipping the coffee with relish in spite of her pessimism. "You are very reckless then," answered Ann, amusement getting the best of her weariness. • · "I always was a reckless sort. My mother used to say: 'Our Annie has the pluck in this family.' My husband says it isn't pluck, but pig-headedness. Well, I married 'im against me mother, so perhaps he's right. » Ann sat and stared in the fire while the tale went on. What love could there be in that sordid union. Was it just animal seeking after animal? She would not believe it. There must be something more, something she did not understand. 

For the first time since she knew Paul she did not want to hurry out and meet him. It was their afternoon for the practice room, but she had no desire to go. Her sadness because he would be disappointed was not so strong as her weariness. She put on her hat and coat and took a basket. 

She would send him a telegram to the shop; Fergerson would take it up to him, she knew. To-morrow she would be herself again, eager to rush out and meet him. When she had sent her telegram she would rest quietly at home. "I will go for you," offered Mrs. Gordon. "The stroll will do me good," answered Ann firmly. 

The grey skies and muted trees fitted her mood. She sent the telegram, made one or two purchases, and walked soberly home again. In the afternoon when Mrs. Gordon had departed she cried a little, thinking of Paul and his

NEW BED 223 disappointment. It was seldom she was alone in the afternoon, and in the grey wintry light the house looked unfamiliar. She pulled her chair to the fire, and tried to read, but after a little while quietness and warmth stole over her and she dozed. She was rosy-flushed with sleep when 

Bill came in an hour earlier than usual. He kissed her warmly and insisted upon her remaining in the arm-chair while he made the tea, and when it was made he brought the tray to the fireside and sat with her, cheerful and almost elated as he cut cake and made jam sandwiches. It was a scene of simple domestic bliss. An onlooker would have written them off as a honeymoon couple. 

When bedtime came Ann was reluctant to go upstairs.

She kept finding little things to do. "I simply must finish this darning," she said emphatically. "You go to bed and 

I'll come up in a few minutes." "I'll wait till you are ready," answered Bill calmly.

The minutes ticked by. The fire fell in and the room began to grow chilly. Bill yawned several times. Finally he came over to her and took the darning out of her hands. "Come along. Do you know it's past midnight.' "> "I don't really want to go to bed," began Ann hastily, but Bill put an arm round her and pulled her out of the chair. "I suppose I've got to go. I can't stay here all night," said Ann to herself, and allowed herself to be led upstairs. "If you'd like to meet me in town to-morrow we'll have a little dinner and go to the pantomime," said Bill as he undressed. "You'd like that, wouldn't you?" Bill, looking very wideawake now, was watching her from the bed.

NEW BED 

"The funny man is very good, I've heard. I think you'll enjoy it." "Yes, I'm sure I will," said Ann politely. Then an idea struck her. "When did you go to a pantomime last?" "I've never been before. I thought perhaps you'd like it."

Ann looked at him, but did not speak. When she could dally no longer she turned out the light, pulled back the curtain and came to the bedside. "You poor little starved thing," murmured Bill compassionately as he reached out and pulled her to him. He rubbed her fingers and folded her cold feet in his own warm ones. NEW BED 225 much, and I love him too, in a way. It is something he wants that I can do for him.

The tenseness went out of her limbs and she lay relaxed against him. "Darling," he whispered triumphantly. "I knew you'd be good... I knew it."

CHAPTER XXI 

THE spring came, and it seemed to Ann the wind howled louder and the rain dashed more pitilessly against all the young growing things in the garden and the sunshine was less warm and beneficent than any other springtime she could remember. The flat sodden fields around Cheet were full of the cries of young lambs, and in the water meadows beyond the spinney peewits kept up an incessant mournful calling as they wheeled round and round. Ann, who had never taken much notice of bad weather before, thought of it now as an enemy, unscrupulous and incalculable. When the wind roared in the trees and flung stinging showers of hail against her face as she walked down the lane to the station it did not awaken echoing wildness in her mind as it once could do, even though the missel thrush was singing his loudest from the topmost twig in the spinney; she cowered and shrank, battling forward with her head down and longing fervently for shelter and a quiet haven. "Will you never be still?" she cried to the wind. "Will you never quiet your wailing and uneasy questing to and fro?" she cried to the plovers, catching a glimpse of their white underparts flashing as they wheeled round: “Hush, hush, little lambs. Huddle close to your mothers. I can't bear to hear you crying in the cold."

Bill revelled in the wild weather. He would come in with his cheeks ruddy, shaking the raindrops from his hat and stamping his feet lustily, and with something of the wind's wantonness embrace Ann. He was no longer morose and watchful, nor did he question her as to her movements 226

NEW BED 

227 during the day. She had a feeling there was no necessity to cover up her movements now and, ironically, she seemed to have lost the desire and energy for adventure. Bill had the air of a man who was satisfied with life. It was not complacency; there was no suggestion of smugness or selfsatisfaction about him; it was a richer content, as of a man in the prime of his powers and conscious of the fact. Physical well-being may have been partly responsible. He ate and slept and loved and worked with gusto. He was felling some old apple trees in the orchard, and Ann thought he swung the axe like a hero, glorying in his sure aim and the crashing sounds. It brought to her mind the forging song in "Siegfried," and she told him so, and felt that she had pleased him. His vigour stood out in contrast against her own vague ill-health and lassitude.

When the winter season ended in March the orchestra, including Paul, went touring neighbouring towns for a further six weeks, and at first Ann put down her lethargy to Paul's absence, imagining that heartsickness and fretting after him was responsible for her miserable condition; but after his return it still persisted and she began to worry inwardly. Was she going to relapse into a semi-invalid? 

How she longed for the sun to grow warm and the days mild and bright.

She was finding it difficult to fit in her hours with Paul and her duties at home. Instead of rushing out eager and alive she would go doggedly, fighting down the desire to rest, and she discovered in herself a tendency to fidget over small insignificant household jobs for the first time. 

Mrs. Gordon, heavy-footed and heavy-minded, irritated instead of amused her. 

Ann slept badly and did not want to eat. Mrs. Gordon, unexpectedly abandoning bread-and-milk, recommended new-laid eggs beat up in brandy, urging Ann to keep up

NEW BED her spirits. Ann detested her constant probings and questions, her not entirely concealed air of "You'll be-worsebefore-you're-better."

Her condition had crept into a letter to her mother, and she received a reply strongly advising her to see a doctor. "Go as soon as you can, and then write to me at once and let me know what he says," wrote her mother, with most of the words underlined to express their urgency. 

Ann was rather surprised at her mother's solicitude, for Mrs. Dibbs was not given to fussing over her daughter's ailments. 

Bill's mother came to see her. "I thought I'd come. I met Mrs. Gordon in Woolworth's buying a poker last week and she told me you were poorly," she blurted out indiscreetly.

Ann was annoyed. "I had a bilious attack. It was nothing," she said coldly, strangely reluctant to discuss the matter with Mrs. Darwin. "That woman has a depressing mind. If I let her she would spend all day describing people's insides.” "But you are not looking so well, my dear. Why don't you see a doctor?" "I'm all right. It's the weather. I can't bear the cold and the wet and the way the wind howls and the trees thrash about." 

Mrs. Darwin nodded sympathetically. "I've known in the winter time I've sat up in the night and nearly begged them to rest their uneasy beating to and fro, it has worried me so. And Bill's father has said to me 'It's a grand sound, the wind roaring and the trees beating,' fair revelling in it. It's something in a man's nature, I suppose, that a woman hasn't got." "I suppose it is," agreed Ann, smiling at the old lady's

NEW BED 229 habit of separating the sexes even in a matter of wind and rain.

Through devious channels of conversation Mrs. Darwin came back to the subject of health. "Bill's looking very well," she began. "Doesn't he think you should see a doctor?" "No, he thinks as I do," said Ann firmly, and began to talk of Alfred.

Bill took his mother to the station. When he came back he hovered round Ann thoughtfully. "You're looking rather washed-out," he began tentatively.

Ann glared, trying to restrain the unreasonable irritation rising within her. "Suppose you pop in and see the doctor in the morning?" "I don't want to," said Ann obstinately. "I suppose your mother's been talking to you... 

"I don't see why you should have any objection to seeing a doctor," said Bill mildly. "It's the usual thing when one is not feeling well." "But I don't see how he can do anything," murmured

Ann, knowing she was behaving childishly and not caring at all. "I wish you'd leave me alone." Her voice choked with tears. Bill was repentant instantly. "You do whatever you wish, my pet. Is there anything you'd like? Anything I can bring you from town?"

Ann shook her head. She was very quiet for some time, staring into the garden, and so occupied with her thoughts that she did not feel Bill's watchful eyes on her face. She turned abruptly and caught the look of anxiety strangely tempered with satisfaction that was in his eyes and wondered at it. It was gone in a flash, and she was too intent upon herself to think twice about it. "I'll go to the doctor's in the morning," she said ear-

NEW BED nestly, as if her words had a significance known only to herself. "You are a good girl," said Bill triumphantly. "I always knew it."

CHAPTER XXII 

"YES," said Ann, speaking more to herself than to the doctor, "I knew. Only I hoped somehow . . ." "And what did you hope?" The doctor was kind and remarkably interested if one considered that nearly every day he asked the same questions and received the same answers; saw the same look of fear and anxiety and pride and satisfaction on the young women who came to him; and gave probably the same advice, the same paternal, reassuring, tired smile. "That it wouldn't be," answered Ann simply. "Ah! Don't worry about that. You'll change your mind in a little while. . . ."

The doctor was not concerned. His smile did not change. 

He led her to the door. "The worse thing you can do is to get nervous. Believe me now. • • "> His manner was wonderfully poised; it suggested on the one hand that to have a baby was a very ordinary, natural, proper thing for a young married woman, and he had every confidence that with his help a good job could be made of it; yet on the other hand he quite realised its tremendous significance, the need for taking every care, and the importance which now invested the person of the young married woman in question. "Fool!" muttered Ann to herself viciously as she walked slowly to the station. "Mechanical fool!" She was at once excited and dismayed, and, as she could not think clearly, excitement, perhaps, was uppermost. She chattered to Paul with some of her old animation, and Paul was glad. 231

NEW BED "Let's take the bus to Tavor and have tea at the cottage and then walk back to Cheet," he proposed. "The sun's coming out."

Ann agreed. She would have done anything at all to please him at that moment. "There is a little while yet; we must be happy while we can," she kept telling herself. 

She deliberately thrust the news to the back of her mind, and when it intruded, as it did again and again, it only made her more responsive and passionately tender to him.

The old woman in the cottage where they had tea was still coy, despite winter rheumatism, and kept coming back with apologies because she could not bear to leave them alone. They bought a bag of treacle toffee at the village store and set off on their walk to Cheet. Pools of water on the fields shone silver and blue, rooks were noisy in the trees, their black wings glistening, and small birds were abundant and busy in the hedgerows. Ann had a posy of celandines and a wand of silver-tipped willow. Paul hummed as he strode along, and when he came to puddles in the road he leapt over them and then came back to help Ann through the mud. Once when they came to a very large muddy patch he motioned Ann to wait, cleared it with a high running jump, collected a pile of stones from the roadside and placed them with great nicety at intervals across the pool. "Now," he said, naïvely pleased with himself. He held out his hand and Ann stepped daintily from stone to stone. 

He kissed her hand gallantly before he let it go and hummed a bar or two of Meine Königin with an interrogatory smile. "Am I?" whispered Ann. "Paul, you love me so much that I am afraid." "Afraid of me?" Paul looked as if he could not understand such a fantastic remark.

NEW BED 

233 "No. Afraid for you," she answered.

Paul, always supersensitive to any remarks about himself, became apprehensive on the instant. "Mother's always saying and looking things like that. What do you think is the matter with me? You aren't going to give me up?" he cried nervously. "I'm being morbid and silly," said Ann. "Don't take any notice." "Don't grow into a woman, Ann. I mean like mother and most other women, full of domesticity and forebodings and wise saws and all the rest of it. . . . There is so much of the earth in women. It is because you are different I love you so. Because you are lovely and above them."

Ann pulled his head down to her shoulder, for she could not bear to see his face so white and passionately assertive, and she did not want him to see her own. "I'm a very earthly person. I like lots of things that aren't at all aloof-treacle toffee, for instance, the sticky kind which makes ones jaws ache,” she laughed. 

Paul laughed as well. "You know what I mean?"

Ann refused to say that she did, but Paul would not believe her. She became very tired towards the end of the walk, so they went slowly, talking and looking over hedges, and Paul searched for a bird's nest while she rested on a stile. Bill was at home when Ann arrived. "I'm glad you've been out in the sun," he said genially. "You are early?" "I don't quite know how I managed it," he answered, looking rather uncomfortable. "Did you go to the doctor?"

Ann guessed then he had been brought home by his desire to hear what the doctor had said to her. He was not surprised when she told him, though he pretended to be. Bill was very bad at pretending. Ann could not rid herself of the feeling that he had known from the beginning, and

NEW BED 

suddenly, startlingly, it came to her that this was what he wanted and what he had meant at Christmas. She pieced together a number of things. His mother's visit and talk of bedspreads, probably while she had been with Paul that evening Bill and his mother had discussed her and his mother had given Bill the idea; Bill's change from jealous watching to mild benevolence and his acts of propitiation or were they rewards? Had she not felt vaguely all the time that his determination to possess her had not been the sudden rising of passion, but a reasoned and thought-out plan, even to the formal instituting of the new bed.

Had he brooded on Maude's tales of Paul, and had it awakened desire in him? He had never taken the slightest interest in children, or given any sign that he wished to be a parent. She groped backwards, looking for clues, for any scrap of conversation, for anything at all that might solve the problem. What was it Bill had murmured to her that night, incoherently, it had seemed then-“something binding-a tie-growing apart-I don't want to lose you." Was it that? Had he planned this thing to bind her to Cheet and to himself? "A good girl" he had called her, because he knew that hereafter it must be as he wished. 

Her freedom was gone, if indeed she had ever had any, ever, in her whole life. Her lips curled bitterly. It was her own fault. She should have stuck out for her independence and the life she wanted, even if it had meant she starved. But how was she to know? She would take care, she vowed, if she had a daughter, that it did what it wanted, even if its ideas seemed as fantastic to her as hers had seemed to her mother. She did not know, poor distressed Ann, how many prospective mothers in the world were vowing the same thing that very moment.

235 She came out of her reverie to find Bill watching her anxiously. "Don't worry, my pet. I will take care of you. You'll be all right," he said tenderly. "Don't brood too much. 

You'll be glad later on." "That's what the doctor said. Do all men say the same in similar circumstances, I wonder?" The words marked

Ann's initiation into that great company of women Paul had been apostrophising only a few hours before. Bill winced. For moment horror of what he had done came to him; not so much because of the havoc it would make, temporarily at any rate, to Ann physically, but because of what might happen to her mind. He could not bear to think that her gaiety, her pretty ways, should be lost to him and a grown woman come in her place who was disillusioned by life like most other married women he had met. "I think you should have told me," said Ann quietly. "You might have asked if I agreed.” "If you loved me," began Bill, and seemed to find the words inadequate.

Ann did not answer, nor did she mention the subject again. The only change in her as the days went by was a grave quietness, as though she were living in her thoughts and the world about her had diminished; and, occasionally, a peaked white face of misery and suffering which made 

Bill's heart go cold. It would appear without warning. She might be sitting by the fire with her sewing or in the window seat, withdrawn but apparently content, and then she would give a little cry, the faintest possible sound, and 

Bill would look up and see her face change into a pinched white mask with eyes that stared unhappily at some picture he could not see.

NEW BED "Are you all right, my dear?" he would ask, and go to her. "Yes, thank you,” she generally answered, and her expression would relax; but sometimes she got up and hurried out of the room, and he would wait miserably about the door until she returned. She was shy and would not tell him when she felt ill, but went away and shut herself up alone. Bill was hurt because before when she had been ill she had come to him for comfort and advice. 

She wrote and told her mother. Mrs. Dibbs was practical, but not inspiriting. She sent Ann a moleskin cloak which she had discarded and which she said Ann would be sure to find useful with coats being worn so tight about the figure, and she also said she was not surprised, though she had hoped it might not be so; still, as it had happened it might be all for the best. Charlie Dibbs, she went on, was making a perfect fool of himself over the news. He wanted to be the baby's godfather, and he did hope it would be a little girl like Ann. Ann was amused at the picture of 

Charlie Dibbs her mother's vivid phrases called up. Her animosity to him was forgotten. Time had killed it and the bitterness and disgust he had once caused seemed real no longer; now she saw him not as a menace, but a vulgar, sentimental, good-natured publican whom her mother had married. She was rather touched by his desire for a little girl. "I was heartless. He must have been fond of me. I might have been kinder," she thought, and then forgot him.

She was disappointed at the attitude of her mother and her mother-in-law. They were sympathetic; they had shoals of advice to give—some of it useful; they were helpful and kind about practical details, admitting her, in fact, to a kind of comradeship they had never done before; but neither of them seemed to think there was any wonder in

NEW BED 

237 it, and Ann, in spite of her sadness because her way of life must now go in a different direction from Paul's, in spite of a recurring sensation of bedraggled and wretched illhealth, could not keep wonder from stealing into her mind that this thing should be happening to her. Strangely enough, Mrs. Gordon was thrilled for Ann and because of her. She was a woman depressed and ill-treated by life; she had borne five children in conditions of poverty and sordid makeshift to a drunken husband who ill-used her, and she had one child who was simple and another a cripple; yet the bringing of another child into the world was to her a matter of rejoicing. She went about the house on what might be described for such a flat-footed person, as tiptoe, leaving behind her on the window seat or the kitchen table copies of "Home Ways,” which contained articles on babyhood and pictures of immaculate curly-headed smiling infants in the arms of immaculate curly-haired smiling mothers. Ann found her one morning pouring entranced over an illustrated catalogue of baby carriages which had been put in the waste paper basket. "Eh, my word!" she whispered. "Aren't they grand!

He'll be a proper little prince." For she had already made up her mind that the baby was to be a boy. 

Bill worried because Ann looked so ill. He went secretly to the doctor, which the doctor inadvertently revealed the next time Ann saw him. "Now what's this I hear, my dear young lady? You are bringing it all on yourself. It is just nervousness. Be calm, and think how nice it will be when it is all over. Now tell me, in confidence, is there anything on your mind?"

Ann played for a moment luxuriously with the idea of telling him about Paul and her anxiety, and asking him how she could best break the news. The relief of speaking to someone about it, someone wise and calm who wasn't

NEW BED implicated would have been immense, but instinct warned her that his bland, wise smile and his reassuring voice was part of his professional outfit, and for problems of the heart neither he nor any other doctor could help. She had begun to feel she could no longer go out with Paul every day. The effort to get through her work in the house and rush out to meet him and then hurry back again to be ready for Bill in the evening exhausted her. Fits of dizziness came over her without warning, draining the colour from her face, making her momentarily helpless. She had one of these fits in the lift going up to the practice room one afternoon. The lift had started suddenly, sending a sickening wave of nausea through her, so that she lurched and would have fallen if the liftman had not supported her. 

Paul was panic-stricken when he saw her being assisted from the lift. When she had sipped reluctantly from the dirty glass of water Fergerson with great concern had fetched from his cubby hole, and he had gone away at last to attend to frenzied bell-ringings from below, Paul gave way to his terror. "Ann, darling, don't be ill! You mustn't be. What shall 

I do if I can't see you for days and days and I know you are at Cheet ill and perhaps getting worse, and nobody would let me in to see you! It would be appalling. I should go mad."

Ann smiled a little at the way Paul's imagination worked, always fearfully looking ahead and seeing the worst. The faintness passed and the colour came back to her lips, but her cheeks were ivory, and there were shadows like bruises under her eyes. She made use of the opportunity, however. 

I won't come out so much "I went to the doctor and he said I must rest more. 

For a little while. she begged.

NEW BED 

239 

Paul stared at her horrified. "If I was dying I'd want to come and see you. Nothing would keep me in, short of unconsciousness or no legs." 

Ann began to tremble. "Paul, don't be wild. Think quietly. It isn't because I don't love you. It's just sense, common sense, that I should do what the doctor says." "And what's common sense got to do with us, I'd like to know?" cried Paul triumphantly. "There wouldn't be any lovers or love in the world if common sense ruled."

Ann did not know what to say. She sat still, pale and unhappy, her hands in her lap and her cloak folded round her, shut in with her thoughts. Her stillness and unsmiling face and never before had she not smiled at his extravagant speeches-affected Paul, and the triumph oozed out of him leaving a frightened boy, nervous as a young horse, with vague fears and apprehensions thick upon him. "You'll come sometimes... 

Ann laughed then and kissed him. "Darling Paul! Of course I will. As much as ever I can. You talk as if I didn't want to come." "However shall I tell him when the time comes?" she wondered.

The emotion of the afternoon tired her. In the train going home she felt old and weary; so weary indeed that romantic love did not seem to matter very much, and all she wanted was to rest in the big arm-chair and to hear Bill's quiet voice telling her not to worry about anything at all. It was incongruous she should be so drawn to Bill when it was because of him all this misery had come about, but he was gentle and devoted, and there was a sense of stability about him which soothed her. 

As the days went by, the child within her grew in her mind as well as in her body. She began to think of it as an individual given into her care. Occasionally when she had to lie to Bill about how she had spent her day she suffered

NEW BED 

torment. "If I tell a lie it' may tell lies later on. It will be my fault. I shall have ruined its character, taught it to deceive by deceiving myself. My life should be beautiful, full of good thoughts. If I am tormented with worry, perhaps it will be born a poor tormented creature who will go through life worried and harassed. If I am afraid, it will feel my fear." So like a saga, went her thoughts, and Bill would say at last, when she had not spoken for an hour or more, "What are you thinking of hour after hour as you sit there?" and get no answer but a troubled smile or a hand shot swiftly into his to be warmed.

She spent less and less time with Paul, making her absences gradually. His quartet had obtained several engagements, and thus encouraged, more of his time was taken up with rehearsals. Because she could not tell him everything she felt the bond between them loosening. He could not be all her life now. She filled the house with tulips and branches of flowering cherry and apple blossom, and she took off the luxurious bedspread and substituted a patchwork counterpane she had found packed away in a box. 

Bill made no protest; the whole of the house was hers to do with as she wished. She would not go into the barn amongst the fauns and satyrs and leaping goats. She wanted to forget them, because occasionally in fevered dreams, they would come and frighten her. One dream persisted. She would be holding a child in her arms, hushing it to sleep and singing to it, and then, without warning, it would wriggle and writhe, rearing itself up so that its face was close to hers and she would discover it had changed into a grinning horned creature. She would awaken in terror, wet with perspiration. She tried to tell Bill, but in describing the details it sounded so much like Alice's encounter with the duchess's baby which turned into a pig, that they

NEW BED 

241 had to laugh; but in the night she could find no humour in it at all.

Bill made some drawings of her at this time in a long loose gown with her breasts showing, spring flowers at her feet and young things following her. "I know it has been done before, but it is an eternal subject," explained Bill. “The orchard is full of young birds just now. We will see if we can catch one or two and you shall hold them for me while I make some sketches. We'll give them back to their parents again," he said, anticipating Ann's question before she had spoken. Holding the tiny warm balls of feathers and down in the curved palms of her hands, she could feel their hearts beating wildly and the heat of life strong in their frail bodies. One movement and her fingers could have crushed the life out of them. She was amazed that creatures so frail and defenceless survived in a world that belonged to the ruthless and the strong. "This piece is for you, Ann. As an offering. . . . It shall be the best thing I have ever done ," stammered Bill, touched by her absorption in the young birds and the sadness in her expression. "Put all the foolish young creatures in it-young rabbits and tiny mice, the silly defenceless kind who haven't any wits and get preyed upon by those who have-because I love them most," she said.

CHAPTER XXIII 

SPRING passed without warning into summer. There were tiny apples and pears in the orchard where only a week or two ago it seemed, had been blossom snowing white in the wind. Ann basked in the sun. She was sluggish and indolent, and often when she was reading she would find herself ready to doze. She was greedy for meal times, and all day long she could feel her body craving for fruit-pears, cool and juicy, apples and oranges and grapes. She was of the earth, concerned with physical needs and domesticities; one of those women Paul had talked about and begged her not to become. Remembering this, she would put down the pear into which she was about to bite and then, a moment after, pick it up again; her will was as flacid as her body. 

She had never known before the voluptuousness of languor, and the sensation would have been pleasant if she could have indulged in it with an easy mind; but she had arranged to meet Paul, so she beat back her heaviness and went to change.

It was too warm now for her moleskin cloak, but she had nothing else which concealed her figure so well. The sunlight made it look shabby, and it seemed to have gained in weight since the spring and to hang heavily on her shoulders when she walked. She hated having to dress herself and go to town.

She knew the moment she saw him that he had discovered her secret. The look on his face drove away the dreaminess into which the jig-jog rhythm of the local train had lulled her and she became alert, fear with a myriad sting- 242

NEW BED 243 ing points awakening her. All her blood rushed to her heart and left the pit of her stomach sickeningly empty, so that she could barely control her limbs. She saw him look at her cloak, and always he had looked first at her eyes to see them shining at him, and she was filled with a shame that burned fiercer and was more painful than any living fire. 

He looked white and sick, his arms hung slackly from his shoulders, and when an impatient woman elbowed him he moved aside unseeingly into the path of another passenger-Paul, whose manners were so charming and who could move so gracefully and skilfully in a crowd! 

Habit took them down the station steps into a little café. 

The waitress knew them and moved the chairs so they could make their way into a corner they had made their own. Ann was a tingling mass of self-consciousness; she imagined the girl looked curiously at her, as if she wondered why on such a radiant June day Ann should wear a fur cloak, and wondering, came upon the reason for it. 

They had to wait until she came for their order and had delivered it before they could speak to each other. "Ann, Mother says . . ." Paul faltered, went red and then white, pitiful in his embarrassment. "Who told her?" "I don't know. She always finds out things. She doesn't want to be told. It's a way mothers have." "Yes. It's funny how they do it," muttered Ann, as if she did not think it funny at all, but humiliating and inevitable. "I haven't been able to sleep all night. The shock when she told me." "Paul, darling Paul. I've been wanting to tell you, but 

I've been afraid. I've kept hanging on, hoping in a mad kind of way it wasn't true, that it was all a nightmare. And yet I knew it couldn't be."

NEW BED 

Paul still hung his head. He would not look at her.

She put out her hand timidly, but he did not put his own into it. She stared at her hand in an impersonal way and felt sorry for it lying there rejected and lonely on the table, and ashamed, she drew it back again. "Won't you even look at me?" she whispered.

He looked at her, and Ann knew how deep the wound had gone. "Ann, why did you?" "It wasn't my fault," she began, and felt the uselessness of excuses. How could she tell him about Bill and of their intimate life together? "It was the kind of trick Bill would do. To make you safe; so we'd never be able to go away together and do the things and see the places and hear the music together like we planned. He wanted to make an ordinary wife of you, to spoil your loveliness. There are hundreds and thousands of ordinary women. Why couldn't he have taken one of them? Then it wouldn't have mattered ... but you .. it's sacrilege—a wicked, wicked thing," cried Paul tears streaming down his cheeks. He forced back a sob. "How 

I wish he were dead! That a thunderbolt would fall this very moment and nothing remain of him. I should laugh. 

I should dance with joy." His voice rose hysterically. The waitress came out of the kitchen, looked in their direction and went back again. "Hush! Hush!" whispered Ann urgently, her blood running cold with his denunciations of Bill. "You mustn't say such things. It wasn't all his fault."

Paul wiped the moisture from his face angrily. His eyes were sunken and red-rimmed, and when he tried to smile sardonically at her words the smile went awry. "Not all his fault? You are trying to excuse him.

NEW BED 

245 

You are a dear, soft, weak creature who won't believe ill of anyone. That has been your downfall-our downfall." 

Ann tried to speak. She wanted to explain in Bill's defence how all their weeks of love-making, hers and Paul's, had awakened something in her which had left her vulnerable to Bill's husbandly embraces, but she could not find the words; at most it was a vague idea. She had no confidence 

Paul would understand, and she could not bear that he should think her a wanton.

They sat a little while apart in mood, in spite of their unhappiness, unable to talk commonplaces and at the same time unable to reveal their hearts. "I'll take you to the train," said Paul suddenly, and helped her up; but he did not look her in the face as he did so. They might have parted like this-Paul with his glance averted, Ann shrunk so small and still that a cloak to hide her no longer felt necessary, had she not stumbled getting into the carriage so that automatically his arms went out to save her and her arms went about him. "Paul, Paul," she cried, her voice imploring him not to part in anger.

They held hands then like two frightened children unable to cope with what life had done to them, their eyes eating each other up, hungry and woe-begone at the desolation of parting. They kissed, found comfort in it, oblivion for a second at least, and kissed again. The guard came down the train shutting doors, his green flag ready to wave the train out. Ann hung through the window for one last kiss, one last look. "Paul!" "Ann!"

The train began to move slowly. Paul ran along the

NEW BED platform beside her. "Curse you, curse you," he was saying under his breath to the guard. "Good-bye, Ann," she caught, faint and quivering as the train gathered speed. 

Frantically she waved her hand. "Darling, darling 

Paul," she cried into the misty air, and the words choked on her lips. She sank back sobbing into the carriage, kneading her hands in and out of each other in an effort to control herself. She became aware that her violent emotion was upsetting the rest of her body. "Poor mite," she cried, to the child within her, rocking herself to and fro half crazy with pain. "It's hard for you having a mother like me, instead of a calm, placid woman who'd take care of herself. 

But in future I'll be good. I'll do everything the doctor says I must. I'll live for you."

When she reached Cheet Barn Mrs. Gordon had finished her work and gone home. She had put the tea-things on the table, the kettle was filled ready to boil, everything was newly swept and dusted. Ann was grateful for the quiet and order. She drank some milk and went to rest on the window seat. She could hear a willow wren's plaintive pipe of distress in the orchard. It kept on, now loud, now soft, as the bird flitted from branch to branch, a tiny discord in the afternoon's quiet. Ann endured it as long as she could then dragged herself to her feet. "A wretched cat after her chicks, I expect," she said. "I'd better go and see."

She found the cat, a stray by its lean ribs and hungry wild eyes, and chased it out of the orchard. She went to lie down again, but the thought of the cat's meagre frame and staring coat persisted. "It was starving, poor beast. It would have been better if I had taken it something to eat. 

I wouldn't like to know its opinion of me. How callous human beings are!"

She could not rest. She went into the larder, cut a slice

NEW BED 

247 of meat from the joint and wandered into the orchard, calling the cat. By now it was pressing heavily on her conscience that she had chased a hungry creature away. "I would like 'it' to be kind to animals," she told herself. 

Bill, coming in an hour afterwards and missing Ann, went searching at once. He found her in the spinney, holding a piece of meat in her hand, calling upon a cat and sometimes upon Boodle. She ran to him sobbing out an incomprehensible story of a willow wren and a hungry cat, and Bill, taking the meat and throwing it in the brook, led her gently away and put her to bed. "I'm going to ask Mother to come and stay for a while," he announced next morning. Ann was sitting up in bed eating her breakfast. She had slept and seemed to have recovered from her adventure of the day before. "I can't trust you not to go roaming about tiring yourself out. Would you like her to come?" "I don't mind," answered Ann; and it went through her with a stab of pain that it didn't matter now who came and stayed, because there was no longer any need to deceive or invent reasons why she must go out. The necessity to live alone, so that no one should know what she did with her days, was gone.

CHAPTER XXIV 

BILL's mother brought up a letter. She stood waiting at the side of the bed. "It's not from London," she said.

Ann examined the postmark also, though it was not necessary, for she knew who had written the letter. "No. 

It isn't," she answered brightly, and without opening it put it under the pillow. Bill's mother was disappointed. 

She moved to the window and stood there. Ann knew that if a member of the Darwin family, a feminine member, that is, received a letter it was taken for granted that the others shared it; but Ann, even if she was now more firmly embedded in the family than before, did not mean to conform altogether to their ways. They made such a difference, she thought exasperatedly, between men and women. If it had been Bill who was receiving the letter his mother would have gone away without an inquiring look, no matter how strong her curiosity. 

The old lady came from the window. "The postman's just gone by with a big parcel. It must be Jones's along the lane sending a parcel to their married daughter in townflowers and vegetables, maybe. I saw he had a letter for them when he called here." 

Ann smiled at her. "I'm thinking of getting up," she said, and reached out for her dressing-gown. "Your letter can't be very important. You don't seem in a hurry to read it. I didn't know you had friends in town. 

Have you been buying something from a shop in town and that's the bill?" Ann smiled again. "Now confess you are dying to know what it is all about? I hope you don't worry 248

NEW BED 249 Pansy like this every time she gets a love letter from 

Alfred," she scolded laughingly. 

Bill's mother bridled. "I can mind my own business," she said. "Shall I bring your breakfast here? Bill said you were to have it in bed. I'm just going to have mine." "I'll have it downstairs with you. I won't be a minute."

Ann pulled her dressing-gown round her, slipped the letter into her pocket, and went quickly into the bathroom. She locked the door and turned on the taps, then tore open the envelope. The letter was from Paul—a wild letter begging her to come out with him again if only for a few moments. 

He would not tire her with unreasonable demands; all he begged was for her to see him, to speak to him, to tell him it wasn't all over between them. He was lonely, half crazy with heartache and fear that she was lost to him for ever. He would go anywhere, any time, if there was only a chance of seeing her. He would wait in the practice room every afternoon this week. Since their parting she had relentlessly beaten down the pictures of him which had come haunting her. It was all over. She had a job to do which needed all her strength and which must come first. She had been very ill that night after the parting and it had frightened her. She must be hard and cruel to Paul. She could do no good to him now. 

If she saw him again heartache would come back and the wild rebellious thoughts which made her turn from Bill. 

The effort to get to town, the difficulty of her mother-inlaw, who was kind but curious, was too much for her. 

Consciousness of her condition embarrassed her, and a feeling that women were looking at her pityingly yet with avid eyes, as if they were going over in their minds how it came about and what she must suffer. She wanted to hide herself away at Cheet Barn and forget the girl she had been.

NEW BED 

Bill's mother came knocking at the door. She turned the handle, but found it locked. "Are you all right, my dear?" she called.

Ann turned off the taps. The bath had filled almost to the top while she had sat there thinking of Paul. "I'm coming now." "I'm not hurrying you, but I couldn't hear you moving about. I wondered if you felt faint."

Ann pushed the letter into her pocket and opened the door. The old lady looked at the bath, at Ann quivering and pale, and shook her head. "Eh, dear,” she said, "you shouldn't have locked the door. You might have been poorly in the bath. I don't hold with all this bathing at such a time. It isn't natural. 

You'll be catching cold."

Ann was about to argue when, to her surprise, she found she was beginning to cry. Her mother-in-law was all comfort and clucking in a moment. She led Ann back to bed, tucked her up, brought her breakfast and a fresh hot waterbottle, and as a concession, the morning paper, though Ann knew Bill's mother did not approve of the paper being opened before Bill had seen it. "Now you stay there, love, while I go and set Mrs. 

Gordon to her work. You're not to worry. . . ." She hovered round for a moment, then fetched one of her shawls for Ann's shoulders, obviously despising Ann's diaphanous bed jacket, and, smiling reassuringly from the door, departed downstairs full of the importance of her job. "I can't go," Ann said to herself definitely. "I must write and tell him. Later on. . . ." But somehow she could not imagine later on. Something had happened to her imagination. She tried to think of the days when she would be slim again, quick on her feet and the heaviness departed; she

NEW BED 251 tried to think of the days when she ran with Boodle and had waited for him to come up with his sides heaving and his tongue hanging out and his tail signalling wild delight; but she could only think of these things as happening or as having happened to someone else—a stranger. 

Later in the day when Bill's mother was rocking herself drowsily in the kitchen Ann wrote her letter. She put on her coat to go to the pillar-box at the end of the lane. "Are you going out?" said the old lady, opening her eyes. "Yes," answered Ann. "I'll take your letter for you." Bill's mother sat upright in her chair, but Ann pushed her gently back again. "A walk will do me good," she said. "Wait, and I'll put on my things and go with you." "I'm sure you are better resting." Ann's voice was firm, almost as firm as Maude's could be. "She is very kind, but persistent, and remarkably like her son," thought Ann, as she went slowly along the shady side of the lane. A wave of foreboding went over her. Between them she would be devoured. She was already devoured. She had given up 

Paul and their beautiful life together was at an end. She had disappointed and cruelly hurt him. She was tempted not to post the letter; to go and see him and get away for a while by herself; for being with Paul before this had happened had been a kind of discovery of her real self. For a second she thought it might still be the same, and then she knew it could never be. She must not go. It was best for them both. She had no strength for an emotional scene. 

The thought of his disappointment was a dull ache that went deeper and deeper as she walked back to the house. 

Bill's mother clucked at her white face and listless hands. "I could have gone. You shouldn't go walking in the heat."

NEW BED "Leave me alone. Leave me alone," cried Ann, and rushed into the scullery.

Mrs. Gordon was at the sink, washing up. The room was full of steam and the smell of greasy water. Her patient, worn face, which often reminded Ann of a dolorous sheep, was mildly reproachful. "Eh, love," she remonstrated, and taking her pink steaming fingers out of the water she wiped them fugitively and pulled from her apron pocket a copy of "Home Ways.' 

She handed it to Ann. 

"There, love, you haven't had Nurse Sunshine this week, have you?"

The paper came open at Nurse Sunshine's page. It was crumpled as if Mrs. Gordon had been taking surreptitious peeps at it when she was working. "Nurse says keep calm.

Agitation is bad for your baby," went the headlines above a picture of a smiling but strong-jawed woman in a nurse's cap. 

Ann's hysteria faded and she smiled with affectionate amusement at Mrs. Gordon. "Nurse looks a bit of a tartar," she offered. "She's firm," asserted Mrs. Gordon, as if the nurse were a personal friend; "but then she has to be. I 'old with it." "Yes," agreed Ann politely. "I don't think you could be very firm."

Mrs. Gordon's weak face took on an expression of mulish obstinacy. "I can when I wants to be," she said, then added mildly, afraid Ann would think her hard-hearted: "It's not often as I wants."

Bill's mother had a knack of effacing herself in the evenings. She would sit knitting in the rocking-chair, taking little part in any conversation there might be between Ann and Bill. She was happy to be living at Cheet Barn again; this time without drudgery. The job of looking after
