253 

Ann delighted her; she was proud to have been asked, and the thought of a grandchild sent her mind back to the time Bill was born, remembering only the joy it had been. 

Sometimes as she knitted she chuckled softly and smiled and nodded at Ann as much as to say, "I know all about it. 

There isn't anything you can tell me. I felt just the same once. It was just the thing to change you from a young girl frittering away her days idly and getting into mischief to a proper wife for Bill, who I remember as a fat crowing selfwilled child needing all my strength and all my time to look after him; and may you have such another." Her conviction that Ann was changing into a sensible wife was strengthened by Ann's attitude towards the cradle. "Do you remember those cradles at South Kensington?" asked Bill one evening in the kind of voice which meant his question was but a prelude to something more important and was no sentimental reflection. "And the spinning wheels as well. Out of Grimm's Fairy

Tales. It seems a long time ago since we saw them. Another age," said Ann.

She sighed a little, purposely, for Bill and his mother seemed to forget that she had had any life before she was married.

Bill did not take any notice of the sigh, if he heard it. "I always remember how you spoke of the cradles; you wanted to take one home with you." "I did," said Ann promptly. "I'm thinking of making you one. I got some old oak years ago when they pulled down Cheet Hall, and I've never used it. As a matter of fact it's only to be finished off," he blurted out, unable to conceal his secret any longer. "Shall I fetch it?”

The cradle was a deep low trough, dark and heavy with crude carving on its sides, almost a replica of one of the

NEW BED 

Flemish cradles Ann had seen in the Museum. As a museum piece she would have admired it, but the idea of putting a modern child into it did not appeal to her. She was not sure that one rocked children nowadays. "Nurse Sunshine would not approve," she began facetiously. 

Bill did not like her laughing voice. He looked cast down and rather annoyed. "It's lovely of you to have made it," said Ann warmly, catching a glimpse of his down-drawn-mouth; "but somehow I couldn't put a real child into it. It wouldn't be fair." 

It was the first time she had spoken of the child, and when Bill had tried to speak of it she had evaded him or made reluctant embarrassed replies; but now the desire to explain herself was greater than her shyness. "You are quite right, love. Who'd ever put a baby in that dark, heathenish thing? White muslin and blue ribbons or pink if it's a girl is proper." 

Bill looked at his mother angrily and her voice tailed off, but she knitted furiously and looked ready to break out again at any moment. "Apart from what Nurse Sunshine calls 'hygenic reasons,'

I'd rather have an ordinary cot. I don't want it to start with a handicap. A child rocked in that cradle couldn't very well be normal; its environment, to quote Nurse Sunshine again, would affect it. It would grow up super-sensitive, an artist or a musician, hankering after bygone things and the picturesque. I want it to be happy. It might even demand period furniture and trappings as it grew out of the cradle."

Ann's manner was playful, but it had an underlying seriousness impossible to disregard. "You don't think artists and musicians are happy folk?” asked Bill. 

Ann was thinking of Paul. "No. They are tormented. I

NEW BED 255 suppose they get a fierce kind of joy out of the things they live for, but mostly it's disappointment. The world's against them." "It wouldn't look too bad," suggested Bill's mother, in an attempt to bring the conversation back to practical things, "standing by the front door with plants in it. A fern or two, or some of that pretty green hanging stuff." 

Bill turned to Ann with a grin. "You're right. The world is against them. Do whatever you wish." He picked up the cradle and departed to the barn. "If he'd made a nice wooden cot now," murmured Bill's mother. "I don't know where he gets his funny ideas from, I'm sure. Not from my side of the family. . . ." She rambled on while Ann stared in the fire and tried to imagine the future and could not.

CHAPTER XXV 

THE letter was face downwards on Ann's breakfast tray, half hidden by the tea cosy. Ann, hollow-eyed and limp, rejected the breakfast. "Shall I make you a cup of coffee or some nice hot milk? 

Do have something, love," said Bill's mother coaxingly. "I'll have it presently." Ann's voice was faint and weary, as if she were bored and utterly disgusted with herself. She gathered a little energy at Bill's mother's remonstrating cluck. "Will this go on for another two months? Is it always like this?" she asked. "You've had a bad turn, my pet. You'll be better now if you lie quiet and don't upset yourself. The doctor says it is only nerves." "Were you like this when Bill was born-sick day after day, night after night?" persisted Ann.

Bill's mother sighed. "I've mostly forgotten. Not quite so bad perhaps, but then I was so set up about having a baby that I didn't feel it so badly, if you know what I mean. 

There was always a lot to do. I'd got no one to help me. I was over being badly in love with Bill's father; he wasn't a hard man but he didn't show his feelings easily, and I was looking forward to something to love all my own, who'd love me and want me desperately bad. I didn't care so much so much for chapel in those days. I hadn't the same need for the Lord."

She looked at Ann shyly with her eyes soft and moist. 

A little ashamed of her confession, she took Bill's pillow 256

NEW BED 

257 and propped it behind Ann fussily. "Now you read your letter while I pour you out a cup of tea."

Ann turned the letter right side up and her heart jumped. She must have exclaimed, because Bill's mother said anxiously: "What is it? Have you got a pain again?" "It's nothing," answered Ann, and opened the envelope almost reluctantly, knowing, before she read it, what the letter contained. She was not prepared, though, for the appeal Paul made; and as she read the short, restrained sentences she seemed to be coming alive again-painfully alive -after weeks of dismal coma. It wasn't that she had forgotten him, but that she had let him drop into the background as she had become more and more preoccupied with herself and her condition. "I've been wicked and selfish and cruel," she cried silently, and hated herself. She knew Paul was desperate, because of the way he had written. It was not the wild extravagant language she knew and had smiled at so often; it wasn't an appeal to her sense of the romantic, but a desolate, heart-broken cry. He wanted to see her again just once more, because he had been offered a job as leader of an orchestra in Johannesburg, and he did not know what to do. He could not bear to go so far away and leave her; yet perhaps it was the only thing to do. If she would come for an hour and talk to him for the sake of all the lovely days they had had together. He was certain she would come, he said. It was the Paul she knew so well, turning first this way and then that, unable to make up his mind until he had talked to her, not because of the wisdom of her advice -he never took the slightest notice of it—but because she was his good friend and he could talk freely out of his heart to her, and so explain his problem to himself. It had often been like this when he had had a decision to make; it might

NEW BED 

be on a reading of a piece of music or a visit to the dentist or anything.

She put the letter back in its envelope and drank the tea 

Bill's mother had poured out. "I'll have the toast as well," she said, and nibbled it deliberately until she had eaten it all, intent on making herself fit to go out. 

Bill's mother was gratified. "It was the letter did you good," she said. "What did I tell you?" She took the tray and went downstairs. Ann waited until she heard the kitchen door shut, then she got up and began to put on her clothes. 

A violent pain stopped her for a moment; she bent over until it was subdued, cursing it angrily for hindering her, and as soon as it was gone she went on cautiously, but with a kind of ant-like persistency until she was fully dressed. 

A bout of pain would hold her up for a while. Angrily she would wait until it calmed down, and then, wiping away the perspiration, she would continue. She scrutinised herself in the mirror and loathed her worn, pallid face, which seemed to have lost youth and prettiness. The shape of her face was different, she thought; the muscles had relaxed, and she did not look ill and interesting, but dull and dejected and unhealthy. She turned her back on the mirror and went to the far window. A breath of air. would invigorate her, but the morning was well advanced, and outside the lane quivered white with heat. She could not see a cloud in the sky. The noises from the railway came raucous and shrill through the open window. "Oh, be quiet," begged Ann peevishly, putting her hand to her head.

She had swung back to Paul, reproaching herself violently for the indolence of the last few weeks. She could not face the question yet, whether it would be best for him to go abroad or to stay in England, because a sense of the distance appalled her. He would be lost there. There would never be any hope of their coming together and she would

NEW BED 

259 never know how he was or if he was successful. That he was not many miles away had been a comfort even while it tormented her. "I must go to-day," she told herself, and beat down a wave of nausea successfully.

She went downstairs. She did not think about the opposition there would be from Bill's mother. She had quite made up her mind to go, and determination gave her a sense of mastery. Her old tweed coat hung on the kitchen door. It was shapeless with age and weather, but it was not so hot as her cloak. She also put on her broad-brimmed garden hat to shade her eyes, for she was dreading the hot streets and the brassy glare of the sun. Dim cool corners she longed for, out of sight where she could hide away. 

Bill's mother came into the kitchen with Mrs. Gordon behind her. "What are you doing? You surely aren't going out?" she said. "There are wasps in the orchard if you are thinking of sitting there. I should stay indoors in the cool, love.” "I shan't be long. I'm just going for a little walk," said

Ann feebly, because she discovered she was trembling all over and did not want her mother-in-law's sharp eyes to notice it. "You're not fit. Why, the look of you'd frighten anyone. Be a good girl now and lie down," coaxed Bill's mother, fighting her desire to be sharp so as not to upset Ann.

Ann sat down temporarily with one eye on the clock. 

Paul was expecting her by the usual train, and it would soon be due and she knew she dare not run to catch it. "I had a letter from a friend. It's absolutely necessary 

I should go. I'll be very careful. I'm heaps better to-day," she said. "Eh, it's going against Providence. And you so poorly

NEW BED all week," whispered Mrs. Gordon, hoarse with anxiety.

Bill's mother began to cry the weak tears of an old woman. She recognised Ann was determined to go and she was crying for her grandchild and because she no longer had the strength to force Ann to stay at home. Ann put an arm round her. "I'll be all right. I must go. It's very urgent. I'll be very careful, and in an hour or so I'll be back as right as rain. 

Don't upset yourself," she said soothingly. "It isn't safe, not with them pains." Bill's mother wiped her eyes and looked at Ann pleadingly. "The doctor said it was only nerves," said Ann, her turn now to be reassuring. She looked at the clock and Bill's mother caught her glance and interpreted it. "That clock's a good five minutes slow. You won't catch the twelve o'clock train now if you run for it." "I haven't heard it go," said Ann doubtfully. 

Mrs. Gordon caught Mrs. Darwin's eye. "It's a good bit slow. It threw me out properly yesterday," she asserted earnestly.

Ann's spirit and energy evaporated. She turned faint with disappointment. "There isn't another until two o'clock. Suppose I got a bus to Tavor and then another to town from there? But I don't know the bus times and they don't go often," she said. "The reek of hot oil is enough to turn you up this weather. I don't know why they've got to smell the way they do." Mrs. Gordon was about to set out on a diatribe against the use of cheap oil for poor people's travel, when a glance from Mrs. Darwin stopped her.

Ann battled with a wave of sickness and won, but it was a hard struggle and left her trembling. "He'll come back and meet the next train surely. He'll trust me to come," she tried to assure herself, but could not. She remembered

NEW BED 

261 how, when he had written before, she had not gone. Perhaps he had lost faith in her. Paul would never think there might be a practical reason for her not being there, such as a slow clock and the consequent missing of the train. His mind would leap to the worst at once. She turned her eyes uneasily from her mother-in-law to Mrs. Gordon and back again, but she did not see them, and they were appalled at the wildness of her look. "What shall I do? What shall I do?" she muttered, and beat her hands despairingly. She walked up and down the room. "I haven't heard the whistle. Perhaps it's not as late as

I thought," murmured Bill's mother, frightened and reproached by Ann's staring eyes and her gestures of despair. "I haven't," echoed Mrs. Gordon, sharing the same fear that to thwart Ann would do more harm than to aid her in this mysterious and tragic affair. Neither of them knew why this journey meant so much to Ann, but as the seconds went by they felt it as something omnipotent and compelling. They were its slaves as much as she was, but ignorant, humble slaves, who could only serve and wait. That they did not know the reason for it only added to the spell Ann had cast over them; they did not even think to ask, but took her word unquestioningly. The coming grandchild, Bill's anger because Ann had gone out, faded into the background; the only thing the two women, indeed the whole room, were aware of was the urgency of Ann's desire and her desolation because she thought she was too late. "You might catch it yet," said Bill's mother, abject because of her previous deceit over the clock.

Ann looked at her sternly. In such an atmosphere there was no need for confession. "Oh, oh," she cried, this deceit being more than she could bear, and picking up her gloves and handbag she rushed to the door. "Take care, love," whispered Mrs. Gordon. "For

NEW BED 

Christ's sake take care of that dear child." She was not a blasphemous woman, or given, even in times of stress, to calling upon the Lord.

The two women stood in the doorway motionless, listening to Ann's hurrying footsteps along the path, the click of the gate, and then, not so plainly, the retreating sound along the lane. The bright sunlight falling upon their worn faces showed plainly all the tragedy and pain the years had brought them, all the bitterness their souls had ever endured. Their heads were sunk a little and there was something harsh and angular in their postures, yet something pitiful as well. They knew they were old and defeated. Bill could have carved them then after the style of gothic figures in gaunt and rigid lines out of hard close-grained wood. 

A screaming whistle broke the silence. "She'll not be in time," said Bill's mother. "Eh, poor thing," said Mrs. Gordon pityingly, her soft shapeless face crumpling into unfamiliar folds as she began to cry.

Ann heard the whistle. She was barely at the end of the lane and she had to go through the village, which was uphill, and then down twenty-one steps to the platform.

She knew there were twenty-one because she had often counted them mounting up hurriedly two at the time and three at the top at the end of a day with Paul. The thought of those steps obsessed her now, for descending was almost as painful as climbing could be. She counted under her breath as she hurried along, one-two-three-four, so that it made a little tune. If she could get to the top of the hill so that he could see her coming the porter might keep the train a moment while she descended, for he was a kindly man. She was panting and her back was wet through with perspiration. Her hair stuck clammily to her head and her hat seemed made of lead. She was gasping as she climbed

NEW BED 

263 the hill and imploring urgently under her breath, "Please wait. Please wait." But nobody heard her. A door or two banged violently, the guard called out, there was a shrill whistle, and the train moved slowly out of the station. Ann, reaching the top of the hill in one desperate stride, saw it go. She leaned on the bridge and looked down on the dusty deserted platform while the tail of the train disappeared into the hazy distance. Slowly she crawled down the steps, the handrail hot to her gloveless fingers. The porter had taken up his large broom to sweep the platform. He nodded in friendly fashion to Ann; then, noticing her distressed condition, he said curiously: "You been running for the train? It's a hot day for that sort of a game." "The clock was slow," explained Ann. "Ah!" The porter nodded his head as if slow clocks must always be the enemies of those who would catch trains. "She was punctual. I've known her a minute or two late, but never when you want it." Feeling he had done all that could be required of him in the way of friendly conversation, he picked up his broom again and Ann went slowly into the waiting-room and sat down on a hard bench by the wall. She sat numb and still, without the power to think or move, reading over and over again the posters on the opposite wall.

Slowly-it seemed another age since she set off from home-she became aware that her heart thumped less violently and the perspiration down her back and under her hat had turned cold. She took off her hat and lifted the hair from her forehead. She was moved to look into the flyblown tarnished mirror, and her face came through the murky glass red and shiny, swollen with the heat. She barely recognised herself. She took out her powder-puff and powdered her face liberally and saw a semblance of the old Ann looking back at her. She had exhausted all

NEW BED her energy of mind in the rush to the station. The urgency and importance of her journey had diminished. She made up her mind to go to the practice room if Paul were not at the station. "He will have faith in me. He knows that 

I will come. He won't give up hoping at once," she told herself. "He knows me too well to think I would desert him altogether." She went to look at the station clock.

There was still an hour to wait before the next train was due, but it did not occur to her to go home. She sat down again in the waiting-room, vaguely longing for a drink. 

Strangely enough, her condition had ceased to worry her. 

She felt tired, but not burdened by herself as she had been for so long. Her body felt curiously light, and she was content to sit with odd wayward little memories of the past running through her mind; a kind of dozing, though her eyes were wide open, and she had no desire to sleep. The train came in at last. Ann avoided the few villagers who had appeared on the platform and found an empty carriage. The porter, seeing her struggle with the door, opened it and helped her in with a smile that seemed to say: "There you are. Trains come, if you only have patience," and Ann smiled back, because she was more hopeful now that Paul would not think she had deserted him. She had quite persuaded herself, by the time the train reached town, that Paul would be there to meet her, having come back again with renewed faith. 

And so it should have been. That he was not there must be put down to the negligence of Fergerson, who had chosen this day of all others to get drunk, not just genially intoxicated with a rolling walk and soft smiles in his watery eyes or quarrelsome with a smell of whisky on him and a faint insolence when his passengers stumbled over the steps made by his inaccurate placing of the lift; but completely and absolutely dead drunk, so that he slept in a drunken

NEW BED 265 stupor no bell-ringing could penetrate, prone on the floor of his cubby hole.

Paul's despair when Ann did not arrive by her usual train was all she had imagined it would be; a counterpart of her own frantic misery and distress. He could not believe she would not come, though the train and then the platform emptied and there was no sign of her. He ran this way and that, his eyes searching frantically, fearful he should have missed her, yet knowing that was impossible.

A woman's figure in the distance beguiled him; he rushed forward and, coming nearer, saw with sickening disappointment that it was a stranger bearing no resemblance at all to Ann. He went back again to the platform. A few porters were piling up goods to put into the next train. He asked one of them if the Cheet train had come in, hope rising suddenly within him that the train he had seen had come for some other place. Reason had no share in his hopes, nor experience, for he had met that same train many times and had seen Ann step out of it and walk quickly, breaking into a little run in her eagerness to reach him.

The porter was indifferent. The train had come in some minutes ago. The next one was at two-thirty. He turned his back on Paul and went on with his work. If curses could have had any effect Paul's would have scorched the porter to a cinder; but his broad waistcoated back was unperturbed and almost insolent in its cheerfulness. Paul's hopes vanished then. Despair came over him, slowly like a fog, smothering first his reason then his love and trust, until he was wreathed in a gloom nothing could penetrate. He had lived for this moment and he had been betrayed. When he had received Ann's letter some weeks before, saying how impossible it was for her to come, he had accepted her word and fought valiantly against sadness and loneliness. It had been hard, because it was difficult to get engagements

NEW BED in town in the summer, and the days were long and empty. 

When he was working there was less time to think, and it was an outlet for his energy. The flaming summer mocked him when he went walking by himself in the country; he thought of Ann all the time and how jolly it had been sitting in the shade together talking about his future and the music he wanted to play; and if he stayed at home his mother's anxious fidgeting, her attempts to question him about Ann, her reproachful eyes when he refused his food or played for hours alone in the attic, aroused an irritation he could not conceal. He knew he was unkind, and it only made him more miserable. The crisis had come when, through an old master, he was offered a job in 

Johannesburg. To make up his mind to leave Ann was cruelly hard, for he knew it meant leaving her for ever. 

He was afraid, too, of the new world and the new life. 

His mother had borne him in fear, and all his life terror at the unknown had dogged him. Ann had been the one person he had met, since those early years when Bill had cared for him, who understood and could exorcise this fear. His mother, though she loved him dearly, was too afraid for him, and too aware of his sensitive tingling nerves; she could only share his fears and suffer with him.

For several nights he had been unable to sleep, weighing up the reasons for going, which were many and cogent, and the reasons for remaining, which was his despair at losing Ann, though he knew she was as good as lost already. 

Ann as a mother was a different person; she would join the ranks of women who had no time or thought for things of the mind and spirit. Haggard, with eyes red-rimmed and sunken, and a temper liable to explode at the slightest opportunity, he had gone out each morning to walk until exhaustion forced him to stop. And then at last, when it seemed he had reached breaking-point, the idea had come

NEW BED 267 to him to write and ask Ann to come once more and talk the offer over with him. It was not a passionate, tragic meeting he desired, but a friendly talk to assuage his longing and take away the burden of uncertainty which was driving him mad.

The idea had brought him peace. He wrote his letter and sent it away. He was certain she would come. His mother, seeing him quiet at last, hoped the storm was over.

That morning he was still worn and his eyes were bloodshot for lack of sleep, but he had not tossed all night in frantic indecision; he had lain awake, going over what he would say to Ann; how they would sit together in the practice room and part at last in a sadness exquisite and exalted, eased of their pain. If he could just see her once he would face the future and make something of it, he told himself.

When she did not come all his high resolves turned to bitter disillusion. She had forgotten. All her kind ways, her love so often protested, her tears and kisses, meant nothing. She had deserted him. She no longer wanted him. 

She had Bill, the baby to come, Cheet Barn, and it was enough for her. He had nothing. His hope of the future became the slenderest, most intangible thing-he lost hold of it. What chance had he against so many? There were thousands of fiddlers in the world better than he could ever be. He was living now on his mother. He wasn't any good to her. Hadn't he seen time after time how her face would change when he came into the room? She would be reading or sewing, or engaged in some household job, with her face placid and content, and then at the sight of him anxiety would come into her eyes.

He could not have told how he got there, but he found himself ringing the bell of the lift to take him up to the practice room. He had crossed two main roads thunderous

NEW BED 

with city traffic, but he did not know how it had come about, nor had he heard the curses of a drayman who had pulled back his horses' heads just in time. "It's no use ringing the bell. I've been trying for ten minutes," said a plump, fair-haired singer tartly as she began to ascend the stairs to her studio. She disliked stairs, and she disliked town in summer, and more than either of these things she disliked dissipated-looking young men whose eyes went through her as if she did not exist. She was accustomed to homage. She turned at the landing to throw out a further sharp remark, but Paul pushed past her and went straight on to the next flight of stairs. His action gave her an almost uncanny sensation that she had not spoken; indeed, that she was not standing on the landing at all but was invisible. She hurried into her room and slammed the door violently, which was the only way of asserting herself she could think of. 

Paul was unaware of it. The place might have fallen about his ears and all the singers in their studios screamed rage at him, but he would not have been troubled, unless he had seen a heavy beam or a slab of ceiling falling in his direction and then in his misery he might have put himself in the way of it and thought the deed a good one. There was nothing to live for, he told himself. He could see nothing before him but heartache and desolation, long dreary days and sleepless dreary nights. His mother would be better without him. Bill would look after her. He played with the idea of suicide. If there were some way so that his mother would think it an accident. He stopped on the stairs, brooding, and then went on again. He came to the top of the landing. The door of the lift-shaft was open, but the lift was not there. "Careless of Fergerson. He'll be getting into trouble," he thought. He went over and looked down into the black cavern. It came to him that here was a way

NEW BED 269 out. The gates were open; it would look like an accident. 

Nobody would ever know. He trembled and would have fallen, had he not held the iron bars of the doorway. They were wet when, steadied a little, he took his hands away. 

If he went to the other side of the landing and walked straight forward with his eyes shut it would be over before he knew anything about it. It was an easy way out; all ready for him as though the gods had known. He went slowly to the other side of the landing, shut his eyes and began to walk in the direction of the lift; but after a few steps he opened his eyes and knew he could not do it. He ran into the practice room and shut the door, to shut out the sight of what he might have done. He was weak and faint, and his heart beat like hammers in his head. He was surprised to find himself soaked in perspiration and shivery with cold, although outside the dusty roofs shimmered in the heat. He sat there motionless, clutching the sides of his chair as he might have clutched at life itself. 

It was quiet in the practice room. The noise of the street came as a monotonous hum from far away below with an occasional hooter or raucous motor-horn or horses slipping on stones setts breaking the general monotone. A pigeon sat on a chimney pot below him, preening its grey feathers.

It shone iridescent in the sun and its legs and feet were delicately pink against the grime. Paul looked at it. "You're a pretty thing to live in this grimy place," he said, and his heart seemed to turn right over when he thought that by now he might have given up his right to look upon beauty. "The world's full of loveliness," he thought, "unexpected loveliness. . . It happens sometimes in music. Ann was lovely like that. . . when I first saw her. Our love was, too. It is now. It is there in spite of all that's happened." 

The reaction came swiftly. He reproached himself for his lack of trust. Some outside circumstance caused the

NEW BED delay. She was ill, maybe, or could not get ready in time for the train. She would come. Of course she would come. 

On the next train, and arrive and find no one to meet her. 

He looked at his watch. It was two o'clock. Although he had half-an-hour in which to get to the station, a ten minutes' walk for an ordinary person, but for Paul, with a fire burning in his bosom, five or six minutes only, he put on his hat and rushed out possessed with the desire of getting there at once to make up for his lack of faith and trust. He was so sure she would be there that he could have sung. How wonderful it would be to see her again, to tell her everything, to hear her soft, "Oh, Paul," and see her smile at him and hold out her hand. He was mazed with the joy of anticipation, white hot with adoration of his beloved.

The lift gate was open. Old Fergie would be sitting within, waiting for a ring from below. “Hurry up, Fergie, I've got a train to meet," he shouted, and dashed into the lift-into nothingness.

CHAPTER XXVI 

BILL came striding up the station approach, possessed by a terrible and unreasoning anxiety. He was an hour earlier than usual, but the afternoon had been so crowded with activity that it seemed to him the evening must be nearly ended instead of just beginning, and if he did not hurry he would be too late. Only one idea was in his mind as he walked swiftly along, and that was to get home and make sure no whisper of the tragedy of Paul's death should reach

Ann. He told himself over and over again that there had been no time for the news to reach Cheet, but reason did not convince him nor abate his fears.

It had been his one idea as soon as the horror and numbness of the shock had lifted a little and let his thoughts flow again. "That such a thing should happen on our premises, and to such an old-fashioned, long-standing firm as we are," said the manager with tears in his eyes. "Why couldn't the boy look where he was going?" he wailed for the seventh time at least. "You will break the news to his mother?" 

Then his voice changed to a whisper, half suspicious, half apologetic. . . "I suppose there was no reason at all to think . . . er .. " ... 

Bill also had that same thought. "No," he said very firmly, for he had not only the manager to convince but himself. "He was apt to get absorbed in his music beyond all ordinary preoccupation. He had more than his share of temperament. You'll have met many musicians like that?" "Plenty," sighed the manager, as if all his life he had 271

NEW BED 

been coping with the eccentricities of the music profession and still felt he had no reliable understanding of them. "I'm afraid they'll bring in negligence. criminal neg- • · ligence. . . . That side door has always been a bugbear of mine. Being away from the main shop one doesn't see.

And the lighting of the fifth floor has always been unsatisfactory..

Bill interrupted him. He had the feeling that the manager would say these things many times over before the tragic affair was allowed to sink into oblivion. "I must go home first. I do not want my wife to hear this news in her present state . . . "> Bill did not lessen his pace, though he had ten minutes to wait for his train. He bought the afternoon paper, but it contained no mention of the tragedy. Even if it had it was unlikely that this edition of the paper would reach Cheet before him. There were bundles of papers waiting to go on the same train as himself. He knew it was too early, but it made no difference to his impatience and agitation. News travelled fast and by strange ways. If only he were at home and knew that Ann were safe. Before he had received the telephone message calling him to the music shop she had been in his mind, filling it with a strange discomfort. True, she had been ill and had disturbed him several times in the night, and she had looked wan and dejected that morning before he left, but he had seen her like that before. He was not given to strange imaginings, and his reason could generally subdue unquiet thoughts, but now it had lost its power. He had the most horrible and terrifying visions of 

Ann-her reason gone, lost to him for ever. He suffered agonies of fear as he walked up and down, so that when he saw Ann huddled and stricken on a seat at the far end of the platform he thought for a moment his anxiety had played a trick on him and had called up her wraith. He

NEW BED 

273 went nearer. It was Ann, but she was so changed he could hardly believe his eyes. This must be some monstrous hallucination that his mind, unnerved by the events of the afternoon, had conjured forth.

She might have been one of his own images, she sat so rigid and still, her face pinched and blue, shrunken it seemed to half its usual size, and her eyes large and numb -the eyes of a creature too deeply wounded to moan. He knew at once all his efforts to keep the news from her had been in vain. "Ann, darling, what are you doing here?" he tried to say; but it was hard speaking to one whose body alone was there and whose soul was wandering in unimaginable mists of pain.

He put his arm round her and lifted her to her feet and half led, half carried her to the train, thankful it was too early for the usual rush of business folk going home. He found an empty compartment, and when he had placed her carefully in the corner he stood at the window, partly to keep out intruders and partly because he was almost afraid to look at her. "How are you?" he asked several times during the journey; but Ann did not answer his question. Once when the train rocked violently she looked at him reproachfully, her face contorted with sudden pain and she whispered entreatingly: "Is it much longer? Are we nearly there?" as if she thought it in his power to make the train do whatever he willed.

When they reached Cheet the misery of her mind had been overcome by bouts of pain which swept everything away but their own violent and racking recurrence.

It was forty-eight hours before Bill saw her again. The nurse came for him. His mother, worn out, had been put

NEW BED 

to rest with a sleeping draught. Bill crept to the door and listened, as he had done many times that day, before he turned the handle with infinite precaution and peeped into the room. It was neat and in order, and it seemed to him its stillness was the quietness of peace and not the silence of foreboding and menace. The doctor beckoned him in. 

Bill went softly to the bedside and knelt down. Ann turned her eyes to him in welcome and with infinite care 

Bill slipped his arm beneath her head, taking no heed of the nurse, who frowned, then abruptly abandoned her protest and followed the doctor to the far end of the room. 

Ann lay with her head on his arm, the signs of a terrible exhaustion upon her, but faintly smiling, as if relief now the pain was finished had spread a balm that was blessed. 

Her face was ivory, of such exquisite fragility that Bill found its beauty almost intolerable to look upon. It was translucent, like alabaster, he thought; then shuddered. No -not alabaster—that was an unbearable image.

He was afraid now to move or draw his arm away, and when he would have called softly to the nurse for her aid he dare not, lest his voice cracked and broke the stillness of the room. It seemed to him that at any sound at all Ann's frail breath would flutter and go out, and if only he could stay in this tense silence, holding Ann with all the force and will-power of his nature it might yet end well. He kept saying a line of verse over and over again as a kind of incantation to the gods of stillness and hushed peace. 

"Now sleeps the crimson petal, now the white . .

He could not remember any more of it. Sleep, that was what Ann needed; soft sleep, like the sleep of roses folded under a calm summer sky. There was healing in such sleep, and life. Frantically he told himself these things, looking at 

Ann's eyes, which were closed now, and frantically he wrestled with his fear and the desolation creeping up into

NEW BED 275 his heart. Ann's head was heavy on his arm. She had often lain like that at night, but rounded and soft and warm as a bird in a nest, not straight and still and cold.

The doctor came from the window and looked down at 

Ann closely; then, very gently, he released Bill's arm.

CHAPTER XXVII 

THE kitchen at Cheet Barn was fragrant with the smell of newly baked bread. On her knees pulling the tins out of the oven was Bill's mother, a white apron over her best black dress and a little pucker of anxiety on her brow as she rapped each loaf and listened to the sound it made. 

The kitchen had reverted to its old appearance. It looked very much the same as when Ann had seen it for the first time on the evening of her wedding day. There was a lace curtain on a tape across the window, and in the windowseat, where she had loved to sit, scarlet geraniums and fuchsias in pots. The brass and pewter articles Ann had collected from about the house to shine in the kitchen firelight had been banished to a cupboard because Mrs. Gordon came no more and the polishing of them made too much work for Bill's mother; and in their place on the mantelpiece were coloured tins which held string and paper spills, and miscellaneous small articles, such as odd buttons and nails and old pen-nibs. There was a shabby, worn Bible on the dresser, and the clothes-rack hanging from the ceiling was filled with freshly-ironed linen. On the rocking-chair was the tablecloth of crimson-plush which had been taken off during the bread-making. A pan of food for the ducks simmered on the hob. There were two strips of faded carpet leading to the scullery door and to the garden door, to save the tread of dirty feet on the flagged floor. Catalogued, these seemed only a few insignificant changes, yet Ann was completely banished. It was as if she had never been.

Maude came into the kitchen and looked round defiantly, but with a certain anxiety. 276

NEW BED 

277 "Where's Bill?" she asked. "He's in the barn." "Has he said anything?' 

Bill's mother shook her head sadly. "The poor lad," she murmured. "I know he has suffered a great loss, and we should feel for him; but I do think he should make up his mind, seeing it concerns us all," said Maude, her hard brittle voice shattering the peace of the room and setting her mother aquiver.

The old lady wiped her eyes with the corner of her apron. "Why don't you ask him? Shall I?" persisted Maude. "I'll speak to him. When he comes in to supper. Mr. and Mrs. Dibbs won't be back for an hour or two yet. They are gone for a run in the car. Mrs. Dibbs was feeling poorly."

Maude sniffed. She did not approve of Mrs. Dibbs. "They want to go home," she said. "Bill should think of that."

Bill's mother sighed and returned to her bread. There was nothing more she could say. Maude fidgeted about the kitchen for a moment. "I'd better be going. I left Pansy and Alfred alone." "Yes, do," answered her mother. "Bill's very funny just now. He can't bear to see you in the place, no more than he can bear the child." 

Maude bridled a little. "Do you think he'll let them take it to London?" she asked.

Her mother twisted her fingers nervously. "It's against my wish if he does." "I'm sorry for Pansy and Alfred," said Maude unctuously. "With Alfred only earning small money, it means everything to them for Bill to let you and Betty stay here,

NEW BED and for them to have the house furnished and everything. 

For their sakes, Mother you might speak to Bill again. He should remember you are his mother." "The lad's all right, only he's thought for nothing but his sorrow," said Mrs. Darwin defensively; then seeing

Maude was about to speak again, "But do Pansy and Alfred want you to share their home? It's not every young couple likes a third person living with them when they're first married." 

Maude looked surprised at this remark, but when she had answered her mother was left feeling she had been wrong to put it forward. "Pansy thinks of what is best for her brother. She doesn't want his child given up to the pharisees and publicans, or for him to be left alone with a housekeeper when he's got a mother to look after him. Alfred is of the same opinion. I shall pay them for my board and lodging and that will help them." "Yes, of course," replied Mrs. Darwin weakly. "I am thankful Pansy has found such a gentle-natured young man." She sighed a little, which suggested she was not quite as thankful as she said she was. 

Maude bent and kissed her mother. "Good-bye. You speak to Bill, then, and I will pray that guidance be sent to him to help him do the thing that is right.”

Bill's mother sat down in the rocking-chair and rocked herself to and fro. It was easy to see her thoughts were painful. She looked up when Bill came into the room from the garden. "Well, Mother, baking?" he said kindly. 

His mother plunged into her subject at once. "Mr. and 

Mrs. Dibbs have gone riding. She was poorly. It's the worry, Bill. They want to go home. They don't know what you mean to do."

NEW BED 279 "I don't know," muttered Bill angrily; and his face was so stern his mother was afraid to speak again. There was silence between them, Bill staring at the fire, his mother thinking of the baby. Bill as a baby had been very like his son, only Bill had been a strong child and Ann's baby was small and weak. Bill's mother had told Mrs. Dibbs of this resemblance with satisfaction, and had brought photographs to prove it. Ann's mother had not been very pleased. 

When Charlie Dibbs had suggested that they should go to Cheet and offer to adopt the baby she had agreed, though she had not been anxious to have a young child in her care, in spite of the nursemaid Charlie Dibbs had promised her. A little girl would have pleased her more; a girl would have stayed at home, been a help later on, but a boy would be off on his own affairs. She did not want to bring up a child who would turn out another Bill. She was convinced, indeed, that it was Bill's fault the baby had been born too soon and Ann had died of shock; and Bill's treatment of them since their arrival had made her detest him still more. He had left it entirely to his mother to entertain them, and when Charlie Dibbs put forth his offer Bill would give no definite word, and kept them hanging on day after day. She was uncomfortable in this quiet oldfashioned home, and feeling this, pitied her daughter and reproached herself for letting her go so far away. 

Mrs. Darwin's blunt, homely speech, her self-effacement, her plain drab clothes and lack of any sort of display irritated Mrs. Dibbs; she regarded them as outward signs of a drab and depressing virtue with which, in contrast, her own pink complexion and fair hair, her pearls and furs and laces, took on an air of artificiality. There was indeed an increased air of artificiality about those buxom and luxuriant charms nowadays. Her mouth had changed, hardened and become a trifle grim, and her softly-dimpled cheeks and

NEW BED 

chin had lost their babyish roundness. They were still plump a little plumper perhaps than before her marriage -but the curves had subtly deteriorated and there were pouches under her eyes in the mornings. Marriage with

Charlie Dibbs could be hard in spite of luxury, and life in "Ye Old Thatched Inn" was not all conviviality and good fellowship. Ugly incidents happened there, as in most public-houses, and she had had to look upon these things and they had coarsened her. This coarsening of her nature had made her more susceptible in other ways; she was harder, yet less selfish. Ann, just as she had made herself more accessible by becoming a woman, had been lost, and Mrs. Dibbs mourned and felt strangely lonely. 

She was angry with Bill and his mother, who could never have understood and loved in the right way the prettiness and charm of her daughter. How could they, with their grim puritanism, their joylessness, their dreary insistence upon virtue and duty? For the first time in her life grief and discomfort had the power to make her ill. It was in a frantic desire to escape for an hour or two from the atmosphere of Cheet Barn that she had insisted that Charlie 

Dibbs should take her for a ride.

Bill heard the noise of the car on their return and left the kitchen hurriedly. He went into the barn and took a cloth from a mask of Ann he had been working on. He had taken a plaster cast of her face as she lay dead, and he was carving it now in wood, idealising the features, emphasising their remote impersonal beauty; for in death the lines made by the burden, the weariness, the exaltation of her love for Paul had all departed, leaving her face childlike, as it had been when Bill had first seen her. He took up a tool, and as he did so a loud apologetic cough came from the door, and Charlie Dibbs appeared, giving the impression he was standing on tiptoe.

NEW BED 

281 "I don't want to intrude," he began, coming forward at a good pace and looking round with interest, as though he meant to see as much as he could before Bill turned him out. "Nice old place," he said ingratiatingly. "I'm very partial to a bit of old timber myself." "Oh, yes," said Bill uninvitingly.

Charlie Dibbs walked round the room. He stopped and scrutinised carefully the mantelpiece and panels Bill had taken from the bedroom, the satyrs in bacchanalian procession. "Bit of hot stuff there. Now that takes my eye. My word, look at those grapes. It's rich, that is." "It went over an old fireplace," said Bill. "But it needs a big room, not a living-room or a bedroom, but something more formal." 

Charlie Dibbs whistled. "It would look a treat in my place. When you come to think of it the subject's appropriate. Are you selling it?" "I'll give it to you. You can take it back in the car." 

Charlie Dibbs came over to the bench quickly. "'Ere, hold on. That's a bit of hand-made stuff," he said concernedly. "You can have it," said Bill, without looking up. He was staring hard at the table, and Charlie Dibbs, suddenly embarrassed at this ungracious generosity, did the same. 

He saw the death mask then, next to the wooden plaque 

Bill was working on. "She didn't alter much. I've seen her like that. Funny, I never imagined her growing older, and she didn't," he muttered. He wanted to ask if Ann had been happy at 

Cheet-happier than if she had stayed with him and her mother; but he didn't know what to say or how to say it.

He had a vague empty feeling that she had not been happy,

NEW BED and that now he would never know or be able to do anything at all about it, only carry a memory of her as a cold white mask that nothing could ever warm into life again. 

Something in Charlie Dibbs' manner and the unhappiness in his round protruding eyes touched Bill. Strange that this gross man with his cheap geniality and good nature, his grasping business instinct, his raucous showman voice, should bring Ann back to him so vividly, not Ann the nymph, but Ann the young girl, eager and alive, adoring all young things. For the first time he thought of the baby without thinking of it as the cause of Ann's death, and his heart softened. "I can understand you hesitating about the kid," began Charlie, who had suddenly remembered his errand and his wife's importunities. "I couldn't let it go," said Bill hurriedly and nervously. "I shouldn't let it go if I was in your place," agreed 

Charlie Dibbs, disappointed but impartial.

Bill hurried upstairs. A desire had come over him to see the child. It lay sleeping in the cot Bill's mother had trimmed with muslin and blue ribbon. Its tiny fists were clenched above the blankets. Betty sat on a chair nearby with a book, very proud of her charge. She looked alarmed when Bill entered the room, for it was the first time he had done so. She got up and offered him her chair, and Bill sat down on it. He sat for a while in silence, then Betty stole quietly away. When she had gone Bill took a scrap of paper from his pocket and began to draw. He wanted another figure for the panel he had started when Ann first knew the baby was coming and the tiny dimpled fists of his son appealed to him. He remembered what Ann had said about all young helpless silly things, and how she had loved them, and he thought this child before him was the most helpless he had ever seen.
